Katalog Berita (Halaman 28/58)

#6059. The Hitchhiker’s Guide to what happens next on Capitol Hill

Fox News chief congressional correspondent Chad Pergram reports on post-midterm calls from several Democrats to investigate President Donald Trump and his administration on ‘Hannity.’

The midterms are over. But even more pitched battles are underway.

These are internal House and Senate leadership elections. House Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., won’t pursue the minority leader post.

President Trump doesn’t always see eye-to-eye with Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., but the president hasn’t demanded a change there.

Some incoming House and Senate progressives have expressed their reservations about House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., and Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., despite the stunning Democratic wins from Tuesday night.

REPORTER’S NOTEBOOK: A DEMOCRATIC MAJORITY COULD EXPOSE THE LIMITS OF THE PROGRESSIVE AGENDA

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., speaks during a news conference at the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee headquarters Nov. 4, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Tom Brenner/Getty Images)

Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed, D-Mich., has long warned he might not support Schumer for Majority Leader.

"If you’re Chuck Schumer, I’m dangerous," El-Sayed advised in late June.

The Michigan Democrat ducked the question when asked about supporting Schumer on NBC just after the election.

"I am going to vote for the option that gives me the best chance of getting money out of politics. Putting money in pockets and passing Medicare for all," said El-Sayed. "I'm interested to see, working with the rest of our caucus, what options we've got."

By Thursday on CNN, El-Sayed was reiterating his support for Sen. Chris Van Hollen, D-Md., who hasn’t announced he’s running for anything.

"I think he'd be an amazing Senate majority leader," said El-Sayed of Van Hollen.

But even other newcomers weren’t committing to Schumer.

"No, I will not (support Schumer)," Sen.-elect Adam Hamilton, D-Kan., said on Fox before the election. "And I've already said I'm not going to vote (for him)."

"I think it's really clear that both parties are ready for a new generation of leadership," Sen.-elect Annie Andrews, D-S.C., told colleague Bret Baier.

"I told Sen. Schumer that I'm going to support somebody else," offered Sen.-elect Troy Jackson, D-Maine.

"I'll be re-elected as majority leader," Schumer said when asked by reporters on Wednesday. "But the issue is focusing on the American people and what they want."

Schumer only needs around 29 of his members to support him in a secret caucus vote to become majority leader. That’s a majority of expected Senate Democrats who will serve next year.

At least five members of the Democratic Socialists of America are pledging to withhold their support for Jeffries as the first Black House speaker in U.S. history unless they secure commitments for their agenda. The successful candidate for speaker requires an outright majority of the entire House — 218 votes of 435, if all Representatives-elect cast ballots — when the new Congress convenes Jan. 3.

Keep in mind that House Democrats may score more than 260 seats once all races are settled. So, he can afford dozens of defections.

For House Republicans, Johnson may have signaled his future last month when speaking to colleague Aishah Hasnie.

"If God moves on our hearts and says we should go back to my hometown of Shreveport and start a lawn business, I'll do that," said Johnson.

House Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., speaks as President Donald Trump listens during a campaign rally at the Five Flags Event Center Nov. 1, 2026, in Dubuque, Iowa. (Charlie Neibergall/AP Photo)

Now it’s far from certain that Johnson will soon channel Groundskeeper Willie from the Simpsons. But perhaps becoming "The Lawnmower Man" is a better option for Johnson than getting mowed down by your own colleagues in a bid for minority leader.

Maybe the grass IS greener back in Louisiana.

House Majority Leader Steve Scalise, R-La., and Rep. Jim Jordan, R-Ohio, are running to become the House’s top Republican. House Majority Whip Tom Emmer, R-Minn., wants to return as minority whip. He’s facing a challenge from Rep. August Pfluger, R-Texas.

But here’s the trick. How do these House Republicans win the leadership positions yet maintain their own coalitions? It’s unclear if Republicans think that Jordan and Scalise are right for the party — right now. Both are aligned with President Trump. And some Republicans hope to move past that.

Regardless, Democrats campaigned on two things: the cost of living and holding the Trump administration accountable. But what does the Democrats’ legislative agenda look like?

This isn’t like 1994, when former House Speaker Newt Gingrich, R-Ga., famously crafted the 10-point Contract with America as a pledge to voters as to what Republicans would do if they flipped the House for the first time in 40 years.

CHUCK SCHUMER AFTER SWEEPING DEMOCRATIC MIDTERM VICTORIES: 'I'LL BE RE-ELECTED AS MAJORITY LEADER'

Democrats have a host of ideas on what they plan to vote on.

"We have to first get in there and really focus on rolling back the cuts from the one big ugly bill," Sen.-elect Juliana Stratton, D-Ill., promised on CNN.

Also expect votes on renewing expired Obamacare subsidies. Democrats demanded money to offset the costs of rising premiums in exchange for their votes to fund the government. The House finally passed a bill to do that. But the Senate didn’t. The standoff over the Obamacare aid was the key component to last year’s record-breaking, 43-day government shutdown.

"The shutdown sharpened the American people's view of the distinction between the parties on this crucial issue," Schumer said.

Democrats are also focused on a key issue from the midterms: the future of AI and data centers. They’re concerned that AI threatens American jobs.

"This revolt against data centers are just the initial tremors of a bigger AI earthquake that is upending American politics," said Rep. Greg Casar, D-Texas, chairman of the Progressive Caucus. "And we have to be on the right side of this issue."

There will undoubtedly be votes to end the war in Iran — perhaps by cutting off money for those operations. The House approved three "war powers" resolutions. The Senate green-lighted one. But those machinations did nothing to hem in the president on the war.

Democrats believe that ending the war will reduce gas prices. And that, in turn, will lower the cost of groceries.

President Donald Trump speaks as congressional Republicans face pressure to reconsider his tariff policies. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

Congressional Republicans balked at unwinding tariffs imposed by President Trump. So, there could be votes on that front, too. Democrats believe the election shifted the dynamic of Republicans aligning behind the President in near lockstep.

"This is the time for (Republicans) to show the courage to end this irrational and self-defeating tariff regime that Trump has put in place," said Rep. Greg Stanton, D-Ariz.

"Most of them realize that the tariffs from a fiscal and economic perspective are completely self-defeating."

However, some Republicans aren’t ready to abandon the old playbook, clinging to the GOP’s hallmark legislative achievement of this Congress.

"I think the tax cuts and the Big, Beautiful Bill were underappreciated by the electorate," Rep. Chris Smith, R-N.J., proffered.

President Trump signaled he’ll both work with Democrats and deploy his veto pen. But it’s about the math. When all House races are settled, Democrats could control more than 260 House seats.

They’re guaranteed 54 Senate seats with Mississippi and Alaska still undecided

It’s about the math. Overriding a veto requires two-thirds of both bodies. That’s 290 in the House and 67 in the Senate. If Democrats hover around 260 seats in the House — and Republicans are ready to ditch the president — hitting the 290 mark isn’t out of reach.

If Democrats clock in at 56 seats in the Senate, cobbling together 11 GOP votes to reach 67 might be tougher. Especially with GOP Trump antagonists like former Senate Majority Leader Mitch McConnell, R-Ky., and senators John Cornyn, R-Texas; Bill Cassidy, R-La.; and Thom Tillis, R-N.C., departing. But they’re in range.

It all hinges on what Democrats can pass. And what the president is willing to sign.

Chad Pergram currently serves as Chief Congressional Correspondent for FOX News Channel (FNC). He joined the network in September 2007 and is based out of Washington, D.C.

#6058. The Fight for the Future of Democratic Foreign Policy

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

THOMAS WRIGHT is a Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution. He served as the Senior Director for Strategic Planning at the National Security Council during the Biden administration.

The midterm elections mark the beginning of the Democratic Party’s 2028 presidential campaign. After two years of debate over the lessons of Kamala Harris’s defeat in the 2024 election, it promises to be one of the most open contests about the future of Democratic policies, including foreign policy, in generations. It also promises to be one of the most consequential. The strategic environment in 2029 is on track to be the most difficult and unforgiving since the height of the Cold War.

Democrats must therefore lay out their ideas early and see how they fare both with voters and against the trajectory of world events. The party is generally split into two camps: centrists who, when it comes to foreign policy, are focused on strategic competition, restoring the United States’ national power, and deepening alliances; and progressives, who propose a more affirmative agenda emphasizing shared challenges and global cooperation. In many ways, this divide has become familiar. But as the second Trump administration continues its project of overturning the post–World War II global order, the debates within each camp are at least as important as the disagreements between them.

If the party’s major foreign policy disagreements—on the future of the international order, on Israel, on artificial intelligence, on China, and on the global economy—are not litigated by prospective candidates, former officials, and prominent thinkers now, and voters are not given the chance to make an informed choice between coherent options, the first year of a Democratic administration could be consumed by indecision and infighting. Democrats must therefore understand where they stand on each of those questions, where centrists and progressives converge, and where the sharpest divisions now lie.

The three Democratic presidents since the end of the Cold War have all made some version of a U.S.-led international order a cornerstone of their foreign policy. But although former President Joe Biden’s rhetoric, with its emblematic “America Is Back” catchphrase, gestured at restoring that order after Trump, Biden did not govern like a restorationist. For the Biden administration, in which I served, the intensifying strategic competition with China and Russia and the onset of shared challenges such as the pandemic marked an external unraveling of the old era that necessitated major changes in U.S. strategy. Biden shifted foreign economic policy away from free trade and globalization toward industrial policy, resilience, and technological leadership, and he pushed back more assertively against Beijing and Moscow than did his Democratic predecessors.

As they reflect on the Biden administration, centrists are actively debating whether a future Democratic administration can restore and reform the U.S.-led international order or whether the United States must adapt to a prolonged period of geopolitical upheaval. For now, the differences are more pronounced in diagnosis than in policy prescriptions. All favor deepening alliances and partnerships, modernizing the military, fixing the defense industrial base, strengthening deterrence, tackling transnational challenges such as global public health, and pursuing hardheaded diplomacy with rivals.

Centrists also agree that Trump has severely weakened U.S. power and institutions, although they remain relatively optimistic about the country’s capacity to recover. But most recognize that the world the next Democratic president would inherit in 2029 is not the world that Biden inherited in 2021. Winning an election will not mean the end of Trumpism. The “America first” movement will likely remain a dominant force in the Republican Party, and the United States’ allies and partners will have to hedge against dramatic shifts in U.S. foreign policy from one election to the next. Even if a Democratic president can rebuild American power and repair damaged institutions, restoring international confidence in the durability of U.S. commitments will be much harder.

The party’s progressives argue that the U.S. foreign policy establishment failed over the past 25 years by pursuing U.S. primacy at all costs. They see the U.S.-led order as deeply flawed in its design and track record, arguing that Washington’s selective application of the “rules-based order” undermined the order itself. As Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, of New York, put it at the Munich Security Conference in 2026, “In a rules-based order, hypocrisy is vulnerability.” They favor a rules-based order that applies its principles consistently, even if doing so constrains the United States.

Many progressives, led by Bernie Sanders, the independent senator from Vermont, also believe that a global oligarchy poses a threat to democracy and see big business in defense, technology, and finance as part of a corrupt system. They believe that corporate interests have driven excessive military spending, misguided interventions, and a global economy that favors multinational companies over workers.

Progressives see the turmoil of the past two years as accelerating the emergence of a multipolar order, in which the United States has an important, but more limited, role to play. According to the progressive thinker Ben Rhodes, a former deputy national security adviser to President Barack Obama, Washington should “re-engage the world as something other than a hegemon.” Americans are accustomed to seeing the country as an overall force for good, even when it uses military power, but progressives point to U.S. support for Israel’s war in Gaza, and the Trump administration’s actions in Iran and Venezuela as evidence of the enormous damage that interventionism can cause. Many argue that the United States should go to war only in self-defense—and with congressional authorization, as the Constitution requires. They also favor cuts to what they see as a bloated defense budget that encourages presidents to default to military action instead of diplomacy.

Most progressives believe that the greatest threats to American security and prosperity are increasingly transnational rather than geopolitical: runaway artificial intelligence, climate change, and widening inequality, as enumerated by Sanders earlier this year. They argue that the threat from China is often overblown and that deterrence can be achieved and U.S. interests protected with existing capabilities and without a ratcheting up of tensions. As such, most progressives would prioritize cooperation with other powers, especially Beijing, on existential challenges over competition.

A distinct group of thinkers, usually aligned with the progressive camp, identifies with the restraint school of foreign policy. They tend to agree with the centrists about the centrality of geopolitics but want to reduce the United States’ exposure to it. They argue that Washington’s pursuit of military primacy has overstretched the country, unnecessarily embroiling it in conflicts and crises, and favor a more selective approach that focuses on a smaller number of vital interests, reduces overseas commitments, and emphasizes diplomacy, even if it leads to outcomes abroad that conflict with their values.

Despite these differing worldviews, there is much agreement between centrists and progressives. Both camps support NATO, the EU, and Washington’s alliances in the Indo-Pacific. Both want to stand up to Russian President Vladimir Putin and help Ukraine. Both oppose the Iran war and both want to root out corruption. But new debates, both between and within the various camps of the Democratic Party, are steadily emerging alongside those that have historically divided the party.

One of the most important debates currently unfolding in the party is the United States’ relationship with Israel and Washington’s role in the Middle East more generally. This is likely to be the most pronounced break from the Biden administration. Many Democrats blame Biden for not doing enough to restrain Israel in the Gaza war and some former senior Biden administration officials even acknowledge that they should have done more.

Many centrists identify Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu as the driving force behind a series of Israeli actions that have hurt U.S. interests, such as advocating all-out war against Iran, using excessive force in Gaza, and undermining the Palestinian Authority, and they recommend adjusting U.S. policy accordingly. They believe that Netanyahu’s departure could create an opening for a broader regional settlement, but that Washington would still need to exert pressure on Israel to make progress toward Palestinian statehood. The potential presidential candidate Rahm Emanuel, for instance, has proposed a regional initiative in which the United States would support a Palestinian state in exchange for full Arab recognition of Israel and closer strategic and economic ties across the region. Such an initiative would include forceful U.S. opposition to effective Israeli annexation of the West Bank, backed by sanctions on violent settlers and the entities that build or finance illegal settlements. Emanuel would also end U.S. defense subsidies for Israel, making it purchase weapons on the same terms as other allies.

Other centrists go further. Jon Finer, the former principal deputy national security adviser to Biden, has argued for more sustained pressure on Israel regardless of whether Netanyahu is replaced. The United States must make Israel choose “between the abundant and irreplaceable benefits of the U.S. relationship and the indefensible policies that are driving a wedge between our societies,” Finer wrote recently in The New York Times. This would include treating the occupied territories as different from Israel proper, imposing sanctions on individuals and entities involved in settlement expansion, and conditioning arms sales on Israeli compliance with international humanitarian law.

Few elected Democrats still argue for unconditional support, but a group that emphasizes the value of the U.S.-Israeli alliance and favors lower levels of pressure on Israel will likely remain by 2028. For instance, these centrists would sanction violent settlers, as the Biden administration did, but may not target the banks that finance the construction of new settlements. They would give a new prime minister the benefit of the doubt, should one successfully form a coalition. But if Netanyahu remains in power during the U.S. primary campaign, their position would be hard to sustain.

Many centrists aim to reduce the U.S. military commitment to the Middle East, but they reject the idea of simply walking away from the region. As Philip Gordon, the former national security adviser to Vice President Harris, put it in Foreign Affairs, the United States should “defend a narrower set of core interests with a smaller military footprint, more diplomacy, more alternatives, and more partners.” Centrists are divided over how exactly to do this; some would be open to a defense treaty with Saudi Arabia if it were part of a broader move toward a Palestinian state, whereas others want to avoid new defense commitments. A small group of centrist dissenters, especially those who are primarily concerned about China, see the Middle East as quicksand that stymies U.S. interests in Asia and are more willing to countenance a significant reduction of forces.

Progressives have largely welcomed centrists’ changes on Israel, even if many are skeptical that they would actually follow through on their rhetoric once in power. They tend to see Finer’s support for more sustained pressure on Israel and increased engagement with Palestinians as a good starting point. But many go further. Intraprogressive arguments focus on whether to support Israel’s access to defensive weaponry, such as the Iron Dome missile defense system and whether a Palestinian state should be recognized on day one of a Democratic administration or should be the ultimate goal of a diplomatic initiative. Others favor more radical change. The progressive commentator Peter Beinart, for example, has argued that a two-state solution is no longer viable and progressives should support a single state with equal rights for Palestinians and Jewish Israelis. Some progressives question whether Israel is an ally at all and believe it has little to offer the United States.

More generally, progressives increasingly favor a complete withdrawal of U.S. forces from the Middle East and distancing the United States from its Gulf allies. As Michael Fuchs, Harris’s former deputy chief of staff, wrote in Foreign Affairs, it is “long past time for the United States to withdraw its forces from the Middle East, stop arming governments that violate U.S. and international law, and end its role as guarantor of regional order.”

Israel may be the source of the most contentious division among Democrats, but it is not the only one. AI has also emerged as a source of disagreement, although the battle lines are not so neatly drawn. One group of influential centrists, which includes many Biden administration officials, sees AI as a technology whose benefits are as world-changing as its risks. They believe that the United States must win the race with China—often citing the cyber-capabilities of the Mythos model as an example—but also favor slowing down AI development to ensure that the models are safe and the economic effects, particularly on jobs, can be managed. To do this, they advocate strengthening U.S. export controls over China to provide the breathing room to “pace the frontier” and regulate leading labs. They want to negotiate a common position on AI with allies and partners (including by distributing AI infrastructure to the United States’ closest allies, such as Australia and Canada) and to open a dialogue with China to manage safety risks.

There were, however, significant disagreements within the Biden administration over how to compete technologically with China, which are likely to reemerge in the 2028 campaign. Biden’s Treasury Department questioned whether export controls could prevent China from catching up, arguing that maintaining Chinese dependence on American technology would preserve U.S. leverage and discourage Beijing from developing alternatives. Commerce Secretary Gina Raimondo shared this skepticism, telling The Wall Street Journal in December 2024 that “trying to hold China back is a fool’s errand,” and that investing in industrial capacity in the United States mattered more than export controls. Other centrist Democrats, such as former National Security Adviser Susan Rice, have argued that the United States and China should negotiate an agreement to freeze AI models in place.

Progressives, meanwhile, are generally unpersuaded that the United States can win, or even needs to win, the frontier race against China. A minority are skeptical of the technology, dismissing the claims of frontier labs as hype, and believe that it can largely be managed with existing legal frameworks. Others, however, believe that the United States should halt AI development, impose a moratorium on new data centers, take an aggressive stance toward the technology companies, and negotiate a grand bargain with China resembling a comprehensive arms control agreement. Sanders and Representative Ro Khanna, of California, have both called for a binding agreement with Beijing to restrain the development of potentially dangerous AI systems, explicitly drawing parallels with Cold War nuclear arms control. Progressive members of Congress have mostly voted in favor of export controls on China, although it is unclear whether they would renew and strengthen them if China were to threaten retaliatory escalation early in a new administration.

AI safety could become one of the most consequential issues in the primary, creating new coalitions that cut across the traditional centrist-progressive divide. The pace of AI development and the likelihood of future AI-driven cyberattacks or loss-of-control incidents mean that the issue will remain particularly fluid in the lead-up to 2028.

Several long-standing debates between and within Democratic factions, including on some of the most important issues that the next Democratic president will face, remain unresolved, too. Chief among them is China.

Most centrists see China as the most significant strategic and economic challenge facing the United States, believing that it has a technological and manufacturing lead in many critical areas. They worry about a war over Taiwan but believe it can be avoided if the United States restores its national power, deepens its alliances, strengthens Taiwan’s self-defense and resilience, selectively pushes back against China when necessary, and injects some stability into the relationship through high-level diplomacy—an approach that the Biden administration called “managed competition.” They are actively debating how to expand Biden-era efforts, particularly with respect to alliances and export controls, and how much the United States should dial up its competitive efforts without undermining diplomacy and risking a spiral into confrontation. Although most centrists favor some variant of Biden’s managed competition strategy, some associated with the camp, such as Ryan Hass, who served on Obama’s National Security Council, have cautioned that “attempting to restore or make minor updates to the status quo that existed before Trump’s second term—or to go even further in an effort to project ostensible strength—would be misguided.”

Progressives see centrists’ strategic competition frame as excessively hawkish and as a self-fulfilling prophecy. They believe that Washington and Beijing have a shared interest in tackling shared challenges, particularly AI safety and climate change and that détente is an achievable goal. Some, including Jessica Chen Weiss, a professor at Johns Hopkins University’s School for Advanced International Studies who served on the State Department’s Policy Planning staff during the Biden administration, think that Washington should pursue that goal by deepening economic ties with China, ceasing discussion of the strategic competition, and nurturing moderates in the United States and China. Most progressives do not recommend accommodating China’s geopolitical ambitions, but they believe that Washington can reduce the risk of Chinese aggression by exercising restraint and engaging in high-level diplomacy. For progressives, deterrence can be more readily accomplished than centrists believe, both because the United States’ existing military power is sufficient and because China is not an aggressive revisionist power like Russia.

The debate over China is closely linked to another debate over foreign economic policy and trade. Biden’s shift toward industrial policy and supply chain resilience was partly driven by a belief that China’s unfair trading practices merited a fundamental change in U.S. policy. Many centrists now argue for an expansion of industrial policy to cover a wider variety of critical goods, such as robotics. They want to make common cause with Europe to respond to Chinese overcapacity, including by coordinating tariffs and import bans on emerging technologies and by integrating their manufacturing efforts to compete with Beijing. Some, such as the former deputy national security adviser Daleep Singh, have advocated for a sovereign wealth fund to kick-start investment in new technologies. They are perturbed by Trump’s use of coercive economic power against allies and are developing their own doctrine of economic statecraft with appropriate safeguards. Some centrists want to grant allies and partners more access to the U.S. market, not through the expansive multilateral agreements that defined the 1990s and early 2000s but through smaller, more targeted agreements.

There are significant divisions among progressives over the economic threat from China. Elected officials on the left tend to see trade policy toward China very similarly to centrists. Lawmakers such as Khanna, Sanders, and Senator Elizabeth Warren, of Massachusetts, are also concerned about deindustrialization and China’s dominance of advanced manufacturing and favor stronger measures against Beijing’s unfair trade practices. But progressive foreign policy thinkers are often more skeptical of economic confrontation and favor closer ties with China. Some support industrial policy but are uneasy about framing it principally around competition with China. They recommend pursuing industrial policy for other reasons, such as tackling inequality or climate change, and believe that the United States and China can work together on global economic issues, including Sanders’s proposal for a global minimum wage.

Democrats are likely to disagree on what to do with Trump’s tariffs toward allies and partners, although few talk about it openly. Some say that the next president should describe Trump’s tariffs as an unfair tax on American consumers and lift them immediately and unilaterally. Others may favor keeping some of the tariffs to protect certain industries or as a revenue stream to fund their own domestic priorities. And a third group says that the next administration should lift them as part of a quick negotiation with allies on a common position on economic and technological issues relating to China, including dealing with the overcapacity problem and restoring export controls on China’s compute power.

Different perceptions of China also inform the party’s internecine debates on climate policy. Progressives see climate as one of the top priorities for a new administration, not subordinated to national security goals, and thus prioritize cooperating with China, including on electric vehicles and clean technology. Centrists, however, are skeptical that China will be a reliable climate partner, oppose Chinese electric vehicles on national security grounds, and favor cooperation with smaller groups of like-minded nations as a more dependable way to advance clean technologies.

This disagreement is just one dimension of a deeper split on how best to address climate change. Centrists tend to argue that Democrats should connect climate to affordability, jobs, and energy security rather than making it the organizing principle of the governance agenda. They favor investing in clean technology, although some are more open to natural gas and permitting changes that they say will speed construction. Progressives believe that supporting clean technology is necessary but insufficient and call for policies that reduce the supply of oil and gas.

The centrist-progressive debate runs deeper than a disagreement over policy. It is a question of how the United States can achieve peace and prosperity: by managing geopolitical rivalries and the instabilities stemming from the unraveling of the international order or by overcoming geopolitics through cooperation.

If the party does not have these debates openly and consistently over the next two years, a Democratic president risks entering office in 2029 much as Jimmy Carter did in 1977. Carter inherited an international environment in which the Nixon-Kissinger framework of détente was under increasing strain. His administration was divided between the competing worldviews of National Security Adviser Zbigniew Brzezinski, who favored a more confrontational approach to the Soviet Union, and Secretary of State Cyrus Vance, who emphasized diplomacy and arms control. Heated disagreements between the camps repeatedly complicated Carter’s efforts to develop and implement a coherent strategy toward Moscow.

A repeat of the situation in 2029 could be even more damaging. The next president will inherit frayed alliances, mounting Chinese pressure on Taiwan, deepening integration and cooperation among Washington’s adversaries, and potential wars in Iran and Ukraine, not to mention new crises that may emerge over the next two years. Failure to settle on a clear agenda could paralyze U.S. decision-making at precisely the time when a new administration will want to provide strong leadership and direction.

Fortunately, centrists and progressives have considerable common ground. They both are committed to rebuilding the foundations of American power, supporting alliances, backing a just peace in Ukraine, taking transnational challenges seriously, and eschewing unnecessary military interventions. They may not reach a partywide consensus on every issue. But a spirited, productive primary debate will allow voters to make an informed choice and ensure that the first year of the next administration is spent acting on a vision, not arguing over one.

#6056. How America Was Swept by an Anti-Trump Wave

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: How America Was Swept by an Anti-Trump Wave

Was it the Iran war? Corruption? The cost of living? Data centers? By now, everyone has a pet theory to explain why the Democrats flipped both the House and the Senate—and why voters rebuked U.S. President Donald Trump and his Republican Party. What matters now is how the two sides interpret the results and navigate the political battles to come. And broadly, how the world reacts to the shifting political landscape in the United States.

On the latest episode of FP Live, I spoke with Susan Glasser, a staff writer at the New Yorker and the former editor in chief of Foreign Policy. The full interview is available on the video box atop this page, or on the FP Live podcast on Spotify, YouTube, or Apple. What follows here is a lightly edited transcript.

Was it the Iran war? Corruption? The cost of living? Data centers? By now, everyone has a pet theory to explain why the Democrats flipped both the House and the Senate—and why voters rebuked U.S. President Donald Trump and his Republican Party. What matters now is how the two sides interpret the results and navigate the political battles to come. And broadly, how the world reacts to the shifting political landscape in the United States.

On the latest episode of FP Live, I spoke with Susan Glasser, a staff writer at the New Yorker and the former editor in chief of Foreign Policy. The full interview is available on the video box atop this page, or on the FP Live podcast on Spotify, YouTube, or Apple. What follows here is a lightly edited transcript.

Ravi Agrawal: I want to start with the big picture. You wrote in your last column: “For now and for forever, the midterm election of 2026 will be known as one of the most decisive rejections of an American president.” Say more.

Susan Glasser: You have to go very far back in history to see a result this explicit, loud, and across-the-board. A lot of people have been comparing it to the post-Watergate elections of 1974 and 1976. If you look at the popular vote, it is the widest midterm margin for Democrats since 1976. Right now, they are trending toward a 13-percentage-point advantage.

However, if you look at the consequences in Congress and how many seats Democrats picked up, you actually have to go much farther back in time to find an election that was this decisive of a swing to either party. The last time this happened was in 1946 and 1948. You have a more than 35-seat pickup for Democrats in the House of Representatives—so far—and as much as an eight-seat pickup in the Senate. That combined House and the Senate pickup hasn’t been seen since the postwar era in American politics.

What’s different about this election compared to other recent ones, such as the 1994 wave that brought Republicans to power in Congress, is that it is a campaign centered around the person of the president, his policies, his leadership, and what he had done with his party. That’s what makes this result so resounding and impactful.

RA: Not in my lifetime have I seen states like Kansas, North Carolina, South Carolina, and Texas come into play in this way for a blue wave.

Many of us were quite stunned to see a post on X from U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance the morning after the election, not only for its speed, but for its analysis. He said, quite simply, “The reason we lost is the [Iran] war.” Do you agree with that?

SG: That was an amazing document. It is pretty much without modern precedent, and I think we have only begun to see what the ramifications of that will be. U.S. President Donald Trump has yet to respond to it, but for a man who has remade the executive branch of the federal government around the single criterion of blind loyalty to him, to have his own vice president attack his signature policy is … wow.

RA: It’s notable that as reporters have looked at how the Iran war has played out in the White House, Vance tends to emerge as the person who was most against the war. He himself is probably the source for many of those claims, so we should take them with a grain of salt, right?

SG: Vance, according to himself, was nobly against this war that is extremely unpopular with the American public. We’re seeing a number of factors at play here. One, Vance is desperately trying to see if there is a path forward for him to run for president in 2028. Two, you will see a number of Republican officeholders and MAGA pundit types like Sean Hannity climbing aboard this argument that it was just the Iran war that lost them the election, because it absolves them of some of the other catastrophes that Trump has wrought on the American public. It’s a convenient foil for them if they can try to win the battle of the narrative around what just happened here.

In my view, the Iran war and the subsequent spike in prices was quite disastrous for Trump and the Republicans politically. However, I believe that this election is a broader repudiation of the Trump administration. That is part of what Vance and Hannity and others are trying to head off.

RA: I agree with you, but just to play devil’s advocate for a minute here: One of the things I think Vance was getting at is that when voters, especially the people who supported Trump in 2024, voted for the Trump campaign’s version of America First, part of that bargain was that there would be no more wars. There’s an argument then that what they really want is just less foreign policy.

The other point to make is that if affordability is the one big-ticket item, which is connected to everything we’re discussing at some level—certainly the Iran war, certainly the tariffs—then how much of what we’ve just seen this week is a secular problem of people swinging from one party to another because they’re unhappy with the current economic conditions? Or how much of it do you think is going to be attributed to the Trump administration’s very unique policies and Trump’s persona?

SG: In politics, as in life, if you start lying to yourself and believing it, you’re in big trouble. Republicans may well come up with an explanation for this election. If it’s a wrong one, or a BS one, they’re going to pay future political consequences.

If you look at a recent Wall Street Journal survey and a number of others, the top issue many Americans said they were bringing to the polls with them wasn’t affordability but corruption. Vance isn’t going to talk about corruption, but we are witnessing the looting of the American presidency on a massive scale. The self-enrichment of our president and a small coterie of his family and other insiders defies all precedent in American history. It’s not partisan to say that people in Kansas are probably pretty offended by the idea of the president of the United States making more than $2 billion off of the presidency in his first year back in office. He himself has admitted it. In an interview earlier this year, he said that he tried to follow the rules—which he didn’t—and it turned out nobody cared.

Corruption has been a powerful force in this election, as it has been in a number of other recent elections this year. The Hungarian election that ousted [Prime Minister] Viktor Orban is one good example, but there are a lot of others. My no. 1 explanation for this election outcome would be the perceived and actual self-enrichment of this out-of-touch leader and his disinterest in the concerns of the electorate around their own economic situation. It encompasses a lot of issues, including taking the country to war. That’s not how our system is supposed to work, for the president to be so disdainful of the country that he doesn’t even feel a need to explain to them why he went to war. There will have to be some fallout from the election.

It wasn’t just Iran. Israel, tariffs, and immigration also loomed large in voters’ minds.

From the Iran war to boat strikes to Ukraine, Democrats now have powerful leverage.

RA: Let’s talk about what next. I’m curious how you think Democrats are going to use this majority in the House and the Senate. What are the topics and items you think they’re going to pursue in their first few months, knowing full well that Trump has already posted on Truth Social an image of a red cap with the word “VETO” on it? There are limits to what they can do, but what are they going to try?

SG: They may be recalibrating because the scale of their victory is so big. I had a conversation a few weeks ago with Chris Coons, who’s going to be the new chairman of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee. At that time, he was hopeful that the Democrats would win the majority, but he certainly didn’t think they would have 55 seats. What Democrats may be able to do with 55 seats and a number of centrist Democrats coming in from places like Kansas, North Carolina, and Texas is a different ball game.

The first stop for Democrats looking to affect national security and foreign policy is appropriations bills and the power of the purse. They can attach policy riders on a must-pass defense authorization bill or put provisions into appropriations bills that outline how money can and cannot be spent. Those are things we definitely will see, especially because there’s a real bipartisan consensus, in particular on Ukraine.

I think we will see, at a minimum, much more aggressive efforts to stop Trump from switching sides in the war. I could see some of those very questionable deals that are being discussed between special envoy Steve Witkoff, Jared Kushner, and the Russians—business deals with Russian entities that might enrich Trump insiders and that no American companies actually want—being a quick casualty of this midterm election result.

RA: I’m imagining that the Kremlin is not going to be looking at these results and smiling. I’m sure they’re a bit worried, although simultaneously it’s also clear that Ukraine needs America less today than it did two years ago. They’re trying to become more resilient, and they’re looking at this election quite warily.

I’m curious how you read this election when it comes to shifting attitudes toward Israel, which just had its own elections. What does it mean for broader U.S. foreign policy in the Middle East?

SG: There is a backlash in both parties, interestingly, about Trump’s embrace of [Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin] Netanyahu and Israel in such an unequivocal way. That’s some of the tone, certainly, of the kind of emergent Vance wing of the party (to the extent there is a Vance wing). There is definitely a backlash in both parties about America’s traditional commitment to Israel in the face of how Netanyahu has prosecuted a scorched-earth war with the Palestinians. That’s true for Democrats in a very dramatic way.

Let’s wait and see what the consequences of Israel’s recent election are. Let’s not forget that they just voted to reject Netanyahu. But given the particularities of that system and his incredible ability to survive, we’ll have to wait and see. Whether Netanyahu or someone else is leading Israel will affect the politics here in Washington.

It’s the Gulf Arabs who have been the engines of a lot of Trump’s foreign policy, not to mention the piggy banks for him and his family. Somebody said to me recently that all the things that we suspect are happening between Putin’s Russia and Trump actually are happening—in terms of vast amounts of sums being directed to Trump and his family—from Gulf interests.

But I think the Gulf Arabs are also very unhappy with Trump. They’re finding out what many others have found out: that Trump doesn’t view relationships as mutual and loyalty is a one-way street with him. Not that long ago, a very senior official from the Middle East asked me: “Why do we have all these American bases and buy all this American military technology to protect ourselves if in fact it’s now the reason that we’re at war? What’s the point of paying the protection money?” It suggests we are at a larger pivot point in the Middle East in terms of geopolitics and the alliances than escaping this narrow quagmire with Iran would suggest. We’re likely looking at a rearrangement of a lot of things in the Middle East in the next two years.

RA: I’m curious how you are thinking about what comes next from the Democratic Party’s side. We’ve all been noting how they won with a very broad tent. There’s a broad slate of candidates, from a Methodist minister in Kansas to a Muslim doctor in Michigan. Are you beginning to detect some coalescing around a coherent foreign policy for the Democrats at this point, or is that a long ways away?

SG: The short answer is “no.” Will Rogers said it a long time ago: “I’m not a member of any organized political party, I’m a Democrat.” Democrats are by definition the more big-tent party in American politics, and they have leaned into that in recent years. Trump spent the last two years campaigning against the very idea of diversity as a strength. So there are some coherent principles that separate these two parties.

Democrats do not have one single coherent view. [Former President Joe] Biden was a backward-looking president in many ways, including with respect to foreign policy. He believed in the possibility of returning to a status quo ante, as a man who was shaped by the foreign-policy debates of the Cold War and perceived threats, opportunities, and alliances through that lens. So there’s a generational shift that’s taking place. I think you’ll see much younger Democratic presidential candidates in 2028. There is also a radical shift in the Senate. This is something people haven’t paid enough attention to. A huge percentage of the Senate is turning over, a much higher percentage than is normal. I think we’re looking at nearly 20 new senators, and the average age of those senators is somewhere in the 50s, and as compared with the average of the people who just left, who would be well into their post-retirement years if they were anybody other than a politician. So a generational shift is definitely going to have consequences on the foreign-policy orientation of the Democrats.

RA: It’s quite literally out with the old.

One thing that we haven’t touched on yet is how this past week could be seen as a repudiation of all the things you’ve said, but also of Big Tech. Elon Musk is the other big loser from this week. He literally poured hundreds of millions of dollars into campaigns, and voters have been quite clear: They’ve rejected data centers. They’ve supported candidates who promised regulation.

SG: It is incredible. Five hundred million dollars is more money than has ever been spent on any election by an individual in the history of ours or any country, by a large order of magnitude. That obscene amount of money produced literally zero victories for him. It’s a remarkable moment. It’s one that will be studied. It happened to play right into a campaign theme that Democrats already had across the country, which is a kind of populism of the center slash center-left. It’s this idea that billionaires and a small circle of oligarchic crony capitalists were conspiring with Trump to make sure that they face no legislative or regulatory roadblocks to reinventing the future however they wanted to and in their own self-interest. So this obscene amount of money came at the exact moment when it was already the message for many Democrats around the country.

Does it mean the new Democratic Congress is going to join hands with the Trump administration and regulate artificial intelligence? I’m going to say “no,” in part because the Democratic Congress, as well as the Republican Congress, opted out of any meaningful regulation in the last round of tech innovation in the era of the internet. They basically punted. They talked and talked and never did anything in a meaningful sense. So I’m very pessimistic about major legislation. But I think this could be probably a defining issue, if not the defining issue in the 2028 presidential election. We may be talking a lot less about Trump and a lot more about AI.

Ravi Agrawal is the editor in chief of Foreign Policy. X: @RaviReports

#6055. Trump highlights NASA nuclear-powered Mars spacecraft mission planned for 2028

President Donald Trump outlined ambitious plans for NASA to launch a nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars by 2028, return American astronauts to the moon and establish a permanent presence on the lunar surface.

President Donald Trump highlighted NASA's ambitious plans to launch its first nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars within the next two years, reaffirming his administration's push to expand American space exploration and eventually send astronauts to the red planet.

Speaking at the UP Summit at Kennedy Space Center in Cape Canaveral, Florida, Trump touted the mission as part of his administration's broader ambitions to expand America's presence beyond Earth.

"Within the next two years, NASA will launch the first-ever nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars," Trump said.

The president described the mission as a step toward eventually sending American astronauts to the planet.

TRUMP CALLS ISS CREW FROM HOUSTON TO PRAISE SPACE PIONEERS AND OFFER 'OVAL OFFICE TIME'

President Donald Trump highlighted NASA's plans to launch a nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars within two years during remarks at the UP Summit at Kennedy Space Center in Florida. (Eric Lee/Reuters)

"We're going to be paving the way for astronauts to plant the Stars and Stripes on the red planet," he said.

The planned mission comes as NASA's Artemis program aims to establish a long-term human presence on the moon before eventually sending astronauts to Mars.

Earlier this year, astronauts completed a lunar flyby during the Artemis II mission. Artemis III is scheduled to test spacecraft and landing systems in Earth orbit in 2027, while Artemis IV is expected to attempt a lunar landing in early 2028, before the end of Trump's presidential term.

PETER NAVARRO: TRUMP'S ARTEMIS VISION IS NOW FLYING — AND CHINA IS PAYING ATTENTION

NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman presents President Donald Trump with a plaque while astronauts greet him during NASA's Max Power event at the Kennedy Space Center, in Merritt Island, Fla., on Nov. 6, 2026. (Joe Skipper/Reuters)

Trump also outlined his administration's plans to accelerate space exploration and establish a lasting American presence beyond Earth, saying the U.S. "will establish a permanent presence on lunar soil."

The president pointed to his recently issued American Declaration of Freedom in Space, which asserts the right of Americans to explore and settle beyond Earth under U.S. law.

"Just this week, I issued the American Declaration of Freedom in Space, proclaiming all of space opened for American settlement, American civilization and American freedom," Trump said.

Before introducing the president, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman also discussed the space agency's Mars ambitions and long-term goals.

CHINA IS RACING TO THE MOON, AND AMERICA MUST CLEAR THE PATH FOR SPACE INNOVATION

A "selfie" taken by NASA's Perseverance Mars rover, made up of 62 individual images, on July 23, in this image released Sept. 10, 2025. (NASA handout via Reuters)

"We are going to venture farther into the solar system and move closer to that next giant leap," Isaacman said.

He described the administration's ultimate goal as the day "American astronauts plant the Stars and Stripes on Mars."

The administration has previously outlined broader space ambitions, including plans for a U.S. Space Academy, which Trump announced in August. The proposed institution would train students for careers in the Space Force, NASA and the civilian spaceflight industry.

Trump also emphasized his broader vision for American space exploration.

The lunar surface fills the frame in sharp detail, as seen during the Artemis II lunar flyby, while a distant Earth sets in the background. "Our calling is to explore. Our purpose is to build, and our aim is always to win," he said.

#6053. Do the Democrats Have a Cohesive Economic Plan?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: Do the Democrats Have a Cohesive Economic Plan?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Prefer to listen? To hear this entire conversation, and more episodes in the weeks ahead, follow Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts.

This week’s congressional elections in the United States saw a historic shift toward Democrats, who won control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, including wins in traditionally red states such as Texas, Kansas, and South Carolina. But with President Donald Trump still in the White House and Democrats still trying to give shape to their own agenda, the implications for economic policy remain unclear.

Was the election outcome primarily fueled by inflation? What are the implications for the AI industry? And what macroeconomic options do the Democrats now have?

This week’s congressional elections in the United States saw a historic shift toward Democrats, who won control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, including wins in traditionally red states such as Texas, Kansas, and South Carolina. But with President Donald Trump still in the White House and Democrats still trying to give shape to their own agenda, the implications for economic policy remain unclear.

Was the election outcome primarily fueled by inflation? What are the implications for the AI industry? And what macroeconomic options do the Democrats now have?

Those are just a few of the questions that came up in my recent conversation with FP economics columnist Adam Tooze on the podcast we co-host, Ones and Tooze. What follows is an excerpt, edited for length and clarity. For the full conversation, look for Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. And check out Adam’s Substack newsletter.

Cameron Abadi: What is the relationship between a political event of this magnitude and mundane cost-of-living issues, which most Americans seem to have been bothered by? Is this the stuff that political tsunamis are made of?

Adam Tooze: The cost-of-living issue, and the continuous shocks of price increases and the felt experience of the exorbitant level of prices, has probably not been as pressing as it is in the current moment in many parts of the U.S. since the 1970s. So at that level, there’s a kind of epochal quality to this. And the other thing surely to say is that nor have we had a government, an administration and a supportive Congress, so blithely refusing to simply take seriously any of those issues in a meaningful way. And it’s that combination, I think, which creates this outcome. It would have been damaging to everyone’s sort of self-understanding as a rational political analyst if something like this outcome had not happened.

Because how can you have this level of dissatisfaction over prices and price increases and a government committed to protectionism, to starting a war, to incredibly unequal tax breaks, to promoting a growth model that is so K-shaped, that is so promoting a wealth inequality, and not get a backlash? That would have tested the ongoing debate about whether the ’90s formula, “It’s the economy, stupid”—this would really have put a stake through the heart of that theory with garlic on top if we had not seen this backlash.

CA: This kind of epochal shift inevitably sparks debates about new, or renewed, political taxonomies in the United States. The term that is now being used to describe many of the new senators and governors elected by Democrats in unlikely places is “prairie populism.” And I’m curious what that term might mean when it comes to economic policy?

AT: Going all the way back to the late 19th century, there has been a brand of radical, challenging, anti-status quo politics, in contrast to socialism, which was populism. Populism came out of the prairie, out of the Midwest of the prairie states of the agricultural constituency, because it was about the way in which the gilded age economy in the United States was rigged in the interests of coastal elites—basically New York and the East coast elite.

And that’s the echo that’s being picked up on in this sort of talk. It’s a little exaggerated because there’s a huge difference between present day consumer orientated cost of living type of politics and the sort of radicalism, the deep structural changes that late 19th-century populists were demanding—who were really challenging the fundamental structures of the American political economy of that moment. And I do think that’s one of the fundamental questions for the Democrats right now, is that coming out of the midterms of 2018—which were also a Democratic breakthrough, against the first Trump presidency, though not on the current scale—there was a real sense that the discourse of American politics shifted. You got figures like Jake Sullivan spiraling to the top of the democratic pile on an agenda that was an explicit break in neoliberalism.

And I don’t quite know where that goes right now, whether there is a mood on the large part of the Democratic Party to resume that kind of quite more fundamental questioning of the orthodoxies or whether we take all that for granted now and we’re going to just focus on trying to deliver. I think we haven’t quite got the bearings yet of what this victory means. It was predicted, but I haven’t so far seen on the part of the Democrats a kind of coherent, new, comprehensive agenda—even in the sense of defining what its limits would be and how far one wants to go—emerging at this point.

CA: Polls suggest that many voters in the United States share skepticism about artificial intelligence and the role it’s playing in the economy, specifically the proliferation of data centers. What is the future of AI potentially with this new Democratic-controlled Congress?

AT: It’s been quite something to behold, the anxiety running through Wall Street over the last couple of days and kind of climaxing last night about the potential threat to the data center build out. Because America’s path to the AI future is very compute heavy. It’s all about data center build outs. And if you look at the states where this is happening, apart from Virginia, Ohio, Pennsylvania, I think the central play is Texas. And there was a real lot of big tech money mobilized on this issue of trying to ensure that a group of AI friendly politicians were elected.

And on the contrary then, the groundswell of Democratic mobilization was around AI and data centers as kind of a bundling issue, right? It’s about local pollution from the noise and everything else. It’s about local electricity rates and the impact that these huge consumers have on local energy prices. And it’s about AI as AI, its direct role on the labor market. And it’s about AI as the symbol of an irresponsible, unanswerable kind of oligarchy that just stamps itself on the face of American society. And these were a very powerful combination.

And I think Wall Street analysts were counting 40 or 41 local referenda, local motions that are being passed. All of which I think went to the anti-AI side. And in Texas, we’re talking about 60 gigawatts of buildouts, so an absolutely gigantic buildout valued at $200 billion, I think. And now the Democrats have a much more powerful hold on Texas politics than any time recently. And as we know, in the regional political economy of the United States, Texas and Florida are two great red state counter-examples to the New York and California visions of America’s future. And so for this break to happen is really very dramatic.

And it does form a connection between this electoral result and the market. On the morning after the election, though the S&P 500 as a whole was doing fine, Tesla had sold off, CoreWeave, all of these cloud companies have sold off quite hard. Trump’s media platforms are down, unsurprisingly. So it does look as though there is a selective shakeout happening across stock that are very exposed on the AI issue, very exposed to this blowback and are, in any case, over the last couple of months, looking increasingly fragile from a financial point of view.

CA: Democrats are promising to make life for ordinary Americans more affordable, but how they’ll do that is still somewhat of an open question. On a macroeconomic level, could finding a way to lower interest rates be something that Democrats in Congress turn their attention to?

AT: I think this is a really interesting question. I mean, there’s a real tension between projects of trying to address the cost of living crisis through concrete interventions on particular prices and market structure and price gouging on drugs and microeconomic policy strategies and the bigger macroeconomic picture.

And of course, Congress doesn’t shape that bigger macroeconomic picture. Really except through fiscal policy. It doesn’t have a direct influence on interest rates. If it wants to see interest rates come down, the way to do that, presumably, would be to adopt tough fiscal policy. It doesn’t necessarily mean you slash spending, but you would need to raise taxes and you’d need to actually build a coalition that was willing to do that. And there’s lots of ways of doing that in a progressive way. But that’s not something the Democrats have been keen to do recently, really, is to push redistributional taxes. Or you have to put the Fed in a position where it has no option but to do various very unconventional interventions. Which again is perfectly logical and sensible from a left Keynesian point of view. But it’s a breach of the kind of orthodoxy which the Democrats have embraced all the time outside moments of absolute crisis.

So I do think this question of what the agenda of the Democrats is really, macroeconomically, is quite unclear. Because we’re clearly no longer in the Larry Summers Bill Clinton 1990s era. We left that behind in the Biden administration. What the agenda, what the paradigm now is I think is very open-ended. Take the issue of AI. One of the effects of the AI boom is to suck foreign money into the American equity markets. It’s one of the fundamental ways in which the American balance payments now currently balances. If that goes away—and there’s very good reasons for wanting to embrace that as American’s future, slow this down, try and close the two legs of the K-shaped economy—the most likely prediction is that the dollar depreciates, the dollar devalues.

And again, you’ll find people saying, Well, that will be great for industrial policy, that’s exactly what we need, American producers are more competitive, who needs tariffs if you have a devaluing dollar? But the dollar is also American purchasing power. And so if the dollar devalues appreciably, seriously, this raises the price of American imports. It raises the cost of buying foreign goods for American consumers. It isn’t a way to increase purchasing power and address the cost-of-living crisis. It does the opposite, right? It restricts imports by way of raising the price of foreign goods.

Now, this isn’t one-to-one or simple, but there’s just a series of tensions here that are not squarely addressed at the current moment. There hasn’t been any cause to address them, though I also think there was an agenda around the Biden administration that pretty clearly spelled out some of the trade-offs here. For instance, we would intentionally slow down the renewable energy surge in the U.S. because it involved Chinese solar panels and that wasn’t the top priority, actually.

So what are the new priorities? What is the new set? If the cost of living is the absolute priority over everything else, then you would think that a radical free trade agenda would be the way to go. But Congress can’t do that without a compliant White House. And which Democrat is actually going to run on that, given what we know the Biden administration did, which was to maintain Trump’s tariffs from the first administration? And big parts of the Democratic coalition, notably what’s left of the blue collar trade unions, are, shall we say, pro-tariff, broadly speaking. Not Trump’s crazy ones, but they are not interested in ensuring that American consumers can have the cheapest possible affordable family car. That’s not their agenda. So there’s a whole series of really quite complicated trade-offs here that will have to be faced again.
