#6052. What Sanctions Get Right

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

S. C. M. PAINE is William S. Sims University Professor of History and Grand Strategy Emerita at the U.S. Naval War College.

ANAND TOPRANI is a Jeane Kirkpatrick Fellow at the American Enterprise Institute.

They are the co-editors of The Strategy of Sanctions: From Antiquity to the Twenty-First Century.

The United States frequently uses sanctions or embargoes to rein in malign actors. When policymakers become dissatisfied with the results, they often embrace military action next. Washington, for example, applied sanctions on Iraq for more than a decade before invading the country in 2003. It did the same with Iran, using sanctions to pressure the government into nuclear negotiations in the 2010s and then, after withdrawing from the resulting agreement in 2018, going to war with Tehran in February 2026.

Washington follows this pattern of behavior because sanctions alone rarely—if ever—make a problem disappear, such as by toppling the government responsible. This frustrates Americans, who tend to seek simple, quick, and permanent fixes to foreign policy problems. Many interpret the persistence of these problems as evidence that sanctions are futile. But the effectiveness of sanctions is measured in crises that do not occur—the nuclear test that never happens, for instance, or the shipping lane that remains undisturbed. These successes are invisible, but an adversary’s continuing provocations are not. As a result, American officials often conclude that it is necessary to turn to war.

This logic reflects a misunderstanding about how sanctions work. Sanctions may not be able to achieve maximalist aims, yet when they are deployed in concert with other policy tools, they can help manage problems. They do this by reliably depressing the target country’s economic growth over time. Although this hurts innocent civilians in the targeted countries as their leaders shift the costs from regime supporters to the politically disenfranchised, no government can escape the inexorable math of slowing economic growth. If an economy starts out with a three percent growth rate—roughly the global average—it will double in size in around 23 years. But if sanctions reduce annual growth to one percent, doubling in size will take 70 years, three times as long. Just compare the North Korean and South Korean economies, or look at nighttime satellite imagery of the Korean Peninsula, to see the compounding effects of sanctions over generations. Although sanctions may not change the targeted regime’s aspirations, they reduce its financial wherewithal and may deter other governments from emulating the behavior that precipitated the sanctions.

Wars can overthrow adversarial regimes, but usually only after years of fighting, and fixing the damage afterward takes considerable time and money. Too often the United States misidentifies its foreign policy problems as solvable on the cheap. It took an Allied occupation force of over 1.5 million soldiers to garrison Germany, a country of 80 million, after World War II. Fast forward 60 years, and Washington was not interested in raising the numbers of troops necessary to subdue Iraq, a country of nearly 30 million. Americans today are likewise unwilling to make sacrifices on the scale necessary to vanquish Iran, with its 90 million people—much less China, with its 1.4 billion. The challenges these countries might pose are too large to solve at an acceptable cost. In such cases, sanctions offer an economically sustainable strategy to manage troublesome situations in the long term.

According to the conventional wisdom about sanctions, these measures discourage unsavory behavior by raising its economic costs. If force becomes necessary, they also weaken the adversary in advance of war. This logic has endured since 1935, when the League of Nations sanctioned Italy for invading Ethiopia, in the hope that doing so would induce Italy to call off its invasion or at least temper its ambitions. Yet because the countries imposing sanctions were unwilling to include vital commodities such as oil and coal on the list of sanctioned items, the effort was ineffective.

Sanctions have evolved since 1935. Today, unlike broad sanctions that inflict significant collateral damage, so-called smart sanctions target specific institutions or individuals and are enforced through the banking system, blocking financial transactions linked to sanctioned activities. UN sanctions against Iraq starting in 1990 were, in a sense, the last gasp of the old sanctions regime. Their lifting was contingent on Iraq’s dismantling its weapons of mass destruction program and agreeing to respect Kuwaiti sovereignty. Because verification of the former proved difficult, many of the sanctions remained in place after a 1991 armistice, and they continued through the 2003 Iraq war. After U.S.-led forces invaded Iraq in March 2003 and overthrew Saddam Hussein, it became apparent that the sanctions had been effective. Isolation from the global economy had prevented Iraq from restoring its economy, rebuilding its military, and resuming its development of weapons of mass destruction.

Between the two wars, however, many observers believed that international sanctions were unable to deter Iraqi aggression and neutralize its weapons programs. Worried that Iraq’s Sunni government still posed a threat to Kurds in the north and Shiites in the south, the United States, the United Kingdom, and, for a short time, France imposed two no-fly zones over those regions, without UN consent. In 1998, after Baghdad expelled UN weapons inspectors, the United States and the United Kingdom bombed Iraq. The same year, a group of hawkish scholars and former government officials sent an open letter to U.S. President Bill Clinton describing the sanctions-based Iraq policy as “dangerously inadequate.” Many of these signatories would return to government in the George W. Bush administration to implement their recommendations.

To a certain extent, the critics had a point. Political divisions within the United States and between Washington and its partners had constrained international efforts to contain Saddam. Arab governments may have been wary of hurting their relationships with the United States, but Arab publics were sick of Western imperialism. The growing U.S. military presence in the Middle East further antagonized both the anti-imperialist left and radical Islamists, and many Europeans resented American unilateralism.

Saddam also pushed doctored stories about civilian hardship arising from sanctions, which Western journalists readily covered. Iraqi suffering was very real, but it was largely a result of Saddam’s own policies, which channeled resources to his regime rather than to the civilian population. Starting in 1995, the UN’s oil-for-food program allowed Iraq to sell some oil to pay for civilian necessities, but Saddam used the profits to pay kickbacks instead. He signed lucrative oil deals with China, France, and Russia, enticing these permanent UN Security Council members to weaken their support for sanctions. It was around this time that the UN and civil society groups started calling for smart sanctions, to narrow the scope of economic punishment and avoid causing wide-scale economic pain, although it would take several more years for the United States to refine this tool.

In the meantime, in Iraq, sanctions and no-fly-zone enforcement became increasingly frustrating, costly, and unpopular. Because their main purpose was to prevent Iraq from developing weapons of mass destruction and funding malign activities such as international terrorism, they required constant vigilance. Then came the 9/11 attacks and the war in Afghanistan, where a U.S.-led coalition toppled the Taliban regime within months, suggesting that wars—at least those conducted by the highly trained, technologically advanced U.S. military—could solve problems, unlike the sanctions that left dictators in place.

When U.S. President George W. Bush announced the Iraq war on March 19, 2003, he described its goals as “to disarm Iraq, to free its people, and to defend the world from grave danger.” This danger was Iraq’s alleged stockpiles of weapons of mass destruction and its links to al-Qaeda, the group behind 9/11. At first, Washington intended to leave the task of reconstructing Iraq to the UN, but it had to abandon that strategy five months after the invasion when foreign jihadists bombed the UN’s local headquarters, killing the head of the mission and 22 others. Within weeks, the UN withdrew most of its 600 staff members, leaving the United States fully in charge.

Rather than ending the trouble Iraq caused, occupation created more problems. The war lent credence to al-Qaeda’s accusations that Washington oppressed Muslims, attracting would-be jihadists around the world to join the cause. In 2006, al-Qaeda’s bombing of the Golden Mosque in Samarra, a holy site for Shiites, triggered brutal Sunni–Shiite fighting. More than 4,000 Americans lost their lives over the course of the war, while more than 200,000 Iraqis lost theirs. After the U.S. withdrawal in 2011, much of the Middle East and North Africa went up in flames. Foreign jihadists and remnants of Saddam’s Baath Party formed the Islamic State, also known as ISIS, and overran much of Iraq and Syria in 2014 and 2015. U.S.- and Iranian-backed forces gradually and at great cost pushed the group out, but many of its fighters simply took up arms elsewhere. Some went to Sahelian Africa, exacerbating violence there. Others joined the opposition to Bashar al-Assad’s regime in Syria. And the refugee crisis sparked in part by the Islamic State’s initial expansion continues to roil Europe.

Whereas war brought spiraling instability to the Middle East, North Africa, and Europe, international sanctions were more effective than U.S. policymakers thought. While they were in place, Iraq was unable to build weapons of mass destruction, and it dismantled many of its existing programs to secure sanctions relief. The 2003 war was not nearly as successful and cost far more than sanctions enforcement. Washington went in with the aim of building a free and stable Iraq allied to the United States and Israel, but it quickly became clear that goal was out of reach.

Another unintended consequence of the war was the unleashing of Iran, which for years Saddam’s Iraq had kept in check. Iran and the United States had been at loggerheads since the Iranian revolution of 1978–79. In November 1979, Washington sanctioned Iran for taking the U.S. embassy’s staff members hostage, lifting them upon the staff’s release in 1981. The Reagan administration reimposed sanctions in 1984 in retaliation for Iran’s support for Hezbollah in Lebanon, only to violate them by selling arms to Iran (and using the funds to arm anticommunist Nicaraguan rebels, in defiance of Congress) in what became the Iran-contra scandal. The Clinton administration, concerned about Tehran’s nuclear ambitions, added sanctions between 1994 and 1996. After the International Atomic Energy Agency found Iran to be in noncompliance with the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty in 2005, the Bush administration imposed additional sanctions, but they targeted specific entities, such as the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, rather than Iran as a whole to minimize the humanitarian costs. Then, in 2007, the world got behind a UN Security Council resolution sanctioning Iran to prevent it from acquiring nuclear weapons. Finally, in 2010 and 2011, the Obama administration, with congressional backing, limited Iran’s ability to sell oil and gas abroad, “virtually grinding the Iranian economy to halt,” as the president bragged.

The growing strength and scope of sanctions contributed to Iran’s election of a moderate president, Hassan Rouhani, in 2013 and empowered the Iranian factions advocating a negotiated settlement to the nuclear standoff. In 2015, Tehran agreed to a nuclear deal with the United States, China, France, Germany, Russia, the United Kingdom, and the EU. The agreement capped Iran’s uranium enrichment levels, reduced its operational centrifuge numbers, required Iran to repurpose its nuclear weapons development facilities, and established more rigorous international inspections to verify compliance. Sanctions relief would occur in tandem with verification over a 15-year period. Thereafter, either the signatories could negotiate a new agreement, or the sanctions would snap back into place. Critics complained, justifiably, that the 2015 deal did not prevent Iran from supporting proxy groups or limit its ballistic missile program. But the deal did offer a sustainable way to delay Iran’s nuclear weapons development and thereby manage the most important problem posed by a hostile Iranian regime. A more comprehensive solution, however desirable, was not feasible at the time.

The United States is discovering, yet again, that military action has delivered a far worse outcome than sanctions. After the first Trump administration withdrew from the nuclear deal in 2018, Iran restarted its weapons program. The second Trump administration and Israel went to war early this year in an ostensible effort to end Iran’s nuclear ambitions but proved unable to destroy all its nuclear sites. The United States and Israel assassinated Iran’s leadership, including the supreme leader and much of his family, yet the regime remains intact. Tehran has promised retribution for these killings, and there is no reason to think it will not follow through on the threat. Meanwhile, the war has wreaked global havoc with no end in sight. Iran responded to U.S. and Israeli attacks by effectively closing the Strait of Hormuz and striking oil, gas, and military infrastructure across the Middle East. The disruption of exports of petroleum, fertilizer, and helium has contributed to inflation, portends crop shortfalls, and even threatens computer chip shortages in countries around the world.

Although U.S. military casualties remain low, U.S. losses in destroyed infrastructure and depleted munitions stocks amount to billions of dollars, and some of it may be irreplaceable. The American military depends on satellite communications and radar to detect and target incoming enemy missiles and aircraft. Iran has heavily damaged or perhaps destroyed air defense systems the United States and its partners deployed at bases in the United Arab Emirates and Jordan, as well as two satellite communications terminals at a U.S. naval base in Bahrain. Iranian strikes also destroyed radomes, the outer shells that protect radar antennae, in Bahrain, Kuwait, and Saudi Arabia. The United States has lost at least 43 warplanes, including specialized refueling and surveillance aircraft and a surveillance plane worth $700 million. Many U.S. military bases are now inoperable, including the main regional naval base, in Bahrain. Because U.S. aircraft cannot operate safely from nearby bases, the war effort has relied on distant aircraft carriers positioned in the eastern Mediterranean and Arabian Seas, where they can avoid the dangers of Iranian missiles, drones, and mines.

Far from removing the Iranian regime, the war has demonstrated that it has the power to hold the global economy hostage. By comparison, the prewar sanctions that devastated Iran’s economy, hobbled its military, and discouraged it from seeking nuclear weapons were a bargain.

Sanctions have a far better track record than war in managing unsolvable problems at an acceptable price for a protracted period. With sanctions, the target country—including its civilian population, unfortunately—pays the costs. In war, by contrast, the costs are shared by both sides, and the collateral damage can be global, drawing the ire of countries distant from the battlefield.

Managing a problem through sanctions can contain it and contribute, along with other measures, to victory in the long run. During the Cold War, U.S. leaders understood that any military attack on the Soviet Union would trigger a ruinous nuclear war. Instead, they managed the Soviet threat for generations with a strategy of containment, which relied heavily on sanctions that blocked exports—the Soviet economy’s means of earning hard currency—and starved the Communist bloc of high-tech imports. The United States established the Coordinating Committee for Multilateral Export Controls for this purpose in 1949, together with Belgium, France, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, and the United Kingdom. Australia, Canada, Denmark, Germany, Greece, Japan, Norway, Portugal, Spain, and Turkey would eventually join. To enforce the controls, the 1951 Mutual Assistance Control Act authorized the cutoff of U.S. economic and military aid to violators. East–West trade plummeted. After the Korean War, Washington reluctantly bowed to European pressure to relax some restrictions. But after détente failed, the Carter and Reagan administrations again limited high-tech exports and tried to block Soviet sales of gas.

The Soviet Union could never overcome the economic damage from these measures. Sanctions greatly exacerbated the bloc’s preexisting economic weaknesses, such as its prioritization of heavy industry over consumer goods, its isolation from the free exchange of ideas, and its inefficient central planning based on mountains of inaccurate statistics. Soviet residents were left queuing up for subpar consumer goods. By the 1980s, new Soviet leadership concluded that their system could never close the growing economic, technological, and military gap between East and West, and they called off the Cold War.

Economic pressure by no means assures victory. But the alternative, war, rarely works as intended. In Iran today, U.S. leaders are learning that big ships and fast planes can close but not open narrow seas. A war ends when both sides agree it is over, so Iran can prolong the conflict even if the United States wants out. And even if the fighting ends soon, none of the prospective deals the Trump administration has announced to eliminate Iran’s nuclear program improve on the terms of the 2015 nuclear agreement. The now defunct memorandum of understanding that Washington and Tehran signed in June promised to lift sanctions and funnel billions to Iran in return for Iran’s reopening the Strait of Hormuz, possibly with Iranian tollbooths. Before the war, freedom of navigation through the strait was not in question.

There are two ways to end conflicts: either remove the capacity to resist or remove the will. Both sanctions and military action diminish an adversary’s ability to resist, but the bloodshed of war makes it more likely than sanctions to harden will. War, therefore, can make the underlying problem even more difficult to resolve. The cases of Iraq in 2003 and Iran in 2026 show that there was a better way. Sanctions alone would not have ended either threat, but even at their least effective, they proved useful at managing both situations, and they did so at a lower cost. Rather than a silver bullet, sanctions should be understood as a form of diplomatic chemotherapy to shrink the tumor and thereby diminish the threat. Sometimes that is the best option.

#6047. The Missing Critical Minerals Investment

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Tom Moerenhout is a Professor at Columbia University’s School of Public and International Affairs, Director of Critical Materials at Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy, and Executive Director at the Geneva Platform for Resilient Value Chains.

Kevin Brunelli is a Nonresident Fellow at Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy and previously served in the Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Supply Chains at the U.S. Department of State.

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

The United States and its allies are finally taking supply chains for critical minerals seriously. China has spent the last three decades building a dominant share of global mineral production, and the export controls Beijing has introduced periodically since 2023 have made clear just how dangerous it is for other countries to rely on China for access to materials that are essential components of semiconductors, defense technologies, and automobiles.

Washington and European capitals are now trying to minimize their exposure. Alongside private firms, these governments have begun investing in both upstream industries including mines, refineries, and recycling infrastructure and downstream industries such as magnets and batteries. The bulk of their efforts so far have focused on mines already in operation or close to it and on refining. It is true that the United States and Europe do not have sufficient facilities to process raw ore into specialized chemicals or refined materials and that building more refining facilities will help reduce their reliance on China and alleviate immediate supply chain concerns.

But this is a temporary fix. Refining facilities are valuable only if they have a steady, long-term supply of raw materials. That, in turn, requires new mines, especially as advanced industries demand ever more minerals. Because building a mine takes roughly 20 years, ensuring a long-term supply of minerals requires investing in new mines now. China, which is committed to maintaining its supply chain dominance, has been willing to allocate substantial capital to such ventures. Japan has, too, ever since China blocked exports of critical minerals to the country amid a dispute over the Senkaku Islands (known in China as the Diaoyu Islands) in 2010, which pushed Tokyo to start the long process of disentangling its own supply chains. But Japan still has a long way to go; after 15 years of investing, the country has reduced its dependence on China for rare earths from about 90 percent to between 65 and 70 percent—not nearly enough to supply its own needs, much less those of its allies.

In short, investing in refining is necessary but insufficient. To avoid future supply chain vulnerabilities, the United States and its allies, especially those in the G-7, must also start building a pipeline of new mines. These investments are long bets, and they are risky. But when successful, they reap high rewards.

The United States and its allies significantly outperform China in mineral exploration. Australian and Canadian firms alone account for roughly one-third of global exploration spending. But the vast majority of geological prospects never become operating mines. This is not for lack of financing in prospecting but because of the many financial disincentives and risks that appear once a mining project moves forward.

The initial stages of mining development rely chiefly on a unique ecosystem of mining companies called “juniors,” so called because they have a small market cap, focus exclusively on one or a few projects, and are nowhere large enough to list on major stock exchanges. Most of them list in Australia or Canada and concentrate on the initial high-risk exploration stages. Geological uncertainty makes betting on junior ventures extraordinarily risky, but if a mineral deposit proves to have significant reserves, the junior can sell the project or bring in a new partner, and that payout is large.

Yet in the next stages, investors tend to lose patience or run out of capital. Although initial prospecting is relatively cheap, subsequent feasibility studies that estimate a deposit’s size, engineering needs, costs, and expected returns can easily run into the tens of millions of dollars, in addition to the costs of drilling, engineering, permitting, and development planning. Commercial lenders stay away because these projects have no immediate revenue, no assets to offer as collateral, and no guarantees they will even be viable. The risk, in short, is too high to take for uncertain returns. Large mining companies also avoid these projects because they face pressure from shareholders to provide maximum returns. Even as demand for minerals has risen, the share of revenue that major mining companies have reinvested in new development has stayed flat.

Without outside financing, juniors cannot complete the studies needed to attract additional financing for construction, even if they have found a promising deposit. As single-asset companies, they have no other cash flow or assets to offer as collateral for additional loans. This moment is often referred to as the “valley of death.” Many companies looking for a way out of this predicament have turned to one reliable source of investment: China. Chinese companies are much more willing to take on this type of risk and provide the necessary capital to advance mining projects, and they are often the only viable option for juniors. Thus, China’s dominance in global supply chains relies on Western companies’ exploration. It endures not because China offers more competitive terms but because at a key financing stage, it is the only game in town.

China has been expanding its supply pipeline for critical minerals for decades. In 2025, it spent a record $32 billion on metals and mining projects through its Belt and Road Initiative, breaking the previous year’s record of $21 billion. Chinese firms are also willing to invest in equity, buying ownership stakes in companies that come with no guaranteed returns. Additional spending takes the form of resources-for-infrastructure deals, by which China funds infrastructure such as ports, energy facilities, and railways in mineral-rich countries in exchange for a share in the profits of mining projects—which Chinese companies and state banks may also help finance. By investing in a broad portfolio of mineral projects across several countries, Chinese firms can let the profits of those that succeed cover the losses of those that fail.

Apart from capital, the most important part of China’s mining strategy is know-how. Because failure is so common, the ability to identify deposits with the highest potential to deliver is crucial. Beijing relies on specialized state-owned enterprises, geological firms, private companies, and government officials to vet projects and work with host governments to secure permits, infrastructure, and delivery. The only G-7 country that maintains a comparable on-the-ground presence for mining projects is Japan, in countries such as Australia, Canada, Peru, and Uzbekistan. These projects are intended to secure critical minerals that can support Japan’s industries, but they are currently insufficient to meet all of Japan’s production needs, let alone broader allied needs.

The disparity between China’s mining investments and those of the United States and its allies is most evident in Africa, home to some of the largest untapped mineral resources. Whereas the United States invested about $300 million in critical mineral projects on the continent in 2023, China invested about $8 billion to $10 billion. Washington has recognized the need to close this gap, but it still has a long way to go.

In the Democratic Republic of the Congo, a particular focus of the Trump administration, Chinese companies control about 70 percent of cobalt and copper mines. Between 2008 and 2020, Chinese banks and companies extended over $9 billion in credit for mine development and operations in Congo, in addition to $3 billion in infrastructure loans from the Export-Import Bank of China. The United States and other G-7 countries had the opportunity to take part in these investments but judged the projects to be too risky. In 2015, for example, the Canadian-listed mining company Ivanhoe Mines sought funding for its Kamoa-Kakula copper project in Congo. With no Western investors willing to take on the risk, the Chinese firm Zijin Mining acquired an ownership stake. Today, Kamoa-Kakula is on track to become one of the largest copper mines in the world. Meanwhile, in 2016 and 2020, the only major American mining company active in Congo sold its stakes in two substantial copper-cobalt deposits, its only remaining stakes in the country, to a Chinese mining company.

The owners of a mine control where its minerals eventually go. When Chinese firms own a mine, the output flows either to their refineries or downstream manufacturers in China. The Chinese battery giant CATL, for example, has an ownership stake in the Kisanfu mine in Congo, where it secures a steady supply of copper and cobalt for its batteries. U.S. automakers and allied battery producers have also invested in some projects, but none to the same degree—even still, much of that material flows through China. The concentration of this refined material in China gives Beijing ever-greater market power and the ability to weaponize it.

In theory, the United States and its allies have the means of financing their own pipeline of future mining projects. In 2024, two Toronto-based stock exchanges hosted nearly 1,100 publicly listed mining companies, 40 percent of the global total. These companies account for roughly one-third of the world’s total mining equity capital raised over the last five years. About half of the listed companies’ projects were based in Canada. The Australian Stock Exchange is another key exchange, with about 850 companies listed in 2024 for mining development.

But these companies are facing pressures that discourage investment in promising projects. The world’s most accessible mineral deposits have already been tapped, so potential new deposits are often located in more complicated geological environments, making the early stages of exploration more difficult and costly. Moving from exploration to production is also taking more time, as increased social and environmental requirements draw out permitting processes. This lengthening timeline lowers private investors’ appetite for risk because they have to wait longer for their investments to start generating returns. At the same time, Chinese overproduction of critical minerals has depressed prices for key commodities. Despite strong signals that demand for the minerals will continue to increase, these low prices discourage new players from entering the game and weaken investors’ incentives to help those already in it.

U.S. and allied governments therefore need to draw in substantial new investment. The United States has made a start by taking equity stakes in domestic and international mining companies and expanding loans to them. Official announcements claim that these investments total around $50 billion, although the value of the deals that have been signed falls closer to $9 billion, and the money actually moving adds up to around $4 billion. Most of this money does not go to the projects languishing in the valley of death. Instead, the projects getting funded are those that are closer to production, because they can help tackle immediate supply risks.

This is a good start, but ventures that remain at early stages still need funding, and private investors are still wary of mining projects. Since 2025, the few institutional investors that have gotten involved tended to do so after the U.S. government announced an equity stake or loan to back a project. Although such caution is not new, relying on government signals and public investment sets up a perverse incentive for private companies not to invest in the absence of public support, which is exactly what governments want to avoid.

To really pull in private capital for exploration and mine development, the United States and the European Union should take a page from Canada’s book. The Canadian government offers tax credits to companies engaged in mineral exploration, and its “flow-through shares” system helps mining companies finance exploration and development by offering tax incentives to investors. If the rest of the G-7 can establish this kind of financial architecture, far more exploration capital would flow to critical mineral projects. And if member countries link their systems by letting investors claim tax benefits for exploration anywhere in the G-7, they could build the large, shared pool of exploration and development capital needed to support projects through to construction. In addition, G-7 countries must remove barriers for large institutional investors to enter the mining sector—primarily by reforming their tax codes, which currently disincentivize tax-exempt investors, such as pension funds, endowments, and foundations, from investing in mining projects because returns on such investments can be taxed as income.

Finally, when the incentives for private investment still do not line up for certain projects, G-7 countries should use public capital to directly help mining companies escape the valley of death. To do this, the United States, Japan, and European countries should set up a joint facility to pool financing for feasibility studies, which are particularly difficult to raise private funding for, establishing a broad portfolio so that the losses from any failures are covered by the winners. This pooled fund would allocate grants to promising projects in exchange for equity in the project or a royalty once the mine goes into production. Many projects would still fail, but with a dedicated, well-staffed facility, the quality of investments would likely improve—as they have for China, which takes a similar portfolio approach to early-stage mining. And the benefits to both investors and national interests would far outweigh the lost capital.

Today’s chokepoints for critical minerals sit mostly at the processing or refining stage, but future threats to the minerals needed for manufacturing, energy, artificial intelligence, and defense technology will be at the mines. G-7 countries have the markets and the policy tools to build a pipeline of secure mining projects and prevent the next economic emergency. Investment in refining facilities means little if the United States and its allies have no minerals to feed them a decade from now. The only choice, then, is to commit to financing new mining projects now.

#6045. DeSantis blasts Washington Post over report he's preparing 2028 presidential bid

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis said Wednesday following the midterm elections that Democrats ‘did not earn a mandate to indulge all their left-wing fantasies.’

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis fired back Friday at a report that he is preparing another White House bid in 2028, accusing The Washington Post of fabricating claims that he has started reaching out to political consultants about a potential campaign.

"The Washington Post is fabricating this out of whole cloth," DeSantis wrote on X. "I have not reached out to anybody about running for anything."

"Just another pathetic attempt to garner clicks. Using anonymous sources of course!"

Citing three people briefed on his plans, the Post reported Friday that DeSantis was preparing to seek the Republican presidential nomination in 2028.

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis speaks during a news conference at the ICE Enforcement and Removal Operations office in Miramar, Fla., May 1. (Joe Raedle/Getty Images)

The newspaper reported that DeSantis, who unsuccessfully ran for president in 2024 and is term-limited after 2026, had already reached out to at least one national political consultant about working on a prospective campaign.

A federal super PAC tied to DeSantis also sent out a fundraising text earlier this week, The Post reported.

DeSantis continued pushing back against the report in exchanges with social media users.

When one user expressed hope that the Post's reporting was accurate, the governor replied that he was "sorry to disappoint."

DeSantis also responded to another user who suggested political consultants may have been using his name to attract attention.

"Probably the case. But maybe just made up by WaPo," he wrote.

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis accused The Washington Post of "fabricating this out of whole cloth" after the newspaper reported that he was preparing a 2028 presidential campaign. (Andrew Harrer/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Speculation surrounding DeSantis' political future comes just days after Republicans suffered sweeping defeats in the midterm elections, losing control of both chambers of Congress and several traditionally Republican strongholds.

The Sunshine State, however, remained a bright spot for the GOP, with Republican Byron Donalds winning the governor's race.

"This is close to an extinction-level event for Republicans," DeSantis said Wednesday, warning that, among the nation's 10 most populous states, Florida would be the only one with Republican control of its government.

#6044. Two New Stand-Out Novels From Ireland and South Africa

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Books: Two New Stand-Out Novels From Ireland and South Africa

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

This month, we’re reading two new novels that explore the long shadow of shame and secrecy across Ireland and South Africa.

Louise Kennedy (Riverhead Books, 288 pp., $28, November 2026)

This month, we’re reading two new novels that explore the long shadow of shame and secrecy across Ireland and South Africa.

Louise Kennedy (Riverhead Books, 288 pp., $28, November 2026)

The first half of Stations, Louise Kennedy’s sophomore novel, reads like a typical tale of unrequited love—albeit with trademark Irish melancholy. But then things get properly dark.

Kennedy tells the story in snippets that span decades. It begins in County Kildare, Ireland, in 1982, during the Troubles. Roisin McCaffrey—an “actively unpopular” teenager—strikes up an unlikely friendship with her rabble-rousing classmate, Redmond “Red” Camelin, whom she describes as “like Dennis the Menace.” Both have strained relationships with their fathers.

Roisin’s father is an alcoholic who is unable to hold down a job. Although the McCaffreys are Northern Irish Catholics, he spent a year in the Royal Ulster Constabulary before moving the family south because he had become “a legitimate target for Republican paramilitaries.” Eventually, he leaves Roisin and her mother, settling in London with a new family that he also later abandons. Roisin “hated that people were known by what their fathers did, aware that she was known for her dad’s abscondment.”

Less is immediately clear about Red’s father, though Red runs away from home while in secondary school, preferring to crash on friends’ couches than cohabitate with his parents. Roisin intuits that “there was something dreadful in Red’s family.”

Roisin and Red engage in standard teenage flirtation and friendship—and, on one occasion, share a kiss—but Red insists that their relationship is strictly platonic. That’s not for Roisin’s lack of trying. After secondary school, she drops out of college to move to London, crashing in Red’s Pimlico squat and working odd jobs. Roisin amasses a roster of romantic conquests but never manages to interest Red. He, meanwhile, is drawn deeper into a dangerous drug scene. In the late 1980s—when “Hep C and HIV were rampant”—Roisin makes the difficult decision to cut ties with Red.

Kennedy leaves a gap between 1989 and 2007, when she finds Roisin again, this time at her father’s funeral. A chance encounter with a classmate (and ex-lover) who also moved from Kildare to London leads Roisin to embark on a monthslong search for Red. In the process, she uncovers disturbing details about his family and subsequent drug use.

Pain seeps through the pages of Stations. The various traumas that Roisin and Red endure—as victims of a sectarian conflict, as immigrants to the U.K., as the children of troubled fathers, and, in Red’s case, as an addict—are inseparable from Irish history. Their story begins when Ireland is still a poor country. In the “vast, disinterested city” of London, they are met with ridicule for their names and accents and struggle to make ends meet. For relief, they turn to substance use.

Despite their material hardships, for both for both Roisin and Red, returning to Ireland is not an option. They are eager to escape its anachronistic social norms—many of which are a result of the Catholic Church’s power in the country. “The outward show of Irishness, of heritage … she was getting as far away from that as possible,” Kennedy writes of Roisin. When Roisin tries to get ahold of Plan B in 1985, she tells her father’s British girlfriend that “it’s hard to get contraception in Ireland. Until four months ago, you could only get condoms on prescription.” At the time, there was also “no divorce in the Irish Republic.” (Ireland legalized divorce in 1996.)

Stations is hardly the first book to explore how the Catholic Church cast a shadow of shame and secrecy over much of Irish society. Claire Keegan’s acclaimed 2021 novella Small Things Like These, which details a cover-up of abuse at a convent, channeled many of the same themes.

Kennedy’s protagonists are hardly pious, but they are no less troubled by religious dogma than Keegan’s. Even at the end of Stations, in 2007—by which point Ireland had transformed dramatically—Roisin notes that most Irish drinking songs are still about the same old themes: “love and loss and loneliness.”—Allison Meakem

Keletso Mopai (Seven Stories Press, 224 pp., $18.95, November 2026)

Since the apartheid era, one issue has remained at the center of political debates in South Africa: land. Specifically, who owns it, who is owed it, and who can lay claim to it. Until the 1990s, Black South Africans could only own land in 13 percent of the country; that decade, Nelson Mandela himself said that “with freedom and democracy, came restoration of the right to land. And with it the opportunity to address the effects of centuries of dispossession and denial.” Yet 30 years later, the country’s white minority still hold more 72 percent of its private farmland.

That dispossession, and the struggle to redress it, is at the heart of Keletso Mopai’s ambitious debut novel, We Belong to the Trees. Set in the northeastern town of Tzaneen in the mid-1990s, the book chronicles the mystery surrounding the murder of a young Black security guard at a farm owned by an Afrikaner family. What happened is revealed in overlapping layers, as a chorus of characters, from the victim’s brother to the farmer’s wife to an unnamed bystander, narrate their experiences of that night and the circumstances surrounding it. The story unfolds meticulously, if a touch too neatly, with new revelations in nearly every chapter. Soon, we learn of the victim’s family’s claims to the land, the brewing discontent among the Black farmworkers, and extramarital affairs that laid the groundwork for the tragedy.

Mopai has said that the novel was partly inspired by her father telling her as a young girl about her own family’s claim to a white-owned farmhouse and his efforts to take it back. It was not until later that she realized the political context of their dispossession. In the book, the Afrikaner farmer increasingly feels pressure to leave his land, bemoaning the “bloody shits [who] are on my neck with the land politics.” “My grandfather owned this farm,” he adds. “You know, just because Nelson Mandela is now president they think they own everything in this country.”

Paranoia and disorientation pervade the novel as the characters try to make sense of their place in a society that remains deeply unequal even as it’s undergoing profound change. Those racial disparities, of course, still mark the country, and in an era in which the Trump administration and Afrikaner lobby groups peddle false stories about a “white genocide” in South Africa, Mopai’s novel brings nuance and humanity to what remains one of the most fraught political struggles of our time.—Chloe Hadavas

A widower undertakes a literary experiment in Spanish author Enrique Vila-Matas’ Camera Obscura, translated by Valerie Miles. Sian Hughes draws inspiration from a 14th-century poem in No Such Thing as Monday, a darkly comic novel set in contemporary England. Veronica Gonzalez Peña traverses North America in her novel of disorientation, Secret Daughter. In Avni Doshi’s The First House, a divorcée sifts through the ruins of family life. Rachid Benzine’s The Man Who Read Books, translated by Sam Taylor, unfolds as a conversation between a bookseller and foreign correspondent in Palestine.

Nigerian novelist Chinelo Okparanta’s This Impossible Life chronicles two siblings across decades and continents caught in the crosshairs of global forces. With her debut novel, The Waiter, Malaysian author Kwan Ann Tan offers up an “interactive story” of a futuristic London. Scholastique Mukasonga reimagines stories from Rwandan folklore in Murmurs from the Hills, translated from the French by Mark Polizzotti. Ukrainian writer Yuri Andrukhovych’s pandemic-era thriller, Radio Night, is translated into English by Mark Andryczyk. And a Metis man communes with a melting glacier in Michelle Porter’s A Glacier’s Guide to Dying.—CH

Books are independently selected by FP editors. FP earns an affiliate commission on anything purchased through links to Amazon.com on this page.

#6042. The Dangers of a Lame-Duck Trump

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANIEL W. DREZNER is Academic Dean and Distinguished Professor of International Politics at the Fletcher School of Law and Diplomacy at Tufts University and the author of the newsletter Drezner’s World.

ELIZABETH N. SAUNDERS is Professor of Political Science at Columbia University, a Nonresident Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution, and the author of The Insiders’ Game: How Elites Make War and Peace.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

With the midterms over, U.S. President Donald Trump is now a lame duck. Second-term presidents who lose control of Congress have to face the reality that the time for signature legislation or other domestic policy initiatives has passed. As they think about their legacy, lame ducks often turn to foreign policy, where the power of the presidency allows them to act unfettered by pesky domestic politics. It is not unusual for a lame duck to reach for a final, lofty goal abroad. U.S. President Bill Clinton, for instance, put great effort into trying to advance a proposal for a final settlement to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict at the end of his presidency.

Trump’s lame-duck period will not be like those of his predecessors, however. He has already gone through what was widely considered at the time to be a lame-duck phase, between November 2020 and January 2021. He used it to contest the election outcome despite clear evidence that Joe Biden had won. Trump is the first president in the past 100 years to have served two nonconsecutive terms, and therefore the only American leader in living memory to have a second lame-duck period—this time lasting more than two years.

With Democrats soon to be in control of the House and the Senate, the threat of impeachment or investigations into the Trump family’s business dealings will likely make the president feel cornered and angry. And that’s what makes the remainder of Trump’s term so dangerous. A boxed-in Trump, with no obvious constructive foreign policy outlet to channel his energy, may well lash out internationally. And Congress, even if controlled by Democrats, will have little power over his day-to-day management of foreign policy.

There are plenty of ways Trump could act out: issuing fresh threats against Canada, launching new attacks on U.S. alliances, or colluding with Russia or China at the expense of Ukraine or Taiwan. But to turn those threats into policy would require serious effort, such as repositioning U.S. troops or painstakingly negotiating with other great powers.

The two places where Trump can inflict the most damage are Iran and Cuba. According to The New York Times, the Trump administration and Israel have drawn up plans for fresh strikes on Iran as soon as the midterms are over. A last-ditch effort to compel Tehran to surrender is unlikely to work and would put at risk the global economy and the security and stability of Persian Gulf allies. Trump might also try to force regime change in Cuba. Military action against both adversaries would only require Trump to give his say-so. With no election on the horizon, all that constrains the president abroad is the sense of his own legacy.

We define presidents as lame-duck when they no longer need to contend with elections, either presidential or midterm. For a one-term president who has lost a reelection bid, this period lasts just a few months, between their successor’s election and inauguration. For a second-term president, it can last two years, between the midterms and when they leave office. (Trump, who has never been particularly interested in the political fortunes of other Republicans, is unlikely to let the 2028 presidential election affect his thinking.)

Freed from the burdens of campaigning and legislating, and often lacking domestic political capital, lame-duck leaders tend to pivot to diplomacy, taking chances abroad that they would not have dared to before. Clinton tried to broker an Israeli-Palestinian peace agreement and arrange a presidential trip to North Korea at the end of his second term. After losing the 2014 midterms, President Barack Obama concluded the Iran nuclear deal and normalized relations with Cuba.

Lame-duck presidents have also used military force in ways they might not have done in the lead-up to elections. George H. W. Bush, for example, intervened in Somalia in late 1992 with a limited mission to ease the famine. More than a decade later, in 2007, George W. Bush sent a surge of forces to stabilize Iraq after a punishing midterm election in which voters seemed to send the message that he should withdraw from the failing war there.

The results of these foreign policy moonshots have been mixed. Clinton’s Israeli-Palestinian negotiations failed; Obama’s diplomatic wins were short-lived; George H. W. Bush’s Somalia mission was largely successful in staving off hunger but left the roots of the conflict unresolved, tempting Clinton to expand the mission with disastrous consequences; and George W. Bush’s Iraq surge quelled violence in the short term but did little to permanently stabilize the country. These endeavors were, at the very least, debated and planned with input from relevant parts of government and outside experts. Their aims were clear, even if unmet. And in each case the president attempted to explain and justify his policies—standards Trump has routinely failed to meet.

With Trump now diminished by the midterms yet still on a quest to win a Nobel Peace Prize, one might expect him to turn to diplomacy. The problem is that he has already exhausted many of the obvious lame-duck gambits. During his first term, Trump swung for the foreign policy fences by pulling out of the 2015 Iran nuclear deal, meeting North Korean leader Kim Jong Un three times, replacing the North American Free Trade Agreement with his own regional trade pact, facilitating the normalization of ties between Israel and a handful of Arab countries, and withdrawing from a raft of international agreements. Since returning to office in 2025, he has presided over a cease-fire in Gaza and the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Trump is not expected to return to most of these initiatives because he has reached the limits of what his spectacle-driven diplomacy can achieve. Trump vowed to take Greenland, for example, but has settled for a deal that expands the U.S. military presence in the territory. His “Board of Peace,” which he pitched as a rival to the United Nations Security Council, will continue to languish as other countries now feel less need to kowtow to his every whim.

There are other long shots Trump could attempt, such as trying to end intractable conflicts in Kashmir or Sudan, to forge a reputation as a peacemaker. But these are exceptionally difficult problems to solve in the best of circumstances. Even forcing a lopsided deal in these cases would require a level of deftness, regional expertise, and patience that Trump lacks. He rarely invests meaningful time in diplomacy, as illustrated by his dubious claims to have ended a slew of conflicts, many of which are still ongoing.

Perhaps the biggest swing Trump could take would be to fashion a great-power concert—a forum in which he, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese leader Xi Jinping worked together to impose global order. As with most of his prior meetings with Putin and Xi, however, future summits are unlikely to produce anything of substance. Neither Russia nor China has expressed much interest in the trilateral nuclear arms treaty that Trump has floated. Nor are they keen on pressing Iran to acquiesce to a deal with the United States.

Trump could use those meetings to throw Ukraine or Taiwan under the bus in the desperate hope of a peace deal. But as the past two years have demonstrated, Putin is not interested in compromise and has the resources to keep fighting. Xi views Taiwan as a domestic Chinese matter and will be unlikely to accept any constraints on his approach to the island. Trump will eventually figure out that he’s better off capitalizing on the optics of a summit of the three leaders than putting in the work to reach any kind of agreement.

Trump is most likely to take action in Iran. He seems to believe that escalating the war could break the stalemate and rescue his legacy. But Iranian leaders won’t back down in the face of more force. Instead, they will probably ramp up attacks on commercial shipping, strike Gulf allies, and ignore diplomatic overtures until Trump leaves office. Moreover, Trump’s own options may be limited: a more radical U.S. escalation against Iran, such as deploying troops to Kharg Island, would probably be so unpopular within his base that he would avoid it. Even Trump’s biggest supporters are growing tired of the high prices that his war has caused. Renewed conflict could plunge the world into economic crisis.

Trump could also attempt to force regime change in Cuba. He has imposed a full-blown embargo on the island for most of 2026 in an effort to weaken its defenses. Cut off from its Venezuelan lifeline and without electricity for all but a few hours a day, the economic hardships and human suffering in Cuba have dramatically worsened in recent months. Ousting Cuba’s government is a personal priority of Marco Rubio, Trump’s secretary of state and national security adviser, and would align with the “Donroe Doctrine,” an assertion of U.S. primacy in the Western Hemisphere.

For Trump, an attack on Cuba would be attractive because it would not require too much in the way of military assets and, at least in his mind, would solve a problem that has bedeviled U.S. presidents stretching back to the end of the Eisenhower administration. Trump might have already moved on Cuba had he not gotten bogged down in Iran. Of course, as the political scientist Alexander Downes has shown, using force to achieve regime change rarely works and often leads to unpredictable consequences—in the case of Cuba, it might mean a new influx of refugees to the United States.

But a lame-duck Trump could see it as a gamble worth taking on the off chance that he can be the president to finally unseat the communist regime. When it comes to foreign policy, Trump loves nothing more than attempting to hack open a Gordian knot. He might also view regime change in Cuba as a way to distract from his failure in Iran.

Another possibility is that Trump will focus his policy efforts abroad on enriching his family. He has already created ample opportunities for foreign entities to seemingly buy influence in his administration. Trump and his family members have accepted a jetliner from the emir of Qatar, a $2 billion investment from a fund led by the Saudi crown prince, and profits from a number of cryptocurrency enterprises, much of which came from foreign buyers. According to The New Yorker, Trump’s family has reaped $4 billion through such profiteering. Trump, secure in the knowledge that the Senate will not have the 67 votes it needs to remove him from office, might figure that he has nothing to lose in pursuing more moneymaking schemes.

The most surprising foreign policy option Trump could take during his lame-duck period, however, is doing nothing at all. The president has busied himself with remaking Washington, D.C., in his own image by redecorating the Oval Office, designing a White House ballroom, and attempting to remodel the Kennedy Center. There is a chance that he could spend his last two years in office issuing pardons, trying to prosecute political enemies on the side, and erecting monuments to himself. Given the alternatives, inaction on foreign policy may well be the best-case scenario for Trump’s lame-duck years.

American Foreign Policy in an Age of Unrestrained Executive Power

The Unconstrained Presidency and the End of American Primacy

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

The Arab Monarchies Must Help Washington and Tehran Save Face

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

#6041. ‘Wild Horse Nine’ Looks Away From Chile’s History

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: ‘Wild Horse Nine’ Looks Away From Chile’s History

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Wild Horse Nine, the latest feature from Oscar-winning British Irish director Martin McDonagh, opens in Santiago, Chile. It’s 1973, shortly before the U.S.-backed military coup that would oust socialist President Salvador Allende. The CIA, which had spent years watering the seeds of discontent with Allende that would blossom into betrayal, is cautiously optimistic about the operation’s outcome.

Everything has been set into motion—the generals paid, the soldiers armed, the soccer stadiums prepared to host thousands of prisoners—and so the only thing left for the two protagonists, CIA agents Chris (John Malkovich) and Lee (Sam Rockwell), is to take a well-earned vacation until the whole thing has blown over. For that, they are off to Easter Island, where Chris wants to “see the big heads.”

Wild Horse Nine, the latest feature from Oscar-winning British Irish director Martin McDonagh, opens in Santiago, Chile. It’s 1973, shortly before the U.S.-backed military coup that would oust socialist President Salvador Allende. The CIA, which had spent years watering the seeds of discontent with Allende that would blossom into betrayal, is cautiously optimistic about the operation’s outcome.

Everything has been set into motion—the generals paid, the soldiers armed, the soccer stadiums prepared to host thousands of prisoners—and so the only thing left for the two protagonists, CIA agents Chris (John Malkovich) and Lee (Sam Rockwell), is to take a well-earned vacation until the whole thing has blown over. For that, they are off to Easter Island, where Chris wants to “see the big heads.”

Chris and Lee are agency veterans—with Chris, at least, having been involved in some of the century’s most high-profile coups. “Guatemala, successful. Congo, successful. Bay of Pigs, less successful,” he rattles off. The Chilean plot is just another day at the office, and jokes are frequently made in this vein. Lee is so cold-blooded that he can kill a man while still eating a sandwich; Chris has killed people in countries Lee “can’t even spell.” As sparring buddies and partners, Rockwell and Malkovich are a potent duo with undeniable chemistry. Their characters have seen terrible things but still have a sense of humor, even if that humor has become darker from lives spent in the shadows.

Chris, however, is dying of cancer and has begun to feel pangs of guilt over his involvement in some of these covert operations. On Easter Island, he has nothing but time to wrestle with his regrets. He wonders whether horses, moths, or even Nazis make it to heaven. Lee assures him that he will get in, since he’s at least better than a Nazi, but neither man sounds convinced. As the pair visit the moai (those big heads), the audience is treated to long shots of rolling hills, steep cliffs, the deep cerulean of the Pacific, and herds of galloping wild horses. The island is small and remote. As a location for spiritual rumination, you couldn’t ask for a better purgatory.

Rockwell and Malkovich in a still from Wild Horse Nine.Searchlight Pictures

Wild Horse Nine is replete with callbacks to McDonagh’s 2008 film, In Bruges, which also starred two mismatched killers philosophizing about sin. These references are undoubtedly a bit of fan service, and they underscore the new film’s ultimate weakness. In Bruges hinged on a smaller-scale crime, the killing of a child, which forced its protagonists to grapple with the immediate, tragic consequences of their actions in a way that is largely absent from a film about toppling a far-off democratically elected government. Wild Horse Nine spends scant time in mainland Chile, which further removes viewers—and our protagonists—from the extraordinary violence and the swift, ruthless consolidation of power under Gen. Augusto Pinochet. Wild Horse Nine mostly speaks in broad language: marching soldiers, menacing black cars, the skin-tingle of surveillance.

The film’s distance from and looseness with its historical context affords its protagonists more leeway to discuss abstract questions of regret, redemption, and human connection—matters that benefit from being set at a remove from everyday life. Yet in doing so, McDonagh squanders the opportunity to consider what made the 1973 coup—and the U.S. government’s involvement in it—so tragic. Though not as hands-on by the time of Allende’s downfall, the United States was no less instrumental in destabilizing his administration. From earlier efforts in the 1960s up to the day of the takeover itself, successive U.S. presidents funded propaganda campaigns to prevent his election, attempted to foment a military coup to block his ascent, and then spent years impeding Allende’s ability to govern.

History buffs may know that U.S. National Security Advisor Henry Kissinger played a central role in Allende’s downfall, yet he is never mentioned in the film—nor is anyone else who was involved. Among President Richard Nixon’s most vocal Chile hawks, Kissinger spoke about the country with a callousness that would have made Chris blush. “I don’t see why we need to stand by and watch a country go communist due to the irresponsibility of its own people,” he said in June 1970, as he approved hundreds of thousands of dollars in funding to prevent Allende’s election. After Allende won, the CIA attempted to block him from taking office, leading to the assassination of Chilean Gen. René Schneider in a botched kidnapping attempt.

The effort to block Allende’s rise in 1970 was not the CIA’s first operation against him. Covert action began in 1962, with funding for anti-Allende operations totaling some $3 million by the time of Chile’s 1964 presidential election. After the CIA’s failed 1970 mission, the agency diligently spent three years funding right-wing agitators, such as the El Mercurio newspaper, while outwardly maintaining a “very cool and very correct” posture toward the Allende administration, as Nixon instructed his aides in November 1970.

Concurrently, the United States enacted a sweeping “invisible” blockade against Chile that severely repressed trade and financial aid to the country (the Chilean military excluded), hamstringing Allende’s ability to enact his economic agenda. This was all straightforwardly anti-democratic, which Nixon officials acknowledged. But it was also understood that a successful Allende administration would be a bad look for Washington, politically speaking, and so should be prevented at all costs.

Kim Philby, parachute drops, and what really happened in communist Albania.

A CIA veteran depicts Moscow’s espionage arsenal. What is the West going to do about it?

How the agency’s program to circulate banned books helped take down the Iron Curtain.

For a film styling itself as a black comedy, the CIA’s handling of its operations in Chile is a rich vein. So why exclude, well, all of it? With few onscreen details of the coup at hand, viewers are left to trust Chris, whose paranoid ramblings and memory lapses suggest he is either succumbing to the gravity of his past transgressions or suffering from dementia.

Chris lectures the leftist college students he has befriended, Monica (Mariana di Girolamo) and Alicia (Ailín Salas), on the impotence of street protest, and later monologues to Monica about what to do if she wants to stop the ascent of fascists and dictators. The latter scene is powerful, and di Girolamo delivers an incredible performance as an idealist who must reckon with the ugliness of the world. Monica grounds Chris: “She’s life, and I’m leaving it,” he says to Lee. Drawn to her youth and hope, Chris is reluctant to disabuse her of her optimistic beliefs but does so anyway, perhaps to prepare her for what’s ahead.

At one point, Chris says he wishes he had spent more time reading about the history of the places where he’s worked before arriving, a nod to CIA agents’ documented misunderstanding of the environments, cultures, and societies they upended. This could have built into a deeper, sustained introspection about Chris’s role in inflicting immense violence at the behest of the U.S. government. But a stronger current pulls against this meditation: the assassination of President John F. Kennedy.

Steve Buscemi in a still from Wild Horse Nine.Searchlight Pictures

Almost immediately after Kennedy’s death in November 1963, conspiracies around his assassination mushroomed. The U.S. government, and the CIA in particular, did not help matters: The agency obstructed investigations and withheld information about the event and its aftermath, fueling the spread of such theories to this day.

It is suggested early and often in Wild Horse Nine that Chris played a crucial role in the assassination, though he is reluctant to talk about it at length. It is also one of the only missions he seems to feel no remorse over—perhaps because Kennedy, like Chris, played a central role in perpetuating the foreign interventionism and violence that Chris has come to regret.

Who killed Kennedy, and how, ultimately becomes a bigger narrative question in the film than anything to do with Allende or Chile—and that’s a shame. There is an effort at moralizing here, and Chris seems to realize his life wasn’t best spent toppling fragile democracies. But centering the Kennedy conspiracy ultimately dilutes the film’s moral clarity. Instead of reckoning with the CIA’s involvement in Chile and elsewhere and the mass suffering that resulted, the audience is served a superficial story about the CIA as a tentacle of the deep state. Chris cannot shake the Kennedy plot, and neither can the film disentangle itself long enough to let us sit with the seriousness of his actions.

As with In Bruges, McDonagh is clearly fixated on a Catholic kind of guilt, one from a conscience suddenly alight with remorse as death approaches. But where that film kept a narrow moral focus and interrogated that guilt rigorously, Wild Horse Nine spreads itself too thin trying to take on institutional ethical failure. Like our protagonists waiting on Easter Island for the coup to play out in Santiago, we as viewers spend much of the film in a bucolic liminal space waiting—for judgment, for an opportunity to absolve ourselves. Of what we need to be cleansed, though, is something McDonagh doesn’t cleanly answer.

History has an answer, and a well-documented one. The film keeps turning away from it, toward a conspiracy that is far easier to accept. I enjoyed the twists and turns, and Rockwell and Malkovich make the wait a pleasure, but I couldn’t help feeling that the film could have viewed this episode in history with clearer eyes, less clouded by the fantasies of conspiracists, no matter how satisfying and easy their answers may be.

#6039. Democrats set sights on Elon Musk probe after midterm wins, but Pritzker draws a surprising line

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., and Democratic Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker said the American people deserve an investigation into the Department of Government Efficiency while leaving The Center for American Progress. (Credit: Nicholas Ballasy for Fox News Digital)

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., and Democratic Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker are calling for an investigation into the Department of Government Efficiency (DOGE), previously led by Elon Musk, as talks have heated up among Democrats to launch a probe into the multibillionaire.

"I had no specific plan," Raskin told Fox News Digital about the potential investigations. "Look … DOGE was a catastrophe for America, and at least one committee, I feel pretty certain, will investigate it. It needs to investigate it."

Musk has emerged as a potential target of Democratic oversight, with some lawmakers calling for scrutiny of his time as head of the DOGE.

The calls for an investigation come as Democrats have started rallying the party to look into alleged corruption under the Trump administration after coming out victorious in midterms.

SCHUMER, JEFFRIES SIDESTEP QUESTIONS ON NUKING FILIBUSTER, LAUNCHING ELON MUSK PROBE AFTER BLUE WAVE

Democrats are calling for an investigation into the Department of Government Efficiency, previously led by Elon Musk. (Getty Images, File)

The tech CEO helped launch DOGE at the start of President Donald Trump's second term to root out waste and be more transparent about the federal government's spending.

DOGE's efforts included deferred resignations and buyouts of employees at federal agencies and contributed to a significant downsizing of the federal workforce, which was reduced by about 250,000 employees during 2025, according to the Government Accountability Office (GAO).

Calls for investigations into DOGE also started over concerns about the department allegedly having access to Americans’ sensitive information.

"I mean, the whole thing was premised on a lie, and our private information was completely compromised by it," Raskin said.

Despite Pritzker saying he had never "heard anything like that" about a potential probe of Musk and DOGE, he agreed that DOGE is worthy of an investigation.

PROGRESSIVE GROUP'S 182-PAGE BLUEPRINT MAPS OUT POTENTIAL DEM INVESTIGATIONS INTO TRUMP, CORPORATE ALLIES

Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker agrees that DOGE is worthy of an investigation. (Terrence Antonio James/Chicago Tribune/Tribune News Service via Getty Images, File)

"I think there should be a lot of inspection of what DOGE did," Pritzker told Fox News Digital. "I think that was pretty awful."

But Pritzker didn’t seem so sure that needing an investigation into DOGE also warranted an investigation into Musk himself.

"I don't know about focusing on one person," Pritzker said when asked about investigating Musk.

ELON MUSK BLAMES TRUMP, US MILITARY FOR SPACEX RESTRICTIONS UKRAINE WARNS COULD HAVE DEADLY CONSEQUENCES

Democratic lawmakers have also suggested looking into Musk’s companies' multibillion-dollar contracts with the federal government and, more recently, his role in cutting internet access to the Ukrainian military.

After the midterm elections resulted in a sweeping blue wave, Democrats in both chambers of Congress have vowed to use investigative committees and subpoena powers to probe not only Musk, but Trump and much of his administration, including trying to impeach Trump.

Fox News Digital’s Eric Revell contributed to this report.

#6038. 4 of nation's largest cities now led by socialist mayors after Tuesday's election, affecting tens of millions

'The Big Money Show' panel analyzes the market rally after Democrats took control of Congress. The panel says the key election issues were based on affordability and immigration.

Two socialist mayors were elected in the United States on Tuesday in Los Angeles and Washington, D.C. which means four of the top 20 largest cities in the country have socialist leaders.

Los Angeles Councilwoman Nithya Raman, a member of the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA) defeated incumbent Mayor Karen Bass and will take office in December as mayor for the roughly 4 million residents of Los Angeles proper.

Also victorious on Tuesday was DSA member Janeese Lewis George, who coasted to victory to become mayor-elect of Washington, D.C., a city of roughly 700,000 residents.

The list of socialist mayors in the top 20 most populous U.S. cities stands at four, after George and Raman joined Seattle Mayor Katie Wilson and New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani.

DEMOCRATIC SOCIALIST SWEEPS INTO DC MAYOR'S OFFICE, SIGNALS OPENNESS TO WORKING WITH TRUMP

Former Biden White House aide Yemisi Egbewole predicted the Democratic Socialists of America would struggle to gain national influence ahead of the 2028 presidential election despite recent electoral victories. (2019 Getty Images)

In total, the four cities are home to roughly 14 million people. That figure does not include neighboring suburbs, which could still feel the effects of policies enacted within city limits through crime, real estate values and the broader business climate.

Problems in a city can spill over into surrounding communities, while suburban residents who work in the city may also be directly affected.

Counting the population in the metro areas of those four cities, roughly 44 million people could be affected either positively or negatively by the socialist policies enacted by the four mayors.

DSA LEADER DISMISSES $49T COST ESTIMATE FOR SOCIALIST POLICIES AS A 'MADE-UP NUMBER'

Los Angeles Councilwoman Nithya Raman drew online mockery after touting a recent 'win' in her role representing her constituents. (Getty Images)

"This makes LA the fourth large city that will now have a socialist mayor, joining New York; Washington, D.C.; and Seattle," Heritage Foundation fellow Mike Gonzalez posted on X Thursday.

"Chicago's mayor is a buffoon, but he doesn't identify as DSA. America used to be an impregnable fortress against Marxism, then Marxists took over universities."

The socialist victories were not limited to city halls. Voters also elected seven DSA-backed or affiliated candidates to the House: Claire Valdez, Brand Lander and Darializa Avila Chevalier of New York; Chris Rabb of Pennsylvania; Melat Kiros of Colorado; Donavan McKinney of Michigan; and Angela Gonzales-Torres of California.

Several other candidates associated with socialist policies also entered Congress or were re-elected Tuesday.

Family members and supporters of D.C. Ward 4 Council member Janeese Lewis George, a candidate for D.C. mayor, cheer as she addresses them at Prince Hall Masonic Temple, a polling location, in Washington, D.C., June 8, 2026. (The Washington Post via Getty Images)

A recent Fox News national poll indicated 32% of voters have a favorable view of socialism, with 64% seeing it in an unfavorable way. But the favorable rating matches an all-time high, first reached five years ago, since Fox News first began asking the question in 2019.

And a September Gallup poll found that 43% of Americans had a positive image of socialism, the highest level since the polling organization began tracking the question in 2010.

The Fox News poll found that 51% of voters had a favorable view of capitalism, compared with 32% who viewed socialism favorably. Another 46% had an unfavorable view of capitalism.
