#6037. ‘Call of Duty’ Has a Message for Hegseth

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: ‘Call of Duty’ Has a Message for Hegseth

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

When Call of Duty 4: Modern Warfare debuted in 2007, it was a revelation. Whereas earlier games in the series followed the familiar pattern of World War II stories, Modern Warfare recreated 21st century combat in groundbreaking graphic fidelity.

Players undertook nighttime counterterrorism raids, maneuvered tanks through urban environments that bore striking resemblance to Baghdad, and controlled drones through infrared displays on missions almost indistinguishable from real footage of U.S. bombing runs. Call of Duty has since become one of the most lucrative video game series in history, offering players all of the excitement of the war on terror with none of its controversy.

When Call of Duty 4: Modern Warfare debuted in 2007, it was a revelation. Whereas earlier games in the series followed the familiar pattern of World War II stories, Modern Warfare recreated 21st century combat in groundbreaking graphic fidelity.

Players undertook nighttime counterterrorism raids, maneuvered tanks through urban environments that bore striking resemblance to Baghdad, and controlled drones through infrared displays on missions almost indistinguishable from real footage of U.S. bombing runs. Call of Duty has since become one of the most lucrative video game series in history, offering players all of the excitement of the war on terror with none of its controversy.

Workers rush to fill orders of the highly anticipated Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 2 in a GameStop store in Las Vegas on Nov. 10, 2009.Ethan Miller/Getty Images

But in Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4, released in late October to critical acclaim, the player’s first mission is to buy snacks from a Korean convenience store. It’s the calm before the storm, as North Korea, led by the murderous sister of its leader, is preparing an invasion of the South. Players move through the world primarily as a South Korean soldier facing combat for the first time, working as part of a mixed-nationality squad. Before the onset of combat, the characters laugh and gripe. “Korean chicks don’t like a man in uniform?” a U.S. soldier asks after halfheartedly catcalling a woman. “Service is mandatory for us,” a Korean sergeant replies. “You’re not special to them.” It’s a remarkably humanizing opening, and a disarming turn for a video game series that has long rested on the fantasy of being an elite soldier.

The series has historically avoided East Asia, in part to avoid angering Beijing and endangering sales in China. The decision to set the game there now is undoubtedly a reflection of South Korea’s growing cultural clout. But it also suggests that its old formula is no longer working. As the U.S. public recoils from militarism and its military leaders’ fixation on lethality, Call of Duty has crossed the Pacific to find more relatable protagonists and more defensible purposes.

U.S. Navy SEALS await a night mission to capture Iraqi insurgent leaders near Fallujah, Iraq, on July 27, 2007.John Moore/Getty Images

Call of Duty’s political and strategic logic has always been flimsy. But the magic of the game was that it never sought to justify U.S. foreign policy or the heinous acts it asked soldiers to commit on its behalf. It stayed at ground level, with soldiers motivated by duty and loyalty to their comrades. “You draw the line wherever you need it,” one of the series’ heroes, Capt. John Price, says in the 2019 game. “We get dirty, and the world stays clean.”

Those soldiers have mostly belonged to special forces units, leaving ordinary troops behind. Call of Duty honed and elevated the mythological status of special forces that emerged during the war on terror. The games let operators be inherently good even when the politics of their missions and the methods they used were questionable.

The creators of “Is It Cake?” set out to answer the question of whether power corrupts.

In the film “NAZA,” Israeli soldiers reveal the workings of autonomous warfare.

“On the Psychology of Military Incompetence” examines the narrow minds of petty men.

In the 2010s, special forces operators had a cultural and political appeal that other soldiers did not. Special units such as the Navy SEALs, Green Berets, and Delta Force shouldered the operational burden of what was supposed to be a more adaptable approach to counterterrorism. If conventional troops were fallible, it was the elite operators undertaking high-risk, highly skilled operations who increasingly commanded public, commercial, and political respect. (This also helped launch the political careers of a disproportionate number of ex-Navy SEALs.) Even when its wars went poorly, the U.S. military could highlight its fearsome, capable special forces whose achievements seemed to vindicate the strategy that sent them abroad.

That impression of military strength—bolstered by the United States’ high-tech, long-range weaponry—has also helped allies like South Korea persuade their publics that hosting U.S. troops helps rather than hurts national security. Recent U.S. foreign policy, however, has put that argument in question.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth promised to retool the military around lethality, and the reactions of Trump administration officials after the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in January showed just how critical the talent of U.S. special forces was in justifying unilateral action. But the Iran war that followed has wreaked havoc on allies in the Persian Gulf, and special forces units have not played a decisive role in the conflict.

Families reunite with sailors from the U.S. Navy aircraft carrier USS Abraham Lincoln in San Diego after a historic 10-month deployment in the Middle East on Oct. 8.David Swanson/AFP via Getty Images

The military’s own condition has not helped. In recent months, the Pentagon has been run down by controversies over the poor conditions sailors faced aboard the USS Abraham Lincoln, Hegseth’s purge of many of the military’s most decorated officers, and the pull back of forward-deployed military assets. When U.S. President Donald Trump attempted to bully South Korea into sending naval vessels to the Strait of Hormuz, President Lee Jae-myung dithered for months, and only recently requested that a nearby anti-piracy naval mission expands its area of operations. Even countries that see the United States as their most important ally are wary of the risks of becoming entangled in its military operations, given Washington’s ongoing struggles to secure favorable outcomes in its conflicts.

The image of elite operators has fallen too. Eddie Gallagher, a Navy SEAL, was accused by his own compatriots of war crimes. Mathew Golsteyn, an Army Green Beret, was charged with murder until Trump pardoned him. In an explosive 2025 book, the journalist Seth Harp reported on the role of Delta Force operators who ran drug trafficking schemes in North Carolina, uncovering lurid tales of over-resourced special forces units who acted above the law. Even the Maduro raid’s success was dimmed by news that a special forces soldier with access to classified intelligence had placed an illegal bet on it.

These controversies have degraded the image of shadowy elite operators as zealously efficient professionals; they are instead associated with waste, degeneracy, and untrammeled violence in a political environment that celebrates militarism for its own sake.

A still from the video game Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4. Activision Blizzard

What Modern Warfare 4 has done is kill the special forces hero paradigm. It simmers with disillusionment over years of special forces deification, a myth Call of Duty itself did much to promote. Price, the grizzled British ex-special forces operator who flouts the rules, appears as a deuteragonist, but his actions are typically in vain. The protagonist, instead, is the far less decorated Private Park Hyun-Jun, a clean-shaven South Korean Marine who carries out missions with little chest-beating patriotism even as his squad assumes greater significance in the broader war. The game is clearly suspicious of antiheros who claim to do bad things for good purposes and is unambiguous about the moral clarity of regular soldiers in the U.S.-ROK squad.

For a game ostensibly about shooting lots of big guns, there sure is a lot of international law. Players can refuse murderous orders, and those who accidentally shoot a civilian must restart from a checkpoint. One of the key plot points involves a North Korean soldier who defects, disgusted by the atrocities committed by his leaders. It’s a stirring contrast to “No Russian,” one of the most infamous missions from the 2009 Call of Duty game, where the player participates in the massacre of civilians in an airport to progress in the campaign. (After a public outcry, the level was made skippable.)

Video games often allow players to entertain immoral and violent fantasies, but the newest Call of Duty swaps out a more typical fantasy of untrammeled action with a yearning for moral agency within the comfort of a structured, law-abiding system.

A still from the video game Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4. Activision Blizzard

While the game is set in Korea, it is not really a game about Korea. Its opening montage incorporates real footage of the 2022 Itaewon crowd crush; the U.S.-based developers seemingly failed to anticipate the outrage this would spark among Korean audiences. (The developers have since announced that the footage will be removed in an update.) Modern Warfare 4 has little to say about the culture or people it uses as a backdrop. Instead, the game says far more about the U.S.-South Korean relationship and the kinds of military figures it deems worthy of veneration.

“This operation represents the strength of our alliance,” a U.S. officer proclaims before an amphibious landing. “When we step off those ramps, we do so as one.” The characters in the game are unlikely heroes. The South Koreans have their gripes—one wonders aloud why there’s always an American in charge of the operation—and few soldiers in the joint squad look like hardened warriors prepared to take on the burdens of combat.

But the squad’s seamless unity through the campaign more than makes up for the fleshiness of its individual selves. It is a corporealization of what many U.S. allies wish the United States could be: a reliable partner, unquestionably committed to its friends’ security, whose capabilities are enhanced when it works with its allies. It’s a theme that harkens back to the series’ roots and the original games that mythologized the heroism of conventional World War II soldiers.

In order to save the world, perhaps we don’t want controversial, bloodied superheroes brimming with masculine vigor. Call of Duty seems to believe we might instead yearn for ordinary people who carry out lawful orders and live up to their commitments. A banal message, perhaps, but one that needs to be delivered in an age of global instability.

This post appeared in the FP Weekend newsletter, a weekly showcase of book reviews, deep dives, and features. Sign up here.

Syrus Solo Jin is the Elihu Rose Scholar in modern military history at New York University. His forthcoming book investigates how the U.S. military both expanded and discovered the limits of U.S. global power in Cold War Asia. Bluesky: @syrussolojin.bsky.social

#6035. The New Congress Can Repair Civil-Military Relations

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PETER D. FEAVER is Professor of Political Science and Public Policy at Duke University and the author of Thanks for Your Service: The Causes and Consequences of Public Confidence in the U.S. Military.

HEIDI URBEN is Professor of the Practice and Associate Director of Georgetown University’s Center for Security Studies in the Walsh School of Foreign Service.

How It Can Reclaim Its Constitutional Powers

Article I of the Constitution gives Congress sweeping powers to raise, maintain, and equip the armed forces. But the current Congress has done little to use these powers. The intense, frenetic activity of the past two years has undone established norms for military professionalism and may shape civil-military relations for a generation. Yet it has been led almost entirely by the executive branch with little meaningful involvement or pushback from Capitol Hill.

The next Congress will face great pressure to reverse course and in doing so could shore up the historical norms of American civil-military relations. The concern today is not a military that is defying civilians—the traditional bogeyman of American political leaders since the founding of the Republic. Rather, the fear is that the thousands of changes, large and small, that the executive branch has so rapidly imposed on the military may end up undermining the professional ethic on which the U.S. form of civilian control depends.

The incoming Congress can push U.S. civil-military relations in a healthier direction by overseeing the executive branch more actively than its predecessor. It can also help restore trust with both the military and the American public if it focuses on providing greater transparency, takes a more assertive role in conducting defense policy, reinforces the military’s nonpartisan professional ethic, and establishes a new bipartisan commission to reinvigorate the military profession. Congress has the power and authority to improve civil-military relations. The new session, starting in January, is its chance to use them before the damage becomes irreversible.

When Pete Hegseth was sworn in as defense secretary, in 2025, he had strong views on the changes he wanted to make, alleging the military had lost its warfighting focus because of misguided priorities and “wokeism.” He has pursued those changes with a zeal not seen since Robert McNamara held the office from 1961 to 1968. Hegseth has reshaped the senior officer ranks: he has fired or removed nearly two dozen senior general and flag officers, and he has gotten rid of more than one-quarter of all four-star generals, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and the chiefs of the U.S. Air Force, Army, and Navy. He has fired, removed, or pulled from promotion lists approximately 80 general and flag officers, a disproportionate number of whom are women or racial minorities. Hegseth has justified those removals in the vaguest of terms, which has convinced most observers that the real reason amounts to little more than because these officers followed the lawful orders of the previous administration. No previous defense secretary has removed such large swaths of the officer corps who honored their oaths to the Constitution and faithfully implemented policies decided by their civilian superiors.

Hegseth has moved quickly, largely ignoring traditional bureaucratic practices, including rigorous staff reviews and close involvement of congressional stakeholders in changing the trajectory of the Pentagon’s policymaking and in conducting military operations. Amid ongoing questions about the legality of the boat strikes the Trump administration ordered against suspected drug traffickers last fall as well as civilian casualties in America’s war with Iran, it is critical to note that Hegseth had already fired the senior, most experienced military legal advisers—sending a warning shot across the bow of younger judge advocates—and begun a significant overhaul of the Judge Advocate General Corps. Ever since the My Lai massacre during the Vietnam War, when U.S. soldiers committed war crimes by killing hundreds of unarmed Vietnamese civilians, the JAG Corps had been empowered to help U.S. personnel abide by the laws of armed conflict. By demoting senior legal advisers and selecting less qualified personnel in those positions, however, the military’s legal voice is more marginalized today than it has been since the Vietnam War. The Trump administration has also centralized decision-making, including by placing routine actions in highly classified channels that minimize opportunities for input and deliberation.

Hegseth has done all of this—and much more—with essentially no pushback from the uniformed military or Pentagon bureaucracy. On the one hand, this shows just how strongly officers accept the principle of civilian control—a posture reinforced precisely because Hegseth has proved willing to punish even unsupported allegations of military insubordination. On the other hand, his actions have had a chilling effect across the force, creating an environment in which the military is reluctant to offer candid advice or uphold its professional standards and values when they are at odds with administration priorities. Indeed, in a highly unusual move, former government officials have publicly called for military leaders to do more to stand up to the Trump administration, even at the cost of undermining civilian control. As we argued in Foreign Affairs in 2024, however, the military cannot save democracy; only Congress and the courts can and should check presidential power.

Hegseth has enjoyed the unwavering support of President Donald Trump. But the real secret to Hegseth’s success in imposing his agenda on the Pentagon has been the unwillingness of the current Congress to exercise its Article I responsibilities or defend its traditional prerogatives. The incoming Congress can take a different approach by using its power to build trust, transparency, and accountability.

Most pressing, Congress should exercise its ability to appropriately scrutinize defense policymaking, much of which has been shrouded in secrecy under the Trump administration. The current Congress has held very few oversight hearings, and the ones that have taken place have been exchanges of insults rather than of information. When secrecy is genuinely needed, of course, Congress can issue classified briefings and hold closed hearings. But open hearings, when properly conducted, are necessary in cases in which Congress is entitled to answers, for example, about military personnel policy.

In the case of the boat strikes in the Caribbean and the eastern Pacific, Congress needs to know what advice senior military commanders and their JAGs offered, and it must understand how JAG firings affect the process by which commanders discern the lawfulness of military operations. Thus far, the Defense Department has provided only superficial explanations of its actions that basically amount to “we did this because we thought it was necessary.” Such justifications fall far short of what the Constitution envisions as the best way to hold the armed forces accountable: open debate that subjects policies to rigorous scrutiny. Greater transparency would also bolster public confidence in defense policy and remove pressure on the military to have to act as a potential savior of the Constitution.

Congress should also faithfully fulfill its “advice and consent” function established in Article II, which authorizes the Senate to review and confirm presidential nominations and promotions. Hegseth’s unprecedented intervention in the meritocratic promotion process has dramatically altered the military’s upper echelons. Congress has normalized this interference, perhaps unwittingly, by approving Hegseth’s altered promotion lists with voice votes and little inquiry.

The president and the defense secretary must retain the right to remove officers in whom they have lost confidence. But Congress has the right to know why a change was made, and it needs to ensure that promotions and removals follow established law and procedures rather than ideological or partisan considerations. Because Congress created the process for meritocratic promotions, it can push the executive branch to adhere to that process. It should mandate that the defense secretary provide a written explanation, supported by documented evidence of misconduct or derogatory information, for any off-cycle removals of senior officers and restore senior officers’ promotions, which would limit unjustified removals and uphold the merit-based selection process. Improving transparency in personnel policy will have one further salutary effect: it will help retain those qualified military personnel who are wondering whether they should give up on a military career that could be derailed by politicized and capricious promotion decisions.

In addition, Congress has a responsibility to police the well-established principle and practice that the uniformed military must stay away from partisan politics. The military has largely tried to uphold this norm, even as civilian political leaders have sought to draw it into partisan debates. Each time, for instance, Hegseth attacks the Biden administration before military audiences, most of whom served during the Biden era, he unwittingly further degrades the nonpartisan ethic on which service members’ oaths of office—and thus their subordination to his own policies—depend. It sends a message that embracing partisan rhetoric is key to career advancement and signals to the public that the military is not a neutral constituency. Congress can police the executive branch’s politicization of the military through inquiries, hearings, and even measures that prohibit the use of appropriated funds for events that treat military audiences like campaign rallies. Members of Congress can also call out for censure uniformed military personnel who behave in a blatantly partisan fashion regardless of whether they support or oppose the party the member supports.

Most ambitiously, Congress should reclaim its role in making defense policy rather than only providing oversight. Legislative deference to the executive in times of war is not unique to Trump’s second term, but Congress should close loopholes that allow presidents to expand their power unchecked. If Congress wants to provide a legal justification to allow the use of force in Iran or against drug traffickers in the Caribbean or the eastern Pacific Ocean, it should do so. If it believes these actions are unlawful, however, it must use its legislative powers to constrain them. Similarly, Congress should amend the Insurrection Act to clarify and restrict the domestic use of military force. The president must retain the ability to respond to true national emergencies, but Congress should narrow the criteria for deployment of troops on U.S. soil to avoid potential abuse.

Even as it becomes more active, Congress must strenuously avoid involving the military in a partisan war. Members of Congress should not press the military to criticize the president or the defense secretary in the made-for-social-media-outrage hearings that have become commonplace in Washington. It is right and proper for Congress to ask members of the military to share their personal and professional opinions on key strategic questions. But too often, politicians try to turn officers into combatants in their partisan battles, hiding behind the armed forces to defend partisan agendas or attacking military leaders who have simply carried out the lawful orders of a president from another party. Above all, Congress should not ask the military to be a check on civilian power. Only other civilians have the authority to do that.

Congress cannot solve longer-term issues through the day-to-day legislative process. But it has other tools to more holistically improve civil-military relations and identify the most essential reforms. The time-honored strategy is to set up a nonpartisan blue-ribbon commission to analyze problems, draw attention to their importance, and recommend solutions. A national commission on the state of the professional military ethic, co-chaired by three respected individuals—one with experience in a Republican administration, one with experience in a Democratic administration, and one retired senior military leader—would have the freedom to investigate how recent developments, including attempts to politicize the military and sustain a high tempo of operations, have affected the force. At a minimum, the commission should explore impacts on recruiting and retention; professional military education, fellowships in civilian institutions, and general and flag officer development; the JAG Corps’ ability to provide commanders with candid, independent legal advice; and the state of the National Guard.

The current Congress has not shown enough interest in exercising its own powers. The next one can make a clean break and rediscover the body’s ability to carry out the functions that are necessary to improve civil-military relations. The new Congress can demonstrate its commitment to fulfilling its responsibilities by providing ordinary legislative oversight in the raising, maintaining, and equipping of the armed forces. And on thorny issues that require a bigger response, Congress can start the reform process by creating a national commission to lay out an agenda for action. Even after two years of enormous strain that has eroded best practices in civil-military relations, the next Congress is well positioned to reverse the slide and shore up Americans’ faith in a nonpartisan, professional military that is essential to democracy.

#6034. A German Movie Previews Its Far-Right Future

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: A German Movie Previews Its Far-Right Future

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Contemporary cinema from Germany seems determined to disabuse anyone—including Germans—who still imagines it as a nation of Birkenstock-wearing, anti-nuclear peaceniks who shrink from their own shadows. A spate of new, sharply edged films deftly take up where the socially critical New German Cinema of the 1970s—featuring directors such as Rainer Werner Fassbinder, Volker Schlöndorff, and Margarethe von Trotta—left off. This new Germany is something much darker than that portrayed in lighthearted films from recent decades like Fack ju Göhte and Good Bye, Lenin!, and the far right’s stunning results in September’s regional elections portend how much darker it may still become.

One of these unflinching filmmakers is the German Iranian director Faraz Shariat, whose Prosecution is a harrowing thriller about a young German lawyer with Korean roots—a lesbian named Seyo Kim (Chen Emilie Yan)—who starts as a state prosecutor in a small eastern German city. After winning three prizes at the Berlinale film festival in February, the arthouse thriller was released in Germany over the summer and was described by one reviewer as “a film of the political moment.”

Contemporary cinema from Germany seems determined to disabuse anyone—including Germans—who still imagines it as a nation of Birkenstock-wearing, anti-nuclear peaceniks who shrink from their own shadows. A spate of new, sharply edged films deftly take up where the socially critical New German Cinema of the 1970s—featuring directors such as Rainer Werner Fassbinder, Volker Schlöndorff, and Margarethe von Trotta—left off. This new Germany is something much darker than that portrayed in lighthearted films from recent decades like Fack ju Göhte and Good Bye, Lenin!, and the far right’s stunning results in September’s regional elections portend how much darker it may still become.

One of these unflinching filmmakers is the German Iranian director Faraz Shariat, whose Prosecution is a harrowing thriller about a young German lawyer with Korean roots—a lesbian named Seyo Kim (Chen Emilie Yan)—who starts as a state prosecutor in a small eastern German city. After winning three prizes at the Berlinale film festival in February, the arthouse thriller was released in Germany over the summer and was described by one reviewer as “a film of the political moment.”

It opens with a tense courtroom scene in which the freshly appointed Kim treats a racist attack on a Black citizen as a petty crime and not a politically motivated assault. She is the proud representative of Germany’s institutional justice, which she believes is “the most objective in the world.”

But her attitude changes quickly. After reprimanding a neo-Nazi defendant, she is deliberately knocked off her bike while cycling through a park by a hooded man beneath a bridge. From above, Molotov cocktails rain down, one of which explodes on her back, setting her jacket ablaze and burning her neck and jaw. The attackers flee yelling, “Take that, cunt!” Despite her injuries, Kim remains at the crime scene to ensure that forensic evidence is properly secured. She’s the victim now, and she’s determined to obtain justice in a system that isn’t objective, and perhaps never was.

Kim’s lawyer, Alexandra Tiedemann (played by Julia Jentsch), is the same lawyer who represented the Black citizen in the first scene, and raised the objection (then against Kim) that the German system is “blind in the right eye.” In other words, the establishment refuses to treat right-wing extremism with the same severity it does other politically motivated acts. Kim tells Tiedemann that, had Muslims been suspected of the attack on her, it would immediately have been labeled terrorism and all of the state’s resources brought to bear. This charge of official partiality has long been made by the German left—going all the way back to the 1950s when the West German justice system was stacked with former Nazis, the topic of Lars Kraume’s The People vs. Fritz Bauer (2015). But that was a long, long time ago, right?

Actor Burghart Klaussner as Fritz Bauer in The People vs. Fritz Bauer (2015).Martin Valentin Menke/zero one film GmbH

Racist hate crimes, critics like Shariat charge, are all too often either not prosecuted at all or treated as apolitical infractions. This spares perpetrators stiff sentences and hides Germany’s ugly secret: Eighty years after Nazi rule, a potent fascist network runs across the country, in all directions, not just in former East Germany. This leads to a sobering conclusion, namely that contemporary Germany has failed at its most elemental undertaking: ensuring that fascism never again flourishes on German soil.

Germany’s highest profile institutional failure (so far) were the murders committed by a neo-Nazi cell called the National Socialist Underground (NSU) between 2000 and 2007. The group murdered a German policewoman and nine people from primarily Turkish backgrounds. Despite evidence that piled up over those years, the NSU continued to kill, its murders constituting the deadliest extremist killing spree in postwar Germany at the time. Instead of homing in on the widespread neo-Nazi networks, the authorities targeted the victims’ families. As depicted in The Barber and the Bomb (a 2015 documentary by Andreas Maus), the German authorities falsely claimed the murders were tied to Turkish organized crime or gambling mafias. German authorities only identified the killers after they found a video made by the NSU claiming responsibility for the murders, bombings, and robberies.

A protester holds a sign with pictures of victims of the National Socialist Underground terrorist group, seen in front of the Higher Regional Court in Munich, Germany, on July 11, 2018.Matthias Balk/Getty Images

In Prosecution, Kim believes that the state’s institutions of justice can and will function, if worked the right way. (In the 2017 German film In the Fade, directed by Fatih Akin, the protagonist gives up on the justice system; she kills her family’s murderers and herself with a homemade bomb.) Nevertheless, Kim doesn’t count on the state to protect her. She arms herself and buys a jet-black, bulletproof sports car. As her legitimate quest for justice becomes an obsession, she crosses lines that make the film more than a black-and-white tale of good versus evil. Does she really have to break the law to make the law work? Do Germans with darker skin tones need to be armed on the street? Does Kim selfishly put others in harm’s way to convict her attackers?

As Kim immerses herself in the case—against the warnings of her superiors—she undertakes the investigative legwork that the city’s authorities themselves should do be doing but aren’t. Secure in her standing as a German professional, she refuses to act the way many non-German victims do: passive and defenseless, cowed by Germany’s intimidating bureaucracy. At night, Kim sneaks into the city’s archives to piece together evidence that reveals a dense far-right network that penetrates deep into the justice and public-security organs. She tracks down previous victims whose cases were swept under the rug—obvious leads not followed up, pertinent questions unasked, connections to other similar crimes unaddressed.

The obstacles that Kim encounters again and again point to sympathy for the far right beyond city hall’s custodial staff and a handful of police. Her superior, a senior prosecutor—an urbane, educated, reasonable-looking man—tells her that 80 percent of cases like hers go unsolved, and hers very well might too. He’s not a beer hall lowlife or neo-Nazi thug, and that’s exactly what’s so disturbing: The institutions of justice belie their neutral and orderly veneers. Germany’s far right wear ties, drive Porsches, possess law degrees. “Yeah, there are Germans around here who think like this,” a colleague tells her. “They can’t be excluded from public service. There’s no law.” The threat to people of color in Germany no longer lies exclusively outside the institutions but inside them as well.

Actress Diane Kruger as Katja Sekerci in the film In the Fade (2017).Magnolia Pictures

The film’s German title, Staatsschutz (literally “state protection”), does work for it that the English translation misses. In Germany, the federal and state-level Staatsschutz is the police organ responsible for defending the constitutional order, state institutions, and national security. This object of prosecution is unnamed, but democratic Germany protects its citizens from the likes of terrorism. But switch out the object and the state could be protecting another group: the perpetrators. What it protects above all else is itself: its own institutions, personnel, reputation. An image stained by far-right xenophobes is the last thing that Germany (of all countries) wants, so it’s not in the state’s interest to make federal cases out of racist and neo-Nazi crimes, neither in the former West or East.

Prosecution is all the more chilling today. It portrays conditions before the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) racked up 44 percent of the vote in the eastern German state of Saxony-Anhalt in September and then 38 percent in another eastern state two weeks later. These votes constitute seismic events in postwar Germany, but Prosecution reveals the extent to which the far right has crept into German institutions without ever being voted into them. Regularly and for years now, German media has reported on cases of far-right chat groups in police units, neo-Nazi cliques in the armed forces, and AfD-affiliated judges and prosecutors. And as we see in Prosecution, for people of color and the LGBTQ community, that’s scary enough. In 2025, right-wing extremists committed 42,544 crimes across Germany, according to interior ministry statistics, including 8,396 explicitly xenophobic hate crimes—and these are the numbers of a system blind in the right eye. Critics suspect they are much higher.

Read More A black-and-white film still of actors Paul Lukas (left), Robert Ryan, and Merle Oberon walking among the rubble of destroyed buildings in “Berlin Express” (1948). A black-and-white film still of actors Paul Lukas (left), Robert Ryan, and Merle Oberon walking among the rubble of destroyed buildings in “Berlin Express” (1948). Filming Among the Rubble of Nazi Germany How Hollywood captured the struggle to make a better world. Analysis | Phil Tinline

How Hollywood captured the struggle to make a better world.

How threatening will life in an AfD-led Saxony-Anhalt be for the far right’s enemies? One has to assume significantly so. And for this reason, many who fall on the far right’s undesirable list are sitting on packed bags, report German media. In an AfD-governed state, the well-being of people of color and the LGBTQ community will likely be distinctly worse than that of Kim in Prosecution. With the justice and interior ministers in its portfolio, an AfD government will control the Staatsschutz and the state intelligence agency, too—the latter of which currently classifies the AfD as “right-wing extremist.” So, too, will it control police personnel, and thus the department’s priorities and deployment, as well as asylum and deportation rules in the state. Ministers appoint political civil servants such as state secretaries and department heads. The justice ministry gives instructions to public prosecutors like Kim.

The AfD and its cronies already set the tone in bastions like Saxony-Anhalt, and have done so for years. But now the hatred bears a stamp of legitimacy, even if it violates the law. Under the wing of the AfD, perpetrators like those who fire-bombed Kim in Prosecution will surely feel emboldened—and protected.

In pondering the larger issue of how this can happen in Germany (a country long considered a pioneer in coming to terms with the past), one has to watch another contemporary German film, Fatherland, directed by Pawel Pawlikowski. The black-and-white biopic follows the great German writer Thomas Mann as he and his daughter, Erika, who have been living in the United States, tour eastern and western Germany in 1949, just as the country’s postwar division is solidifying. In Frankfurt, in the West, the state is stocked with former Nazis. In the East, the Soviet system, with all of its flaws, is assuming shape. There’s something profoundly wrong and disturbing at this birth moment of the two Germanys, and the Manns sense it. These birth defects resonate to today.

Paul Hockenos is a Berlin-based journalist. His recent book is Berlin Calling: A Story of Anarchy, Music, the Wall, and the Birth of the New Berlin.

#6032. American war hero details dire living conditions US troops face as Hegseth taps him to 'fix' it

Medal of Honor recipient Dakota Meyer details troops living with mold, no hot water and broken AC as he takes on Hegseth’s push to fix military housing.

Some troops have gone a year without hot water. Others are sleeping in their cars to escape barracks without air conditioning. And some are asking for little more than drywall, spackle and paint so they can make repairs themselves.

Those are among the conditions Medal of Honor recipient Dakota Meyer says he has encountered while touring military installations on a mission from War Secretary Pete Hegseth: Fix a barracks system plagued by decades of neglect.

"I would tell you that there's only a couple that I've seen that are good," Meyer told Fox News Digital in an interview Thursday.

DAKOTA MEYER TALKS SERVICE, BLASTS CONTROVERSIAL DESIGN FOR GLOBAL WAR ON TERROR MEMORIAL

Meyer, who serves as a senior advisor to the Pentagon's Barracks Task Force, has visited more than eight installations across the Army, Navy, Air Force and Marine Corps since taking on the assignment and plans to visit 30 before the end of the year.

"What I thought was gonna be a professional job very quickly turned into personal," Meyer said.

Extensive apparent mold and water damage cover walls and a closet inside military barracks inspected as part of the Pentagon's barracks overhaul. (Courtesy of Dakota Meyer)

Medal of Honor recipient Sgt. Dakota Meyer listens to remarks during a reenlistment ceremony at the Pentagon April 17, 2025, in Arlington, Va. (Alex Wong/Getty Images)

At one installation, Meyer recalled asking a 19-year-old service member what she would change about her living conditions if she could "wave a magic wand."

"If she could just have a hot shower," Meyer recalled. "It's absolutely heartbreaking."

The Pentagon's barracks problems long predate Hegseth and have survived repeated attempts at reform.

A sweeping 2023 Government Accountability Office investigation documented serious health and safety hazards and found that the department lacked reliable information about the condition of its own barracks, could not determine how much of its facilities spending went toward them and provided insufficient oversight.

Congress and the military services have since committed billions toward improvements, including initiatives ranging from the Marine Corps' Barracks 2030 to a Navy overhaul of unaccompanied housing. Yet serious problems persist. As of this year, 12 of GAO's 31 recommendations remained outstanding.

Hegseth has sought to centralize the effort under a department-wide task force and accelerate improvements, and Meyer said the secretary has made clear that identifying problems is no longer enough.

"The secretary has made it clear to me he doesn't expect me to observe this, study it or to make it better. He expects me to fix it," Meyer said. "And that's exactly what we're going to do."

A damaged ceiling hangs over a bed in military barracks visited as part of the Pentagon's effort to assess living conditions for service members. (Courtesy of Dakota Meyer)

Meyer, the only Medal of Honor hero to reenlist after receiving the award, said one of his first requests was for Hegseth to give the task force an "authoritative role," rather than simply create another layer of oversight.

The goal, he said, was to give the team the ability "to go out and make decisions, to be able to have impact, to be able to make change and to be able to direct where that funding goes and how fast it gets to our service members."

EXCLUSIVE: HEGSETH TO DOUBLE CUTS TO GENERALS, ADMIRALS IN MAJOR PENTAGON SHAKEUP

Meyer argued the problem is not simply a lack of money. Funding shortfalls, continuing resolutions and years of deferred maintenance have all contributed, he said, but so have layers of contracting rules and bureaucracy that can slow even relatively basic repairs.

A separate watchdog review found the Pentagon facing a $285 billion maintenance backlog across its broader military facilities' portfolio, underscoring the scale of the department's infrastructure challenges.

"When you look at the contracting aspect, it's easier for people to say no," Meyer said.

Funding is also part of the problem, according to Meyer, who pointed to Congress' failure to enact full-year defense appropriations for fiscal 2027. The Pentagon is operating under a continuing resolution through Dec. 11, which generally funds ongoing programs at prior-year rates while lawmakers negotiate a broader spending package.

"When you can't get a budget, when you're waiting on Congress to pass a budget, to be able to get the funding there, I mean, that is a huge issue," Meyer said.

A barracks room shows a sink without a basin and makeshift lighting installed around a bathroom ceiling during an inspection of service members' living conditions. (Courtesy of Dakota Meyer)

But he also argued that the Pentagon has too often measured progress by how much money has been obligated or put under contract, rather than whether troops actually see improvements in their living conditions.

"Our service members don't, they don't live in our budget lines," Meyer said. "They live in the rooms that those budget lines are there to impact."

For Meyer, that has meant getting out of Washington and hearing directly from the troops living with the consequences.

"You can't fix these problems or understand these problems by standing on the carpet of the Pentagon," he said. "You've got to go out there and talk to these service members."

What he has heard, Meyer said, is striking partly because of how little troops are asking for.

"They're asking for a hot shower. They're asking for a place to wash their clothes. They're asking for a door that locks. They're asking to be able to go to sleep in a place that doesn't have mold. I mean, they're asking for light bulbs."

"They're willing to be part of the solution," he added.

Meyer stopped short of placing the blame solely on installation commanders, saying many want to improve conditions but have been constrained by a system that has normalized poor barracks for years.

"I think this is a multidimensional problem, and it's complex," he said. "I think that you have commanders out there that want to take care of their service members. I've seen that across the board. But ... they still have to have the ability to do that."

Meyer was far less forgiving of previous Pentagon leadership, arguing that years of investments and reform initiatives had failed to translate into adequate living conditions for troops.

"Don't kid yourself," Meyer said. "In the past ... it wasn't a priority to the leadership."

A window air conditioning unit and apparent mold and water damage are seen during inspections of military facilities as the Pentagon works to improve service members' living conditions. (Courtesy of Dakota Meyer)

Previous administrations and military leaders did launch barracks improvement efforts and devoted billions of dollars to construction, renovation and maintenance, even as government watchdogs continued to document serious deficiencies.

Meyer said Hegseth has made the issue a personal priority.

"We have the secretary of war who is putting people first, and he's not just saying that. He's doing it through his actions," Meyer said.

Meyer has put his own name on whether the latest effort succeeds, saying previously that if the barracks aren't fixed, there is "only one person to blame" — himself.

Asked what "fixed" would actually look like, Meyer offered a simpler standard than any Pentagon metric.

"People are living in places that I would be OK for my daughters living in," he said.

#6031. China’s Economics of Denial

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Oliver Melton is a Director with Rhodium Group’s China practice.

Logan Wright is a Partner at Rhodium Group and leads its research on China’s economy and financial markets. He is the author of the book Broken China: How the Economic Miracle Shattered and What it Means for the World.

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

China has correctly diagnosed its most pressing economic problems. The Chinese Communist Party has acknowledged that its economy relies too much on investment and exports and that persistently weak household consumption is the core threat to future growth. The leadership also knows that this imbalanced growth model is causing trade friction with developed and emerging economies alike and will continue to do so. They know there is a growing chorus of economists (including many quiet voices in China) raising the alarm that, if Beijing does not make major changes soon, China will face ever-slower growth, cascading trade disputes, and potentially stagnating household income.

But what is puzzling is that the country’s leaders are not acting. Beijing has announced only modest plans to boost household consumption and domestic demand, and its overall economic strategy remains essentially unchanged—focused on expanding investment in manufacturing, especially in advanced technologies and new strategic industries.

Theories abound as to why Beijing has chosen to ignore the warnings. Many speculate that Chinese leader Xi Jinping is not receiving accurate information or that he and his closest allies have abandoned development as a priority in favor of national security goals or a desire to control the commanding heights of global industry. Others argue that Beijing knows what it should do, but political or institutional constraints prevent it from taking decisive action.

In truth, while these factors all play a part in shaping policy, the real explanation is far simpler. The party is sticking to this path because the leadership believes it is working. It sees the same troubling data—including rising external surpluses, weak consumption, slower household income growth, weak credit demand, rising unemployment and underemployment, and persistent deflation—but it has reached wildly different conclusions about what these developments indicate and what Beijing should do in response.

Chinese economic policy is nested within a methodical planning process, which is meant to ensure that short-term pressures do not distract from long-term priorities. The problem is that the long-term strategy is predicated on the belief that control of advanced technologies and industry will trigger a new round of high-speed growth. This economic strategy can best be understood as a Marxism-infused misapplication of neoclassical economic development theory: Beijing is obsessed with control over technology and physical capital and believes that its ultrahigh levels of investment will accelerate productivity gains and wage growth. But a disproportionate focus on manufacturing and technology cannot increase productivity or wages if those investments are decoupled from the needs of the market and trends in aggregate final demand. This is especially true in a country as large as China—already the world’s largest exporter—that cannot rely on further exports to fuel growth.

China’s worldview treats development as equivalent to capital accumulation and sees productivity as determined by the control and deployment of technology. It posits that the growth of the service sector, higher wages, and higher consumption are the consequences of that physical underlying foundation—they follow naturally in later stages of the development process once the productive industries have flourished. In this conception of economic development, the right strategy is always more investment, more industry, and more technology. The role of the state is to ensure that household savings and industrial profits are recycled through the state-owned banking system or government-guided capital into priority investments rather than frivolous activities, such as services or consumption. The resulting structural imbalance that weakens household income growth, the service sector, and public services was thus not an accident; in China’s economic development theory, this is seen as self-discipline that will pay off with more rapid long-term development.

A key corollary to this view is that the government’s macroeconomic management must guide resource allocation proactively through hands-on intervention, not impassively through interest rates and exchange rates. The state can harness the dynamism and ambition of markets to promote efficiency and innovation in defined spaces, but it is the government’s job to identify the correct focal points for investment to ensure that priority sectors grow at maximum speed and then to intervene to ameliorate imbalances when they inevitably arise.

In this conception of macroeconomics, the economy consists of a massive input-output table where every cell in the matrix is a sector that consumes other cells’ outputs and provides inputs to other producers. If there is an imbalance in supply or demand for certain products—which becomes evident in high or low prices—it may be because of overinvestment in the producing sector or underinvestment in downstream consuming sectors. Government policy seeks to increase output and steadily lower prices by facilitating investment and production throughout the matrix, adjusting the pace and balance of resource allocation as needed if one cell’s output exceeds the demands of the others. The goal is not to develop overcapacity or low profitability in these targeted sectors, but the optimal path for rapid development will inevitably push sectors in that direction.

Beijing has followed this playbook closely. When, for instance, the real estate sector collapsed in 2022 and the structural imbalances in China’s economy became too big to ignore, Beijing responded by increasing its efforts to micromanage credit flows, reversing experiments with interest rate liberalization and financial market reforms while tightening oversight of capital markets and industrial policy funding. Central bank relending facilities now channel low-cost bank loans to prioritize industrial upgrading and equipment purchases for targeted sectors. Thousands of government-run equity funds provide low-cost capital to firms that support a web of industrial and technology strategies. State-owned enterprises intervene to help boost valuations in the stock market, and regulators prioritize firms in strategic sectors for new listings. When a sector is overinvested, policy campaigns such as “anti-involution”—a neologism that describes a basket of discordant policies to resolve overcapacity—are deployed to slow future investment, buttress profitability by fixing prices, or set standards to restrict market entry in a bid to slow output while the other cells in the input-output tables catch up and restore balance over time.

Although hard to comprehend in the context of a market economy, some aspects of this approach have an understandable internal logic in China, especially at a micro level. The collapse of the property sector—roughly a quarter of GDP at its peak—sent massive shock waves through very specific supply and demand pathways in the economy. It made sense for Beijing to calibrate policy for the affected sectors and think in terms of input-output tables as it designed its responses. The frequency of bubbles in China’s equity, commodity, and property markets over the past 15 years has left policymakers deeply wary of accommodative monetary policy for the entire economy, which helps explain why they are experimenting with more targeted tools aimed at specific sectors.

Likewise, a certain level of triumphalism in Beijing is understandable, given that Chinese companies are breaking into pole positions in major conventional industries, such as autos and telecommunications, and many others are poised to thrive in cutting-edge sectors, such as robotics and biotechnology—all in the wake of a massive hit to GDP. For a generation of economic policymakers who cut their teeth trying to revitalize a moribund planned economy in the 1990s and the first decade of this century, the emergence of world-leading (often private) firms is definitive evidence that Beijing has found a winning recipe. From this perspective, Beijing is also dismissive of foreign complaints about unfair market interventions or trade imbalances as the sour grapes of rich countries that had grown accustomed to rent-seeking technology monopolies.

China’s leaders also see some deflation as the fruits of hard-won efficiency gains: the elimination of bottlenecks, economies of scale in manufacturing clusters, construction of world-class infrastructure, improved logistics, and removal of market barriers that have lowered costs up and down manufacturing supply chains. One-off cost reductions from efficiency gains or the abrupt loss of construction activity must be digested by the economy, they would argue, not offset by stimulus. And while foreign commentators and dignitaries warn of growing trade pushback, the data tells a surprisingly different story: China’s export growth barely slowed as the Trump administration imposed sweeping duties and the European Union set up defenses against a handful of sectors, such as electric vehicles.

The core problem with China’s economic strategy, however, is that aggregate domestic demand consistently falls far short of domestic supply. Chinese planners’ input-output tables will not balance unless domestic workers are buying the goods that China’s industries produce. Otherwise, everything else must be exported, often at lower prices. The leadership understands this in principle but believes that the control of advanced industries and technology will, ipso facto, cause productivity to rise, sustain economic growth into the future, and generate higher wages and therefore higher consumption.

This is the flaw in Beijing’s approach—the dangerous legacy of vestigial Marxism and misapplied neoclassical growth theory. In reality, state intervention in markets can depress overall productivity and workers can see flat wages even when China succeeds at promoting advanced industries.

It is true that capital and technology create productivity gains in well-functioning market economies. But the causality usually breaks down if resources are allocated by policy interventions that are divorced from returns on investment. The control or ownership of advanced industries does not inherently cause economic growth, because they are far too small a share of the economy. Instead, new products or technologies generate productivity when they are distributed and used throughout the economy in ways that improve efficiency or increase output. It is not the success of a robotics firm that leads to efficiency gains, for example, it is the deployment of its robots in factories to save production costs—namely by displacing workers—and boost output. If those benefits do not exceed the true cost of the new industrial robots—even with layers of subsidies—then the net effect is not positive, no matter how impressive the factories themselves are, or how successful the robotics firm itself appears.

The use of new production technologies also requires a degree of creative destruction that Beijing’s own industrial policy interventions have inhibited. When governments, not price-conscious firms, make technology and investment decisions, policymakers are not just gambling that they know better than the market how to boost long-run performance. They are also betting that they know how to use those technologies to create innovative new business models. Yet in China, state influence over resource allocation has ossified markets, especially by reducing bankruptcies, in ways that limit the fruits of innovation and efficiency gains in the broader economy. Additionally, much of China’s industrial policy aims to build self-reliance, which, by definition, focuses on duplicative investments that buttress domestic industrial prowess without necessarily adding efficiency.

Moreover, even though China’s many layers of industrial policy have helped launch successful companies and built an impressive fleet of ultramodern factories, there are also tremendous inefficiencies and waste that never make the headlines. Overall productivity must be calculated comprehensively, evaluating the net output gains against all the industrial subsidies, bad debts, foregone profits, and indirect support for investment that the government has provided. The reality is that China’s aggregate success rate is far lower than the shining stars would suggest: the remarkable global reach of electric vehicle and battery makers such as BYD, CATL, and Li Auto masks the dozens of failed companies that generated nonperforming loans and ate up unprofitable capital subsidies throughout the bulk of the sector and its supply chain. It is far from obvious that the net return on investment from industrial policy spending, estimated by the International Monetary Fund to be up to four percent of GDP, is positive, and it could be deeply negative, despite pockets of success.

Finally, Beijing is betting that superefficient manufacturing and success in strategic industries will eventually lead to higher wages, which will then cause consumption to surge. It is true that productivity growth causes real incomes to rise in a competitive market economy. But if investments in labor-saving technologies are driven by state policy, not returns on investment, wages can fall even as firms become more efficient. Rising household income requires a key intermediate step: employment, which is not a core characteristic of China’s capital-intensive, advanced manufacturing investment strategy. Indeed, state funding has helped China—a middle-income economy—become a world leader in high-tech “dark factories” that operate without distracting human accommodations, such as light. This may increase output in prized manufacturing cells in the input-output tables, but if demand for labor is not growing in aggregate, the whole equation starts to fall apart.

Still, the most pernicious consequence of Beijing’s economic model is entrenched deflation. The focus on expanding production combined with China’s weak consumer demand has caused the producer price index to fall for longer than it has risen over the past decade. This is a far larger threat to China’s economy than a mere nuisance of sectoral imbalances. Deflation reduces private-sector credit demand; undercuts incentives for new investments; and crushes employment, wage expectations, and, therefore, consumer confidence, which leads to weaker domestic demand—not in carefully demarcated input-output relationships but throughout the economy. Deflation also saps fiscal revenues and puts pressure on financial institutions in ways that severely limit the state’s capacity to act decisively the longer it lasts. Deflationary pressures in China also spill over to the global economy through cheaper exports and a weakening real exchange rate, driving more tensions between Beijing and its trading partners.

There is immense risk in falling behind the curve in fighting deflation, but Beijing is not likely to change course if it continues to believe its strategy will boost productivity and jumpstart domestic demand in the long run. Some analysts speculate that Beijing could be forced into a reappraisal of its vision in response to unemployment pressures or the proliferation of foreign trade friction, but it has already shown its cards. The accumulation of unemployment, underemployment, and low wages for Chinese households and workers are already significant, yet Beijing has responded with only modest new household subsidies and incremental investment in social services. Likewise, as external trade tensions have risen, Beijing has managed these country-by-country disputes as diplomatic issues, through a mix of threats and negotiations, to preserve the bulk of China’s export markets.

The decision to pursue short-term Band-Aid measures for a weak labor market and foreign trade tensions is confusing at first pass. For a government laser-focused on self-reliance and eliminating foreign dependencies, it is a curious strategy to become ever more dependent on foreign demand—over which Beijing has little control—as its primary driver of economic growth. Likewise, for a regime that is obsessed with social stability, it would seem prudent to focus more on wages and labor demand. Yet the strategy makes a degree of sense when viewed from the leadership’s perspective that China is in a transitional period. It may be unpleasant and difficult, but it is an unavoidable step on the path to a technology-led growth model.

The leadership’s current confidence in this transition process means that it could be many years before the warning signs, already visible, become persuasive internally. This all but guarantees that Beijing will continue to defer action while deflationary pressures and external trade tensions build. Deflation and weak corporate profitability are already eroding China’s fiscal revenues, which have basically been flat in nominal terms since 2019. Deflation and the growing debt burden are also corroding the financial system, which will ultimately require large write-offs and recapitalization from the government budget to prevent credit growth from slowing further. A $54 billion bank recapitalization just took place two months ago, with little effect on lending.

Beijing’s ideological commitment to its industry- and technology-focused development strategy is creating a dangerous cycle that will likely produce weaker consumption, slower income growth, and even greater trade friction with the rest of the world. Moreover, in the face of such challenges, China’s economic leadership is compensating by expanding state intervention in the economy in ways that further undermine market forces and the independence of the private sector. At this rate, even if the leadership eventually recognizes the need to change strategy, China may not have the fiscal or financial space to rebalance the economy toward a more sustainable and diversified growth model.

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

#6029. The U.S. Senate Just Got a Lot Younger

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Infographic: The U.S. Senate Just Got a Lot Younger

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

The U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday saw the biggest Senate turnover in nearly 50 years, with at least 15 new lawmakers elected to join the upper chamber come January. Several of Capitol Hill’s octogenarians will be leaving the Beltway, and a whole batch of younger legislators will be moving in.

The United States once had one of the oldest average upper houses in the developed world, according to data from IPU Parline. As of this month, the average age of U.S. senators is 65.4 years old. Among members of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) for which data is available, the next eldest nation’s class is roughly five years younger, with Poland’s Senate having an average age of 61.9 years and the Netherlands’s Senate an average age of 58.4 years.

The U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday saw the biggest Senate turnover in nearly 50 years, with at least 15 new lawmakers elected to join the upper chamber come January. Several of Capitol Hill’s octogenarians will be leaving the Beltway, and a whole batch of younger legislators will be moving in.

The United States once had one of the oldest average upper houses in the developed world, according to data from IPU Parline. As of this month, the average age of U.S. senators is 65.4 years old. Among members of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) for which data is available, the next eldest nation’s class is roughly five years younger, with Poland’s Senate having an average age of 61.9 years and the Netherlands’s Senate an average age of 58.4 years.

But Washington’s new crop of senators will reduce that number by quite a significant margin. The average age of the 15 departing U.S. senators is 70 years old. Their replacements are coming in at roughly age 55. That will bring the average age of the U.S. Senate down to at least 62.8 years—still above most of its OECD counterparts but a dramatic shift nonetheless.

That also means that the Senate is moving toward closing the age gap between its members and the constituents they represent. According to the Economist, the United States had the least age-representative government within the OECD as of December 2024, with lawmakers more than 20 years older than the average population age. The outgoing class of senators has only eight members under age 50.

The new class, however, will nearly double that number, with at least 15 lawmakers under 50. Although the eldest age will remain at 93 (Republican Sen. Chuck Grassley of Iowa), the youngest age will go from 39 (Democratic Sen. Jon Ossoff of Georgia) to 37 (Democratic Sen.-elect James Talarico of Texas).

With more and more young people voting Democrat, according to Associated Press exit polls, it may explain why the upcoming Senate class also shifted younger. Still, only around 1 in 10 voters in the 2026 midterms were under 30.

Alexandra Sharp is the World Brief writer at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @alexandrassharp.bsky.social X: @AlexandraSSharp

#6028. GOP eyes Senate comeback after Democratic bloodbath, but another political nightmare could await

The 2026 midterm elections result in a political shift as Democrats are projected to take control of the U.S. House and Senate. Fox News host Jesse Watters breaks down the major Democratic victories.

Republican control of the Senate could return as soon as the 2028 general election, but only if the party can pull off a series of crucial victories in competitive races, a possibility that seems distant following Tuesday night's devastating Democratic sweep.

As of publication, Democrats and independents who caucus with them have secured 54 Senate seats, a total that could grow to as many as 56 pending election calls in Mississippi and Alaska.

Assuming Republicans hold the vice presidency in 2028, far from a guarantee, they would need to flip at least four seats during that election cycle to win the Senate back.

While there are ample pickup opportunities for Republicans in 2028 if the party can return to its typical national performance benchmarks, several vulnerable GOP-held seats could complicate their efforts to retake the upper chamber.

DEMS FLIPS YET ANOTHER SENATE SEAT IN TRUMP COUNTRY AMID SWEEPING GOP UPSETS

The U.S. Capitol on Dec.12, 2024 (Fox News/Paul Steinhauser)

Iowa Sen. Chuck Grassley, for instance, turned 93 in September and faces a decision about whether to seek another term in 2028, when Republicans will be defending his seat in a state where Democrat Josh Turek just defeated Republican Ashley Hinson by roughly four percentage points. His departure before then could also give Iowa's incoming Democratic governor the opportunity to appoint a temporary replacement.

Another possibility that could make the GOP's path back to a majority even more uncertain is the admission of Puerto Rico and Washington, D.C., to the union as full states.

Such a move would likely require the Democratic-led Senate to abolish or overcome the legislative filibuster, something lawmakers representing more moderate or competitive states may balk at, as well as support in the House and either presidential approval or enough votes to override a veto.

CHUCK SCHUMER AFTER SWEEPING DEMOCRATIC MIDTERM VICTORIES: 'I'LL BE RE-ELECTED AS MAJORITY LEADER'

The proposal comes as Republicans seek to convince voters that keeping the GOP in power would translate into more money in Americans' pockets. (Finn Gomez/Getty Images)

Even if the filibuster is done away with, President Donald Trump could veto admittance bills. Democrats currently lack the numbers to override such a veto.

If Republicans lose the White House in 2028, however, Democrats could have a clearer path to advancing statehood for Puerto Rico and Washington, D.C., a move that would add four Senate seats and potentially complicate Republican efforts to win back the chamber, though significant political and legal obstacles would remain.

Assuming none of that happens, Democrats will be defending six Senate seats that could offer Republicans potential pickup opportunities in 2028.

Republicans will have pickup opportunities in New Hampshire, Nevada, Arizona, Georgia, Pennsylvania and Ohio that year. They will, however, be forced to defend vulnerable Senate seats in Wisconsin, Iowa and North Carolina.

Indiana, which saw a significant blue shift, also has a Senate seat up for contention in 2028, while Nebraska, where independent Dan Osborn mounted a competitive but unsuccessful challenge to Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts on Tuesday, will not hold another regularly scheduled Senate election until 2030.

While 2028 could deliver Republicans a majority if they play their cards right, another defeat on the scale of 2026 could put the Democratic Party dangerously close to the 60-vote threshold necessary to invoke cloture and counter a filibuster.

"It may be unlikely, but I do think it's feasible," Chip Wyatt, Heritage Action's government relations director, told Fox News Digital. "We're just as capable of having a night like the Democrats had on Tuesday in 2028.

"If anything, that election, the election we had on Tuesday, has shown us that two years is a very, very long time in politics. I think if you had told anybody in 2024 that this is what happened in 2028, they probably wouldn't believe you."

For Republicans to have a good night in 2028, Wyatt argued that conservatives would have to focus on affordability issues while Democrats overstep their mandate by allowing the fringes of their party to dictate its policy agenda.

Texas Sen. Ted Cruz took a similar line in reacting to Tuesday night's election results.

"We will rebuild as a true blue-collar party, not by embracing socialism, but by being real free-market conservatives, the way we built the Texas economic miracle in the first place, and we will build and rebuild America as well," he said.

"When the zealots overreach, and they will, we will stand ready to lead in the direction of freedom, of prosperity, of safety and security and common sense. We will return to a new dawn of freedom in America."

The year 2030 features slightly fewer pickups for the GOP with a comparable number of states to defend. While Republicans will have pickup opportunities in Arizona, Nevada, Wisconsin and Michigan, they will also be forced to defend seats in Texas, Ohio and Pennsylvania.

MAJORITY LEADER THUNE SAYS SENATE GOP WON'T 'STAND IDLY BY' AS DEMOCRATS TAKE CONTROL OF CONGRESS

Sen. Chuck Grassley, R-Iowa and chairman of the Senate Judiciary Committee, speaks during a hearing in Washington Sept. 16, 2025 (Al Drago/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

The 2032 election will feature the same regularly scheduled Senate seats contested in 2026, giving Republicans another opportunity to retake deep-red states they blundered this year, such as Kansas and South Carolina. Historically, however, some moderate Democrats have managed to hang on to statewide offices in typically red states, making a reversion far from a certainty.

Former West Virginia Sen. Joe Manchin, for instance, managed to survive multiple GOP challenges in the deep-red state. Kansas and Kentucky, similarly, have Democratic governors that survived Republican challenges in races that received national attention.

While many Republicans, and even some Democrats, have suggested liberal pickups in deep red states could be one-cycle wonders, Wyatt cautioned against complacency.

"We should always assume the politicians want to get re-elected, and, so, the senators in the deep red states that have been recently elected as Democrats are going to want to keep their seats. And, so, they're going to be very strategic in how they vote and in what kind of bills they're introducing," Wyatt said.

"In some of these deep red states, it's going to be difficult for [Democrats] to hold on to these seats. ... Now, granted, I've already said two years is a long time, six years is an even longer time.

"So, who knows what kind of political landscape we're looking at in 2032, but Republicans should definitely start off with a strong advantage in many of these states. ... But it's never wise to counter chickens before they hatch."

Electoral politics is famously unpredictable. Nobody in 2024, for instance, envisioned South Carolina or Kansas sending Democrats to the Senate in 2026. It is possible that the Republican path to a Senate majority, or lack thereof, will be comparably surprising.
