#6027. Don’t Soak the Rich

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Daniel WALDENSTROM is Professor of Economics at the Research Institute of Industrial Economics in Stockholm. He is the author of Richer and More Equal: A New History of Wealth in the West.

Weak Institutions—Not Private Fortunes—Deform Democracy

As private wealth continues to grow to levels never before seen in modern history, a powerful idea is taking hold in the West: the accumulation of great fortunes poses an existential threat to democracy. This idea is not novel. Over a decade ago, the political scientists Jacob Hacker and Paul Pierson asserted that the rise of plutocracy has been a defining trend of American politics since the late 1970s. The economist Paul Krugman argued in 2011 that the “extreme concentration of income is incompatible with real democracy.”

Lately, however, it has begun to gain traction in the halls of power of Western democracies. In his farewell address, former U.S. President Joe Biden argued that an “oligarchy of extreme wealth, power, and influence” was threatening “our entire democracy, our basic rights and freedoms.” Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez echoed the sentiment at a 2025 G-20 summit, warning that concentrated wealth “corrodes democratic institutions.”

Some now see confiscation as the answer. After a bitter debate and over $300 million of cumulative spending by supporters and opponents, Californians rejected a statewide “billionaires’ tax” ballot measure in this week’s midterm elections. But the proposal counted among its proponents six Nobel laureates in economics, including Daron Acemoglu, Peter Diamond, and Joseph Stiglitz, who endorsed the tax in a letter framing it as “a turning point in the battle between democracy and oligarchy.” The French economist Gabriel Zucman called the measure’s defeat “just the beginning” and vowed that though “Silicon Valley billionaires have won this battle … they will lose this war.” Several European political leaders, including the former German Green Party leader Annalena Baerbock and Danish Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen, have floated wealth tax proposals of their own in recent years.

The risk they have identified is genuine. Democratic societies are not doing enough to prevent the superrich from unduly influencing politics. But their diagnosis—not to mention their preferred solution—is incorrect. Billionaires’ participation in politics does not in itself indicate that a democracy has been captured by moneyed interests. The strength of the political and civil institutions meant to protect against the exercise of those interests is a far more important indicator.

Instead of imposing wealth taxes, countries that are serious about safeguarding their political systems should take their cues from those democracies that have successfully insulated their politics from the influence of large fortunes. They should adopt transparent election laws and restraints on private campaign contributions and encourage a strong, independent media. Such measures will help make societies more democratic. Taxing wealth will simply make them poorer.

Arguments in favor of taxing wealth in the name of democracy often hinge on an understanding of billionaires as a monolithic bloc, committed to an agenda that runs counter to the public interest. These claims contain a kernel of truth. According to the benchmark V-Dem and Freedom House indices, the United States has indeed been in a state of democratic decline since the early 2010s. But it does not follow that billionaires as a group are responsible for this decline—or that such a unified group even exists.

To empirically determine its existence, I classified the richest Americans in the 2026 Forbes 400 listing according to their traceable political donations, analyzing both their average annual giving and their total giving over the past three decades. This method is no doubt imperfect—donations can be informal or hidden and are not the only way to achieve political influence—but the pattern that emerges should nonetheless cast doubt on the idea of a single billionaire class marching on Washington.

The evidence shows that roughly 30 individuals, or eight percent of U.S. billionaires in the Forbes 400, are high-level donors, giving more than $1 million each year for political purposes. Roughly half of American billionaires give on average less than $30,000 a year, 35 percent give less than $10,000, and 15 percent, or 60 of the listed billionaires, have no visible political footprint. Just 16 donors—four percent—have given more than $50 million over the last 30 years. This small minority may well plow enough money into elections to make up for the majority of billionaires with no apparent interest in politics (although if Elon Musk’s midterm outlay is any indication, even vast sums of money are no guarantee of political outcomes). But the very fact that both these groups exist suggests that the presence of the ultrawealthy alone is not necessarily responsible for American democratic decay. The weakness of the institutions meant to check their influence, on the other hand, is.

For the American billionaires who do engage in politics, federal rules and institutions make it easy to achieve disproportionate influence through large-scale private spending. Super PACs are permitted to receive unlimited contributions from individuals, corporations, unions, and other political committees. The hollowing out of independent public-service media and the consolidation of private media make matters worse, allowing a small number of wealthy owners to shape the dominant narrative unchecked.

Much of the momentum behind a wealth tax comes from the United States. But it is hardly the only country with a high proportion of superwealthy residents. Sweden and Switzerland have among the most billionaires per capita in the world, with around 4.7 and 5.0 billionaires per million inhabitants, respectively—higher than the United States’ 2.9 and dwarfing the average of 1.3 for Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) countries. If wealth tax proponents were correct about the relationship between extreme wealth and democratic health, one would expect both Sweden’s and Switzerland’s democracies to be in dire shape.

But in neither country has the presence of large private fortunes significantly damaged democracy. Both rank highly among liberal democracies when assessing the fairness of elections, the rule of law, checks on the executive, trust in government, and media freedom. Sweden ranks fifth in the 2026 World Press Freedom Index, and Switzerland ranks eighth. As of 2025, they were tied for sixth in Transparency International’s Corruption Perceptions Index; the United States ranked 29th. According to the OECD’s 2025 trust survey, 62 percent of Swiss respondents had confidence in their national government, the highest of all countries polled. Sweden, at 42 percent, ranked 15th among the countries polled, higher than the OECD average of 40 percent and far exceeding the United States’ 27 percent, as measured by the Partnership for Public Service.

It might be tempting to point to tax policy as an explanation. Indeed, social democratic Sweden, with its 41 percent tax-to-GDP ratio as of 2024, has one of the world’s highest tax levels. Switzerland, however, maintains a tax-to-GDP ratio of roughly 27 percent, well below the OECD average of 34 percent and about even with the U.S. level of 25 percent. That these countries can support large entrepreneurial fortunes without sacrificing their democracies is in fact not the result of draconian restrictions on how those fortunes are spent in politics. Two other shared institutional features offer more compelling answers.

In Sweden, political parties receive funding mainly through public structures. Private contributions are allowed, but individual donors represent a relatively small share of total party funding, and disclosure rules make all large private contributions visible. Sweden’s constitution grants citizens and journalists the right to access almost any official document. Ministers hold little direct influence over administrative agencies, allowing the agencies to operate independently of executive authority. These rules do not eliminate lobbying, but they largely prevent private money from discreetly entering and influencing the political process.

Switzerland’s barriers between private wealth and the levers of government, although similarly sturdy, stop money from influencing politics in slightly different ways. The country’s federal system diffuses power across 26 regional cantons and over 2,000 municipalities, and frequent regional and federal referendums give citizens veto power over legislation. Switzerland has no direct federal funding of parties and no general spending ceiling, but national disclosure rules introduced in 2022 require major campaigns and large donors to identify their financing. The country does have a wealth tax dating back to the nineteenth century, but it is administered at the canton level and its highest marginal rate is nowhere near as punitive as the California proposal. Dispersing authority and enshrining accountability over government decisions through direct democracy are far more responsible for the success of Swiss democracy.

Both countries also have long traditions of private journalism and independent public-service media. A mix of private outlets and public-service media, which has been shown to foster greater public trust in the news and resilience to online disinformation, prevents any single news provider from monopolizing the airwaves or suppressing information.

These institutions are not perfect, and private donations still play a role in Swedish and Swiss politics. That countries with nearly twice the number of billionaires per capita as the United States have thriving democracies should, however, give the lie to the idea that the superrich, by their mere existence, deform democracy.

The proposals currently in vogue are presented as a novel solution to the novel problem of concentrated wealth. But societies have attempted to tax wealth before. Throughout the twentieth century, many European countries imposed similarly designed taxes on household net wealth for redistributive purposes. Problems quickly emerged. Taxing individuals who rarely generated cash income and whose fortunes were thus not easily estimated outraged entrepreneurs, lowered investment, and hampered growth. In the 1970s and 1980s, governments on both the left and the right took note. They tried to discount certain business assets and exempt closely held firms from taxation to mitigate the distortions. But tinkering around these edges only caused new problems of unfairness. Revenues, which at that time constituted less than half a percent of GDP across Europe, compared with total taxes that often stood at 40 percent of GDP, slumped. Ultimately, most countries scrapped the tax.

Many of today’s proponents are more likely to cite the threat of unprecedented fortunes to democracy than the need for redistribution as the impetus for a wealth tax. But they offer no new solutions to the problems of old. Nor would a wealth tax stop billionaires from financing politics, since the wealthiest hardly need to throw the full weight of their fortunes around to wield political influence.

Supporters might argue that the pro-democracy case for a wealth tax is much broader in scope. After all, the superrich exert influence on politics in more ways than just their donations. A wealth tax might not stop a billionaire from buying a state election, but it might stop one from, for example, buying Paramount or The Washington Post. Imperfect a solution as it is, a billionaires’ tax referendum represents a more achievable goal than amending the Constitution to wrest power from the executive.

But a wealth tax has never been an efficient fiscal tool or a substitute for substantive democratic renewal. Whatever small democratic gains it might feasibly bring would be outweighed by the threat to business ventures, capital formation, and long-term economic development it would pose. As anything more than a symbolic measure, a tax on uncertain, often illiquid values is essentially a tax on entrepreneurial risk-taking. But the case against it should not be mistaken for a broadside against all capital taxation. Governments should still seek to raise substantial revenue from taxes on the returns on capital and should set rates and brackets how they please. They must not, however, forget the crucial distinction between taxing already realized income and taxing assets that may yet generate new returns.

A better agenda to safeguard democracy against the influence of money should prioritize reforming the institutions whose weaknesses make such influence possible. Limits on campaign and party finance and more transparent disclosure of lobbying and conflicts of interest would, at the very least, make elections more difficult to buy. Increased funding for public media and stricter antitrust rules to block media mergers would create a more diverse landscape of both private and independent public-service outlets. These measures would affect just the few American billionaires who plow significant parts of their fortunes into politics, leaving the majority of the country’s wealthiest to keep doing what they do best—creating value and driving growth.

Democracies should, of course, always fear oligarchy. They should actively work to curtail the influence of money on politics. They should not, however, confuse economic success with political capture. Countries need entrepreneurs who are willing to take risks and build wealth. Democracy will survive in an era of extraordinary fortunes not by antagonizing successful people but by making political power harder to buy.

#6025. Global AI Stocks React to U.S. Midterm Results

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: Global AI Stocks React to U.S. Midterm Results

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at how global AI stocks are reacting to the U.S. midterms, Pope Leo XIV’s tour of Latin America, and more hoax bomb threats across Europe.

Global stock markets in Europe and Asia plunged on Friday in the wake of big Democratic gains in this week’s U.S. midterm elections. Their reactions came a day after Wall Street saw massive sell-offs, as investors feared that Washington’s blue wave could bring tighter tech regulations for the artificial intelligence industry.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at how global AI stocks are reacting to the U.S. midterms, Pope Leo XIV’s tour of Latin America, and more hoax bomb threats across Europe.

Global stock markets in Europe and Asia plunged on Friday in the wake of big Democratic gains in this week’s U.S. midterm elections. Their reactions came a day after Wall Street saw massive sell-offs, as investors feared that Washington’s blue wave could bring tighter tech regulations for the artificial intelligence industry.

According to NBC exit polls, 57 percent of U.S. voters believed that AI would do more harm than good to society, and 56 percent of individuals characterized the tech’s controversial advancement as “crucial or a major factor” in determining who they voted for. Hoping to capitalize on that hesitance, many Democratic candidates had promised to rein in AI’s development and slow the construction of data centers. Many voters ahead of Tuesday’s election had expressed concerns about data centers’ impact on job creation, water usage, energy bills, and local pollution.

Some of Silicon Valley’s most influential executives, from SpaceX CEO Elon Musk to OpenAI co-founder Greg Brockman, collectively spent more than half a billion dollars backing Republican candidates. However, that didn’t appear to be enough to stop the U.S. midterms’ AI reckoning, FP’s Rishi Iyengar and Christina Lu wrote. Notably, several historically red states that host a vast number of data centers, such as Texas, swung blue.

This has made investors worried. On Thursday, the Nasdaq dropped by 5.52 percent (or roughly $2.5 trillion). The S&P 500 also fell by 4.36 percent, and the Dow Jones declined by 3.44 percent. All three index averages recorded their worst days since April 2025, when U.S. President Donald Trump launched his sweeping global trade war.

Such dips prompted foreign markets to react with matching concern. London’s FTSE 100 index sank by 2.35 percent on Friday, Germany’s Dax index tumbled by 2 percent, France’s Cac 40 decreased by 0.8 percent, and Spain’s Ibex declined by 0.6 percent. In the Indo-Pacific, Japan’s Nikkei 225 index dropped by 6 percent, and South Korea’s Kospi index fell by more than 6.5 percent.

Still, the initial market panic appears to have slowed for now. Wall Street began to recover on Friday from its mass stock sell-off, with the Nasdaq and S&P 500 both rallying. The rebound may be partly related to a new U.S. Labor Department report on Friday that saw the U.S. economy add 165,000 jobs in October.

Leo’s big trip. Pope Leo XIV kicked off a 12-day tour of Latin America on Friday, less than four weeks after undergoing surgery for lung cancer. The trip, which will see Leo deliver nine masses and 36 speeches across three countries, will test the pope’s health and stamina at a time when some observers have wondered whether papal succession talks need to begin sooner than expected. The Vatican, though, has made clear that Leo requires no special accommodations for this visit and will be accompanied by his usual delegation.

Leo will begin his trip in Uruguay before heading to Argentina and Peru. In Uruguay, Leo is expected to appeal to the country’s increasingly secular population; only 1 in 3 Uruguayans identify as Catholic, the lowest rate in Latin America. Leo has a history of prioritizing trips to countries with secular-leaning populations, such as his visit to France in September.

From Uruguay, Leo will head to Argentina, the homeland of his predecessor, Pope Francis. During that visit, the pope will meet with far-right Argentine President Javier Milei, whose policies have often run contradictory to the Vatican’s preachings. That includes Milei’s efforts to transform Argentina into an AI hub; Leo has made consideration of AI’s ethical implications an early focus of his papacy.

Finally, Leo will end his trip in Peru, where he spent a significant portion of his life. The pope is expected to travel to the city of Cusco, which sits at more than 11,000 feet above sea level. Thinner air quality there could be a challenge for the pope’s recovering lungs; however, the Vatican has stressed that Leo is healthy enough to do it.

More bomb threats. Another round of hoax bomb threats targeted Europe on Friday, adding to this week’s onslaught of suspected hybrid attacks on the continent. Newsrooms in Bulgaria and Ireland reportedly received emailed bomb threats from a “Mikhail Andropov” on Friday, forcing the media outlets to suspend programming and evacuate their offices. A British broadcaster also said that it had received a security alert, though it is unclear if that incident was linked to the other two.

Several European planes were also diverted on Friday due to false bomb threats—the third such incident this week. The fake threats have caused massive air traffic disruptions across the continent and upended travel plans for thousands of passengers. Friday’s incidents also came amid a surge in other types of hybrid warfare operations targeting Europe, including recent cyberattacks on supermarket refrigeration systems in at least 10 countries.

It remains unclear who is behind these attacks and if they are all related, but security officials suspect that Russia may be responsible, with analysts telling Reuters that the threats could be punishment for countries that have armed or financed Ukraine. Moscow continues to deny any involvement.

Chris vs. Chris. New Zealand voters head to the polls on Saturday for a closely watched general election defined by cost-of-living concerns. Analysts predict that the race will be a tight one. According to local estimates, the incumbent coalition led by the center-right National Party is polling at 26.4 percent, and the opposition left-leaning alliance led by the Labour Party is polling at 30.7 percent.

Whichever coalition wins a majority will likely put a Chris in power. Helming the National Party is Prime Minister Christopher Luxon, who has held power since 2023. Luxon is known for inking a free trade deal with India, implementing education reforms, and cracking down on gang violence; he is also known for verbal gaffes and appearing disconnected from ordinary people due to his millionaire status.

Leading Labour is Chris Hipkins. Having helped spearhead the country’s COVID-19 response as a cabinet minister under former Prime Minister Jacinda Ardern, Hipkins has pledged to reduce high prices and bolster healthcare funding. He has also been plagued by verbal gaffes (most notably, when he told people during the pandemic to “get outside and spread their legs”). If the National Party is ousted, it will have led New Zealand’s first one-term government in more than 50 years.

Brazil on Tuesday approved legislation that strengthened the government’s ability to do what?

A. Fight criminal gangs B. Cut back on deforestation C. Prosecute Flávio Bolsonaro D. Overcome presidential vetoes

It is perhaps the most divisive issue of our time: Should pineapple go on pizza? In May, Italian chef Davide Tamburella was fired from Australia’s now-shuttered La Costa restaurant for refusing to serve the topping, calling it an “insult to his culture.” This was the third time that Tamburella had refused to put the fruit on pizza in the past four years, among other complaints from management. On Tuesday, though, Australia’s Fair Work Commission dismissed Tamburella’s unfair termination claim on the grounds that his application had been filed too late.

Although he is fresh off an electoral victory, 81-year-old President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva will need to start cultivating a successor as soon as possible, Oliver Stuenkel writes.

#6024. Democratic senator-elect calls Trump 'unserious fool' while defending combative political style

Michigan Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed mocked Vice President JD Vance and President Donald Trump in his victory speech.

Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic senator-elect from Michigan, leaned into accusations of acting cocky on the path to victory over Republican candidate Mike Rogers, citing a need to push back on President Donald Trump’s manner of communication during an appearance on "Pod Save America."

"Long ago, you predicted that you would win this race by seven points, which, at the time, seemed like a pretty cocky and ill-considered thing to say. Then you won the race by seven points. People are still calling you cocky," podcast host Jon Favreau said during his interview with El-Sayed.

"I think there is a responsibility that we have to engage with [Trump] as the unserious fool that he is," El-Sayed replied.

EL-SAYED MOCKS TRUMP, VANCE AND ROGERS IN GLOATING VICTORY SPEECH SIGNALING COMBATIVE TONE IN SENATE

Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate, speaks at the Michigan Democratic Nominating Convention in Lansing, Mich., Aug. 29, 2026. (Anadolu via Getty Images)

El-Sayed’s response sheds light on his view of his own campaign and the tone that he believes Democrats need to adopt to counter Trump’s presence in American politics. It also raises questions of whether El-Sayed’s campaign strategies will yield results in Congress.

In his view, Trump has changed the style of acceptable communication. In turn, he accused Democrats of being hesitant to abandon a kind of decorum to fight fire with fire.

"I just think there’s a responsibility for us to walk out of 'The West Wing' episode that our politics used to feel like and into the reality of where we are," El-Sayed said, referring to a television drama known for its idealistic portrayal of American politics.

"I say that because if you’ve ever watched a bully in a room, if you don’t stand up to the bully, it’s not like the bully will just go away. The bully just gets stronger."

During his campaign, El-Sayed repeatedly pointed a finger at Trump for, in his view, ushering in a new age of political rhetoric. But that view also preceded his Senate bid.

TRUMP DOMINATES EL-SAYED'S ATTACKS AGAINST ROGERS, BUT GOP RIVAL OFFERS UNEXPECTED POLICY SPLIT

President Donald Trump delivers remarks during a campaign rally at the Ocean Center Arena Oct. 30, 2026, in Daytona Beach, Fla. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

He once accused President Donald Trump of inciting terrorism in a 2020 interview, citing a foiled plot to kidnap Gov. Gretchen Whitmer.

"These are terrorists; we have to call them that. We have to recognize that that is what the face of terrorism looks like right now. We have to step up against it," El-Sayed said.

"We gotta ask: Why are these people getting radicalized? Well, there must be some radical, extremist cleric out there who may be tweeting up a storm about all the things that they think need to be done, like liberating Michigan."

Although President Donald Trump did not call for any kind of violence against Whitmer, El-Sayed said he believed the president was at least partially responsible, pointing to a tweet Trump had made six months before the discovered plot.

"LIBERATE MICHIGAN!" Trump wrote in a post on Twitter, the social media platform now known as X. The post was one of a series calling to "liberate" Virginia and Minnesota from COVID-19 restrictions imposed by Democratic governors.

According to FBI comments to local media, the plot originated before Trump's post.

Even so, El-Sayed called the posts emblematic of a cultural toxicity that had made violence more likely.

"The idea that people would threaten her safety because she was willing to stand up to keep us all healthy is, to me, the example of what the kind of toxicity that we see out of Donald Trump creates," El-Sayed said.

MAJORITY LEADER THUNE SAYS SENATE GOP WON'T 'STAND IDLY BY' AS DEMOCRATS TAKE CONTROL OF CONGRESS

Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic Senate nominee in Michigan, takes questions from reporters after a campaign rally Oct. 24, 2026 in Detroit. (Paul Steinhauser/Fox News Digital)

A spokesperson for El-Sayed did not immediately respond to requests for comment on whether he believed in a red line for what kind of rhetoric Democrats should refrain from using.

Having won his U.S. Senate election, El-Sayed will be sworn into office Jan. 3, 2027, the start of the 120th Congress.

Leo Briceno is a politics reporter for the congressional team at Fox News Digital. He was previously a reporter with World Magazine.

#6022. The True Meaning of Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

“International politics, like all politics, is a struggle for power.” Hans Morgenthau wrote this sentence from his desk in Chicago at the dawn of the Cold War, as the United States and the Soviet Union embarked on an open-ended competition for global dominance. Politics Among Nations, the volume that Morgenthau ultimately produced, would quickly become the most widely adopted international relations textbook in the United States and provide an intellectual framework—realism—that would guide generations of scholars and policymakers.

Although much has changed in the nearly eight decades since Morgenthau’s most influential work was published, the fundamentals remain the same. Human nature, which for Morgenthau drove the struggle for power, is unmoved. States are still the most significant actors in the international system and compete for influence. Great powers continue to drive the course of history. Much of today’s world would seem to affirm Morgenthau’s conclusions: Russia’s attempts to establish a sphere of influence in Eastern Europe, China’s quest for hegemony in East Asia, and the United States’ revival of the Monroe Doctrine in the Western Hemisphere all suggest the enduring primacy of national interests over moral aspirations and international norms.

For many, Morgenthau’s contributions begin and end with his identification of power as the key determinant of state behavior. But this reading misses one of his core observations: that even if humans tend to be power-maximizing creatures, they are also moral beings who seek to limit their own power. Morgenthau’s writings reveal a dialectic between power and morality—two seemingly incompatible concepts that he spent his life attempting to reconcile. Far from romanticizing power, Morgenthau saw the need to tame it.

An immigrant who escaped Nazi Germany and owed his life to the United States, Morgenthau had specific guidance to offer his fellow Americans. Even as he urged the United States to protect its vital interests by maintaining a favorable balance of power in key regions, he implored the country to wield its power prudently. Morgenthau saw the United States as the only country with both the capacity and the will to fight on behalf of transcendent values that he believed power had to serve. He was committed to preserving American power as a check on the violence and bigotry he experienced. Yet he feared that U.S. overreach would hollow out the foundations of that power. This, for Morgenthau, would be a tragedy for humanity.

Perhaps no government has misread Morgenthau more than the second Trump administration. Its officials revel in the exercise of military force around the world, from Iran to Venezuela and the Caribbean, while dismissing concerns of alleged war crimes and threatening long-standing allies. White House Deputy Chief of Staff Stephen Miller typified the Trumpian outlook when, in January, he invoked the “iron laws of the world,” one “governed by strength,” “governed by force,” and “governed by power.”

Some analysts have also erroneously rooted the Trump administration’s actions in realism. A lengthy essay in The New York Times Magazine, for instance, ran under the headline “The Theory That Gives Trump a Blank Check for Aggression.” This is understandable, as the Trump administration has repeatedly claimed its actions are underpinned by “flexible realism.”

But any reading of realism that does not consider the moral dimensions of Morgenthau’s theory is both mistaken and dangerous. When the United States limits its own power, abiding by international norms, consulting allies and partners, and wielding its strength in pursuit of transcendent values, countries find American influence more palatable, which helps sustain it. But if Washington’s actions become untethered from these restraints, others will no longer defer to American prerogatives, and the world will become a more dangerous place.

Returning to the foundations of realism—to the man who introduced this intellectual tradition to Americans—is critical. If Morgenthau were to witness this moment in U.S. history, he would conclude that the country had overlearned his lessons about the importance of power and erred in equating it with the use of force. He would see a need to elevate moral questions and rebuild normative constraints on the exercise of power. And he would warn against foreign entanglements that undermine American democracy, which remains the wellspring of the country’s power. These are the lessons that will help the United States navigate an era of intensified great-power competition, one in which the margin for error is thin and the cost of miscalculation could be catastrophic.

Morgenthau was born into an assimilated, middle-class Jewish family in southern Germany in the winter of 1904. His conservative and authoritarian father, Ludwig, raised his only child to revere the monarchy and take pride in their country’s achievements. Morgenthau’s childhood might have passed as that of a shy, precocious boy in a self-assured bourgeois community, but Germany’s defeat in World War I and the rise of anti-Semitism set his life on a drastically different course.

In 1922, as a top student in his high school, Morgenthau was chosen to address his classmates and neighbors in a public ceremony honoring the school’s namesake. When he began to speak, he noticed that some had turned their backs and others were holding their noses at this “stinking Jew.” Morgenthau finished his remarks, but as he left the square, his classmates hurled anti-Semitic insults and spat on him. Just months later, Hitler descended on Morgenthau’s hometown of Coburg with hundreds of storm troopers, who assaulted protesters while the police looked on. Coburg soon gained the ignominious distinction of being the first town to elect the Nazis to power.

Morgenthau left Coburg hoping to become a writer or poet but was forced to pivot to law after hyperinflation ruined his family’s finances. During his apprenticeship under Hugo Sinzheimer, a leading left-wing labor lawyer, he saw conservative judges undermine the Weimar Republic and aid those who sought its destruction. Morgenthau later reflected in his intellectual autobiography that this led him to conclude that “what was decisive was not the merits of different legal interpretations but the distribution of political power.”

As life for German Jews deteriorated, he fled to Geneva to teach law. Switzerland was unfortunately not far enough away; after Hitler took power in Germany, students began boycotting his classes because they did not want to learn German law from a Jew. He then found a teaching position in Madrid but was displaced by the Spanish Civil War and wandered through Europe before arriving in New York in 1937 as a penniless refugee. Having witnessed the rise of fascism up close, Morgenthau was shocked to find that so many Americans believed that human nature was fundamentally good, that peace was the normal condition of humanity, and that history was a story of progress.

In New York and then Kansas City, Morgenthau grew alarmed as the United States watched Nazi Germany rearm, annex Austria, and invade Czechoslovakia. He believed that a failure to understand human nature had inspired Western democracies’ blind faith in international law and the League of Nations, leading them down the path of least resistance with appeasement of Hitler, straight into the moral abyss.

Shaken by the fall of France in 1940, Morgenthau began writing his first English-language book, taking aim at Americans’ confidence that the methods of the natural sciences could make sense of the social world and that the sheer force of reason would eventually eliminate conflict. In Scientific Man vs. Power Politics, published in 1946, he charged that rationalism and liberalism had misunderstood the nature of man, the nature of the world, and the nature of reason itself. Humans, in addition to having rational needs, possessed irrational impulses that could not be reasoned away. Man’s “lust for power would be satisfied only … if he became like God.” Countries, like humans, sought to maximize their power over one another, making conflict inevitable.

Scientific Man received disastrous reviews, and Morgenthau later recalled that it “shocked people to no end.” He adjusted his approach, tailoring his message to Americans and packaging it in a textbook that would appeal to their pragmatism. He wanted this book to mark an evolution in American thinking about foreign policy, privately writing that it would differ from its predecessors “as an automobile differs from a horse and buggy.” Two years later, in Politics Among Nations, he achieved that goal. Morgenthau stressed that the struggle for power was inescapable. All states sought to keep, increase, or demonstrate their strength. Disarmament, collective security, and international law would not contain this struggle. Only a balance of power could prevent countries from attempting to overthrow the existing order. Power could be tamed only by countervailing power.

It is easy to see why many misread Morgenthau as a champion of realpolitik—an heir to the likes of Machiavelli or Bismarck. After all, he placed power at the center of his analysis. But he never romanticized it. As he wrote in a private letter, “I am an analyst of the nation state and of power and have emphasized time and again their negative moral connotations.” Morgenthau’s personal encounters with power had taught him that its unbridled pursuit led to calamity. He understood that normative and moral limitations were required to keep the struggle for power within tolerable bounds. As he wrote in another letter, “It is the task of civilized societies to temper the power drives and direct them into socially useful channels.”

Indeed, Morgenthau had less tolerance for those who failed to reconcile power with morality than for those who underestimated power’s importance. Although President Woodrow Wilson’s universalism and idealism made him one of Morgenthau’s favorite targets, he preferred him to a Machiavellian prince: “Wilson’s failure was at least the failure of greatness, of too great a vision. Nobody will say as much for the epigones of Machiavelli.” He similarly criticized the British historian and political theorist E. H. Carr, whose moral relativism had led him to advocate for appeasing Hitler. Carr, he wrote, had “no transcendent point of view from which to survey the political scene and to appraise the phenomenon of power.” While both men are seen as pivotal realist thinkers, Morgenthau witheringly concluded of Carr that “it is a dangerous thing to be a Machiavelli. It is a disastrous thing to be a Machiavelli without virtù”—that is, it is ruinous to practice power politics without skill and judgment.

In Politics Among Nations, Morgenthau took issue with leaders who both glorify the struggle for power and reject any limits on their own use of power. He argued that “philosophies and political systems which have made the lust and the struggle for power their mainstay have proved impotent and self-destructive.” And he drew attention to common moral standards that could sustain order and moderate the struggle for power. He observed that throughout history, humans insisted on subjecting political action to ethical scrutiny. When such restraints held, countries did less than their power enabled them to do; once commonplace practices, such as assassinating a rival country’s leaders or killing a large portion of its civilian population to reduce its strength, became stigmatized. Morgenthau concluded, “What has changed is the influence of civilization which makes what is desirable and feasible ethically reprehensible and, hence, normally impossible of execution.”

In the immediate aftermath of World War II, however, he believed that the limitations on the struggle for power had reached their weakest point since the advent of the modern state system. Hitler had attempted to extinguish a whole people, and both the Axis and the Allied powers had bombed cities indiscriminately. Although Morgenthau believed the Allies were morally justified in taking such actions to defeat a genocidal regime, he nonetheless feared that the struggle for power was on its way to becoming boundless. In the nuclear age, failing to reestablish these moral barriers could mean the end of humanity.

Morgenthau’s hard-nosed reputation has long obscured the moral core of his realism. He was unequivocal that power must be a means to an end—“an attempt to realize moral values through the medium of politics.” He recognized that maximizing power for its own sake was both destructive and pointless, arguing that “a nation must pursue its interests for the sake of a transcendent purpose which gives meaning to the day-to-day operations of its foreign policy.” In his view, ancient Greece and Rome are remembered because their power served a purpose that outlived their empires: Athenian democracy and philosophy, and Roman law and republican government.

As he assimilated and raised a family in Chicago, Morgenthau came to see the United States as the inheritor of this tradition. The country’s contribution to the world and the source of its power lay in its democracy and civic life. A realist approach—one that prudently wielded power abroad—was best suited to protect the American experiment at home, enabling Americans to live in freedom and security while offering an example for other countries to emulate. In other words, American power had to be bound to purpose—a purpose the country fulfilled “as a light to attract strangers, not as a flame to be spread throughout the world.” This was not merely the observation of a dispassionate scholar. The United States had accepted Morgenthau when no other country would, and it was a place where immigrants like him could rise to the top of their fields.

By the 1960s, Morgenthau saw that American power faced a threat of its own making. He began to develop deep misgivings about U.S. intervention in Vietnam, warning as early as 1961 that it could become a quagmire reminiscent of France’s war in Algeria. Two years later, as clashes between the South Vietnamese government and protesters intensified, Morgenthau urged the United States to get out. He built his opposition on realist grounds, arguing that a communist takeover of Vietnam would not affect the balance of power in Asia, thus rejecting the domino theory, which held that communism would spread inexorably outward once it gained a regional foothold. Morgenthau also recognized that communism was no longer monolithic and believed that Ho Chi Minh could lead a nonaligned country as Tito did in Yugoslavia. He saw the conflict as a civil war and worried that the United States was attempting to maintain a status quo that was itself the source of instability.

Successive administrations failed to heed Morgenthau’s warnings and instead plunged the United States deeper into the war. He came to resent American policymakers’ misappropriation of his lessons on power and entered public debate with new vigor. At a packed rally in New York’s Madison Square Garden in June 1965, he warned that a guerrilla war could be won only through the indiscriminate killing of the South Vietnamese people, which would tear at the moral fiber of the United States. Later that month, Morgenthau debated President Lyndon Johnson’s national security adviser, McGeorge Bundy, on national television. He led with his moral outrage: “I am opposed to our present policy in Vietnam on moral, military, political, and general intellectual grounds.”

The greatest risk of the war, Morgenthau discerned, was “the risk to ourselves, to our mission in the world, to our very existence as a distinct nation.” He lamented, “Here is the last best hope of the downtrodden and the enslaved, to which men of good will throughout the world have looked as a shining example, relieving its frustration in blind ideological fury and aimless destructiveness upon a helpless people.” Vietnam, in his telling, had corrupted the very purpose that gave American power its meaning.

The Vietnam War validated Morgenthau’s belief that power untethered from questions of morality would lead to overreach. It proved that the United States had an interest in constraining itself, if only to avoid disaster. More fundamentally, Vietnam showed that misadventures abroad could boomerang, weakening American democracy and societal cohesion and, ultimately, undermining the country’s international standing.

The second Trump administration’s foreign policy record has thrown these insights into sharp relief. Its officials view questions of morality as a costly sideshow, the remit of woolly-minded idealists who do not understand how the world works. The White House has time and again resorted to military force, striking boats in the Caribbean, kidnapping Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro, blockading Cuba, and waging war on Iran. It has also threatened military action against Canada and Denmark, two of the country’s long-standing allies.

American democracy, far from serving as “a light to attract strangers,” is fast becoming a cautionary tale. The second Trump administration has undermined the foundations of American power through attacks on the courts, the press, and universities. It has gleefully bulldozed through any checks on its writ. Its assault on institutions and its aggressive foreign policy are intertwined; the administration not only failed to seek congressional authorization for the war in Iran, but it also refused to even make the case to the American people before striking.

President Donald Trump has justified his aggression on the basis of the “iron laws that have always determined global power.” Morgenthau would not have recognized this as realism. He contended that “superior power gives no right, either moral or legal, to do with that power all that it is physically capable of doing.” The Trump administration, by contrast, seems to believe that to not use the power at its disposal is to waste it. Morgenthau’s verdict on Carr applies equally to Trump: he is a “Machiavelli without virtù,” playing power politics without judgment and leading his country to disaster.

Morgenthau would argue that the Trump administration misunderstands the very concept of power, which should not be equated with military might and must instead be understood as a complex psychological relationship. Real power, Morgenthau insisted, lies in the ability to influence a country through consent, not coercion. In this view, the use of force signifies the abdication of political power rather than its culmination. The Trump administration’s belligerence betrays its weakness: its acts of aggression around the world are confessions of its inability to shape events through other means.

Although Morgenthau grew up on the statecraft of Bismarck, he came to admire Abraham Lincoln above all others, even attempting to write a book about him toward the end of his life. Morgenthau saw in Lincoln a rare leader who was never possessed by power, instead possessing it and, ultimately, subordinating it to transcendent values. Today, just as at the height of the Vietnam War, Morgenthau would see his task as restoring morality and ethics to their rightful place and highlighting the need for power to move the world toward justice.

The United States has lost its purpose, and unless it rediscovers it, discrete foreign policy successes will be irrelevant. As Morgenthau wrote, if the country loses its soul, any victories are merely “footnotes to the history of the decline and fall of a once great nation.”

DAVID SACKS is Senior Fellow for Asia Studies at the Council on Foreign Relations and the author of the forthcoming book The Realist: Hans Morgenthau and the Purpose of American Power.

#6020. House GOP erupts at Stephen Miller as Trump elevates him after midterm massacre: 'Megalomaniac'

Fox News senior White House correspondent Peter Doocy reports on President Donald Trump's announcement that Stephen Miller will replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff on ‘America Reports.’

Several Republican lawmakers are fuming over President Donald Trump’s elevation of Stephen Miller to be his chief of staff.

"Horrible decision," Rep. Marlin Stutzman, R-Ind., told Fox News Digital Friday after Trump announced his decision.

A Republican lawmaker who was granted anonymity to speak candidly described being in "utter disbelief."

Tensions were rising against Miller in the wake of Republicans’ terrible performance in Tuesday’s midterm elections. Multiple House Republicans who spoke with Fox News Digital just moments before his promotion said Miller, who spearheads Trump’s immigration policy, should be fired.

TRUMP NAMES STEPHEN MILLER NEW WHITE HOUSE CHIEF OF STAFF, REPLACING SUSIE WILES

White House deputy chief of staff for policy Stephen Miller outside the White House Oct. 6, 2025. (Aaron Schwartz/CNP/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

When later asked about his promotion, one House Republican who said Miller should be fired replied simply with a facepalm emoji.

Republicans suffered historic losses that saw both the House and Senate fall into Democrats’ hands in the new Congress. Democrats are on pace to win more than 250 seats in the House and at least 54 in the Senate.

AMERICA SAW THE BLUE WAVE COMING, BUT DRASTICALLY UNDERESTIMATED THE EXTENT OF THE DEM TSUNAMI

And while voter analyses have shown that Trump’s war in Iran and tariffs played the biggest roles in turning people away from the GOP, his handling of illegal immigration served to alienate voters in key areas. That’s in no small part due to Hispanic and Latino voters turning their backs on Republicans in places like Texas that shifted massively toward Democrats in this election cycle.

"Because of his immigration positions, I just think we ruined a lot of our goodwill with Latino voters," Stutzman said.

The second GOP lawmaker told Fox News Digital that Miller ignored multiple warnings from House Republicans in the past, arguing that Trump’s immigration crackdown is what voters asked for.

"I've been in several group meetings where a lot of us have told him, ‘Your immigration enforcement policies are backfiring back home. Our people are mad, our businesses are mad — even in deep red districts — and it is impacting not just the voters, but the donors, and everybody top to bottom," that GOP lawmaker said.

"But it looked to me like Stephen Miller was only after numbers. … He would not listen to us."

Other Republicans who spoke with Fox News Digital also criticized Miller but did not go as far as to say he should be fired.

Rep. Marlin Stutzman, R-Ind., attends a House Financial Services Committee hearing May 7, 2025. (CQ-Roll Call, Inc. via Getty Images)

REPUBLICANS TURN ON TRUMP AFTER DEVASTATING MIDTERM LOSSES HAND DEMOCRATS CONTROL OF CONGRESS

"I think he's greatly hurt the administration and cost us many seats on Tuesday," retiring Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., told Fox News Digital before news of Miller’s promotion broke.

"He took an immigration policy that favored the president 2:1 in the public, over-reached and made it 2:1 unfavorable.

Miller is widely known as Trump’s right hand on immigration policy, including earlier this year when the administration faced bipartisan backlash over its handling of anti-immigration enforcement protests in places like Minneapolis.

Several Republicans, particularly those running in battleground districts, had called for Trump to limit deportations to illegal immigrants who have committed crimes while in the United States.

A fourth GOP lawmaker said, "Stephen is a megalomaniac who should have never been given the keys to the Ferrari. That being said, he's not the reason why we lost. ... No one knows who Stephen Miller is in our districts."

Various other administration officials also got their share of blame in the fallout from Tuesday’s midterm massacre, including Secretary of War Pete Hegseth and Trump himself.

But the president and people in his orbit have blamed candidate quality, arguing that, in many cases, the president campaigned harder than some of the people he supported did for themselves.

And in his Truth Social post announcing Miller’s promotion, Trump called him one of his "most trusted advisors." He later told reporters Miller would "do a great job."

Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., arrives at a House Republican Conference meeting in the U.S. Capitol Building July 21, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

When Fox News Digital reached the White House for comment, spokeswoman Lauren Bis said Miller "has been a key player in delivering on President Trump’s America First agenda since day one."

"He has spearheaded the administration’s crime crackdown, including in D.C. and Memphis; record-setting deregulation; unleashed American energy; dismantled drug cartels; and secured our border. In his new role, he will continue to deliver historic successes for the American people," Bis said.

Bis also pointed to a list of positive comments about Miller by administration officials and members of Congress.

"Very grateful to Susie for her friendship and service to the president and our country. Stephen Miller is an amazing patriot and will do great things. Congrats to him on a well deserved promotion!" Vice President JD Vance said.

"Stephen Miller has built a proven record focused on securing our border, combating crime and advancing a now much-needed commonsense conservative agenda for our country," House Freedom Caucus Chairman Andy Harris said.

A White House official told Fox News Digital Miller had experience working with Democrats on the D.C. Safe and Beautiful Task Force and that he has been a "valuable, trusted aide" since 2016.

#6018. California prosecutors slam Newsom's death row commutations, accuse governor of putting 'politics over people'

San Luis Obispo County District Attorney Dan Dow accused Gov. Gavin Newsom of ignoring crime victims and violating the will of California voters following a sweeping announcement on death row commutations.

District attorneys and law enforcement unions across California have publicly condemned Gov. Gavin Newsom following his announcement to commute the sentences of 548 death row inmates, a decision one prosecutor called a slap in the face to victims' families.

Newsom’s sweeping order to commute the condemned inmates to life in prison without the possibility of parole has drawn intense criticism alongside praise from anti-death penalty advocates.

"I think it's all about politics, sadly. Politics is over people in this case, and that's a real sad state of affairs," San Luis Obispo County District Attorney Dan Dow told Fox News Digital.

ALABAMA GOVERNOR COMMUTES DEATH SENTENCE AS DOUBTS EMERGE ABOUT EVIDENCE IN MURDER CONVICTION

Law enforcement officials and prosecutors have criticized Gov. Gavin Newsom for issuing a blanket commutation for all of California's death row inmates. (Jane Tyska/Digital First Media/East Bay Times via Getty Images/Frazer Harrison/WireImage)

In an open letter detailing his decision, Newsom said California's capital punishment system is fundamentally at odds with the state's principles.

"After nearly 30 years in public office, I have come to understand the limits of what government can justly accomplish," Newsom wrote. "I take this action because I firmly believe that state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California’s values, undermines the foundations of our legal system, and diminishes each of us."

POPE LEO DENOUNCES DEATH PENALTY AS 'INADMISSIBLE' AHEAD OF PENTAGON'S PLANNED LIVESTREAMED EXECUTION

Upon taking office in 2019, Newsom issued an executive moratorium on capital punishment. California has not carried out an execution since 2006.

The backlash to the governor's announcement was immediate.

FLORIDA PUTS 77-YEAR-OLD MAN TO DEATH FOR DEPUTY'S MURDER AS STATE CLOSES IN ON EXECUTION RECORD

"Justice will not have been served for these victims, dozens of them in San Diego County," said Lt. Jared Wilson, president of the San Diego Police Officers Association.

"Their last memories were of these evil, evil men who took their lives. What Gavin Newsom has done is awful."

Lt. Sam Robinson calls to open a door at San Quentin State Prison's death row in San Quentin, Calif., Aug. 15, 2016. The prison is California's oldest penitentiary and houses the state's only death row for men with 700 condemned inmates. (Justin Sullivan/Getty Images)

Wilson and other law enforcement officials strongly criticized the blanket clemency, citing cases like that of Jesse Michael Gomez, who was sentenced to death for the 2016 murder of San Diego police officer Jonathan "J.D." De Guzman.

"Californians have been asked on two separate ballot initiatives to abolish the death penalty, and both times they have chosen to keep it for the most serious crimes," said Los Angeles County District Attorney Nathan Hochman.

"Until the law is changed, my office will continue to evaluate every death penalty-eligible case with the rigor, care and restraint these decisions demand."

NEW YORK BILLS COULD ABOLISH LIFE WITHOUT PAROLE FOR SERIAL KILLERS AND COP KILLERS, CRITICS WARN

Orange County District Attorney Todd Spitzer called the commutations "mercy for the wolves … while inflicting the ultimate act of cruelty on the sheep," saying voters in California have upheld the death penalty in recent years.

Riverside County Sheriff Chad Bianco, who previously ran for governor, noted that 127 inmates on California’s death row were sentenced for crimes committed in his county.

"These sentences were not handed down without due process," he said. "They followed extensive investigations, lengthy trials, evidence presented in court and decisions made by juries and judges.

"Yet, with one sweeping executive decision, the governor has chosen to override those outcomes, disregarding the voices of victims’ families, the work of prosecutors and the pursuit of justice that took years to achieve."

College students Rachel Newhouse and Aundria Crawford were killed separately. Their killer, Rex Krebs, was sentenced to death for the brutal slayings. (San Luis Obispo County District Attorney)

Among the 548 inmates whose sentences were commuted are two men convicted in San Luis Obispo County: Michael Whisenhunt, 61, who was sentenced to death for the 1995 torture and murder of his girlfriend's 1-year-old daughter, Kesha Gurke; and Rex Krebs, 60, who was convicted in the brutal slayings of two female college students.

CHARLES MANSON CULT FOLLOWER BRUCE DAVIS, CONVICTED IN TWO 1969 KILLINGS, DIES BEHIND BARS AT 83

Dow noted that families of the victims were neither consulted nor notified prior to Newsom's public announcement.

"I was just sickened, frankly, for the crime victims in California who were slapped in the face by our governor," Dow said.

JUDGE BLOCKS ALABAMA'S NITROGEN GAS EXECUTION METHOD, RULES IT IS UNCONSTITUTIONALLY CRUEL

Addressing arguments from anti-death penalty groups who contend that capital punishment is inherently flawed and racially biased, Dow defended the current legal framework.

"We have a rigorous defense procedure that weeds out cases. I have strong confidence in the integrity of our convictions," he said.

#6016. Democrats Shouldn’t Squander Their Chance to Launch a Foreign Policy Revolution

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MATTHEW DUSS is Executive Vice President of the Center for International Policy. From 2017 to 2022, he served as Chief Foreign Policy Adviser to Senator Bernie Sanders.

The Imperative of Ambition in a New Congress

Tuesday’s election made clear that endless wars, political corruption, and the high cost of living are three of the American public’s top concerns. They are deeply interrelated—and the new Congress must address all three. That will require a true foreign policy revolution that grapples not just with the outrages and failures of the Trump administration’s foreign policy but also with the ways in which past Democratic or Democrat-supported foreign policies have contributed to today’s crisis. There will be a temptation among some Democratic leaders, in Congress and beyond, to limit the scope or pace of change. But to find a way out of today’s crisis and move beyond the failures that have afflicted American foreign policy for the past several decades, Democrats will need to have the courage to face a hard truth: that President Donald Trump’s abuses of power and unilateral warmaking are not so much a break from Washington’s militarist mindset as they are a particularly aggressive, predatory expression of it.

The incoming Democratic majority will face a national security environment in which the most severe challenges to U.S. safety and prosperity come from within. That’s why Congress should shut down the Iran war immediately but also recognize that it can make the United States more secure only if it pairs a foreign policy shift with a frontal assault on spiraling costs and political corruption. Political polarization; government corruption and dysfunction; increasing dominance of political and economic life by a small handful of mercurial oligarchs; and a president who recklessly and unilaterally launches unauthorized military interventions while relentlessly seeking opportunities to enrich himself, his family, and his cronies—all pose more immediate danger to the safety and prosperity of the American people than does any foreign enemy.

When Democrats take control of Congress in January, they will have considerable power to challenge the corruption and malfeasance of the Trump administration. Control of committees and of the congressional calendar will provide the opportunity for sorely needed oversight. But although the Trump administration deserves aggressive investigation, Democrats must aim higher—and use the opportunity to articulate a genuinely compelling alternative for American foreign policy.

The first and most urgent task will be ending the Iran war, a reckless and unpopular conflict that has become even less popular as Americans have felt its effects on their wallets. Congress must respond to widespread bipartisan anger among voters at Trump’s war and abuse of war powers by using all the tools it has, especially its power to cut funding. It has been encouraging to see Democrats continuing to oppose any supplemental budget request by the administration for the war. It is an area where Democrats have gotten tripped up in the past when they’ve been afraid of accusations that they don’t “support the troops” if they oppose funding for foolish, counterproductive wars. With Democrats having held firm against any additional funding for the war, those days appear to be gone. That is good.

But Democrats need to go further. They should be prepared to oppose all funding for the military, including blocking votes on the National Defense Authorization Act, the annual bill setting out the broad scope and budget for the Defense Department, in order to put an end to hostilities in Iran. In fact, not only should they be willing to withhold funding from the Pentagon, but they should also be willing to withhold funding for the entire government, and threaten another government shutdown if the bill does not prohibit any funding for Trump’s war. December 11 is the deadline for a continuing resolution to keep the government funded and operating. If ever there was a reason for Democrats to threaten to withhold their votes, stopping a blatantly illegal and globally destructive war that American voters have now resoundingly rebuked is it.

Democrats must also be willing to take a hard look at the continuity between Trump’s use of military violence and past uses of force by administrations of both parties, and the bipartisan support for that violence. The groundwork for the Iran war was laid by the Biden administration’s unstinting support for Israel’s genocide in Gaza (as even some former administration officials have acknowledged), but also by years of U.S. refusal to impose any consequences on the Israeli government despite clear evidence of ongoing violations of human rights and international law. Trump’s strikes on alleged “narcoterrorist” drug boats in the Caribbean represent the logical evolution of long-standing treatment of the United States’ drug problem as a “war,” and of casually applying the label of “terrorist” to justify any and all measures, including execution without due process. Seeking accountability for these abuses will require acknowledging the past complicity of both Republicans and Democrats.

Democrats must also take on corruption, a key node connecting the country’s domestic and foreign policy crises. In doing so, they will be walking through an open door. Polls consistently show that political corruption is high on Americans’ list of concerns. According to a recent Harris poll conducted for The Guardian, “Nearly nine out of 10 Americans said that government corruption, as defined as elected officials putting their own or their donors’ financial interests ahead of the public’s, is a problem.” A mid-October CNBC All-America Economic Survey found that voters are more concerned about government corruption than any other issue, including high grocery and gas prices. In September, Gallup reported that “Americans’ perceptions of government corruption in the U.S. are at their highest level in 20 years, with 89% of U.S. adults saying it is widespread.”

To say that the Trump administration provides a target-rich environment for a strong anticorruption push is to commit felony understatement. Exposing foreign gifts to Trump and conflicts of interest by family members, contracts awarded to Trump’s oligarch donors and new tech buddies, and the selling of pardons, as well as restoring enforcement of the Corporate Transparency Act, the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act, and related task forces, should all be part of the plan. Democrats should drill down into Trump’s wildly corrupt “Board of Peace” for Gaza, which U.S. Representative Jamie Raskin, the Maryland Democrat set to chair the House Judiciary Committee, rightly referred to as a “global slush fund.” The obvious conflicts of interests of Trump’s diplomatic envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner also demand investigation.

The good news is that effective anticorruption does not necessarily require new legislation; it merely requires that existing legislation be enforced. An August 2025 essay in Foreign Affairs, co-authored with my colleagues Casey Michel and Trevor Sutton, laid out a more expansive case for Democrats putting anticorruption at the center of their foreign policy platform. (A recent essay by Casey Michel lays out a more detailed agenda for the new Congress.)

Ultimately, the most important step toward tackling corruption will be reforming the campaign finance system. The United States is an outlier among developed democracies in that its system of campaign finance allows for what is essentially legalized bribery. Legislators speak openly about campaign contributors expecting “returns on investment” for their political donations. This is an outrage. In recent years, foreign actors have increasingly begun bankrolling American legislators, taking advantage of massive loopholes in campaign finance laws and the Federal Election Commission’s lack of enforcement.

Confronting this will be no small task. At a minimum, Congress must move to legislate a response to the Supreme Court’s 2010 Citizens United decision, which effectively greenlighted bottomless campaign funding from American corporations. It must also reform the Federal Election Commission, providing the body with much-needed resources to investigate campaign finance crimes and restoring the practice of allowing congressional appointments to the commission, which has become little more than an appendage of the executive branch.

All of the above steps must be connected to the top concern among American voters at this moment—the high cost of living. Democrats have disagreements in several policy areas, particularly in foreign policy. Their coalition is diverse. But there is consensus around addressing Americans’ material needs urgently and aggressively. Such an approach would confront Trump’s reckless use of tariffs (while recognizing that the neoliberal taboo on tariffs is a thing of the past), rising gas prices because of the Iran war, the need to restore the global minimum tax, the threat of job losses to AI, and the increased power of tech oligarchs over Americans’ economic and political lives, including on foreign policy, as Elon Musk’s restriction of Ukraine’s Starlink connection demonstrated. The approach should involve a mix of tactics, including hearings, oversight investigations, and legislative proposals.

Many of these efforts may not produce new laws in the next two years. But they could become the backbone of a winning agenda for Congress and for a Democratic administration in 2029. Most important, they would represent a step toward the forging of a new American political consensus on which a new and durable foreign policy can be built.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

How It Can Reclaim Its Constitutional Powers

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

Weak Institutions—Not Private Fortunes—Deform Democracy

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

The Imperative of Ambition in a New Congress

How Long Will Elites Stay Loyal to Kim Jong Un?

Weak Institutions—Not Private Fortunes—Deform Democracy

#6014. Republican state lawmakers reported Omar to the IRS in 2019

Chad Pergram reports on Capitol Hill regarding the reopened investigation into Minnesota Rep. Ilhan Omar's 2009 marriage to Ahmed Elmi.

EXCLUSIVE: A group of Minnesota Republican state lawmakers asked the IRS in August 2019 to "conduct a detailed reexamination" of tax filings by Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., based on a report from a state board suggesting she filed joint tax returns in 2014 and 2015 with a man she was not legally married to, according to a letter obtained by Fox News Digital.

The letter was sent after the Minnesota Campaign Finance and Public Disclosure Board issued a report on June 6, 2019, indicating that Omar had filed joint tax returns in 2014 and 2015 with Ahmed Hirsi, despite being legally married to another man, Ahmed Nur Said Elmi, at the time.

The joint tax returns came up during the Minnesota Campaign Finance and Public Disclosure Board’s examination of a $1,500 expense associated with Omar’s campaign committee. The board was investigating whether the expenditure was a legitimate campaign-related expense or whether campaign funds had been used to pay for services that benefited Omar personally. The payment was part of a larger $2,250 reimbursement to the Kjellberg Law Office for legal and other services.

In 2019, a group of Minnesota Republican state lawmakers asked the IRS to investigate tax filings by Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn. (Adam Gray/AP Photo)

ILHAN OMAR ISSUES STUNNING ADMISSION WHEN CONFRONTED WITH BROTHER MARRIAGE RUMORS

In response to the board’s questions, Omar’s campaign committee explained that the Kjellberg Law Office had retained the accounting firm Frederick & Rosen to obtain and review financial records as part of the campaign committee’s efforts to respond to allegations surrounding Omar’s marital history. According to the committee’s explanation, Frederick & Rosen prepared releases for Omar and Ahmed Hirsi to sign, so the firm could obtain copies of their filed joint federal income tax returns for 2014 and 2015 from the IRS.

Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., continues to generate headlines related to her finances. (Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call, Inc. via Getty Images)

The board concluded that the committee had not demonstrated that the $1,500 expense benefited the campaign and required Omar to reimburse the committee.

JD VANCE SAYS DOJ LOOKING INTO IF ILHAN OMAR COMMITTED IMMIGRATION FRAUD AMID BROTHER-MARRIAGE ALLEGATIONS

Omar married Hirsi in 2002 in their Muslim faith tradition and that they ended their relationship in that tradition in 2008, Newsweek reported. She then legally married Ahmed Nur Said Elmi in February 2009, according to public records, as reported by Newsweek.

According to divorce documents obtained by Fox News Digital, Omar legally divorced Elmi in December 2017.

Carla Kjellberg, a lawyer for Omar and her campaign committee, stated during the board proceedings that Frederick & Rosen’s services provided "some personal benefit to Rep. Omar," because "there was an issue with her tax returns that needed to be corrected regardless of her status as a candidate," according to the board’s report.

In a letter to then-IRS Commissioner Charles Rettig dated Aug. 6, 2019, the state lawmakers asked the IRS to undertake a review in light of the Minnesota Campaign Finance Board report.

"Due to recent developments, I believe probable cause exists for an investigation into Rep. Omar's tax filings," read the letter, signed by Minnesota state Reps. Steve Drazkowski, Linda Runbeck, Glenn Gruenhagen, Shane Mekeland, John Poston, Eric Lucero, Duane Quam, Tim Miller, Cal Bahr and Jeremy Munson.

Drazkowski told Fox News Digital that the IRS never responded to the request.

The IRS did not respond to Fox News Digital's request for comment. Fox News Digital could not confirm whether the IRS followed through with the lawmakers’ request.

Federal tax confidentiality rules generally restrict what the agency can disclose about individual taxpayers. The Minnesota Campaign Finance and Public Disclosure Board issued its findings on June 6, 2019, following two complaints filed by Drazkowski. Although the board required Omar to repay the $1,500 that her campaign had paid the Kjellberg Law Firm for Frederick & Rosen’s services related to the tax returns, it rejected the allegation that this money was payment for Omar’s personal divorce proceeding, in which Kjellberg also represented her.

The board also required Omar to repay campaign funds used for certain travel expenses, including trips to Boston, New York and Washington, D.C.

Following the board's 2019 findings, Omar said she was "glad" the process was complete and welcomed the board's determination regarding allegations that she had improperly used campaign funds for personal expenses. However, she did not address the portion of the board’s report concerning the joint tax returns she submitted with Hirsi.

Omar did not respond to Fox News Digital’s request for comment.

In a separate letter obtained by Fox News Digital, the Republican state lawmakers, led by Drazkowski, asked Cynthia Bauerly, then-commissioner of the Minnesota Department of Revenue, in August 2019 to review Omar's joint tax filings.

Drazkowski said neither Bauerly nor the Minnesota Department of Revenue responded to the lawmakers' request.

The Minnesota Department of Revenue did not respond to Fox News Digital's request for comment.

The letter from the lawmakers urging the state and federal tax reporting agencies to investigate Omar’s joint tax filings came two months after Drazkowski urged Rep. Angie Craig, D-Minn., to refer his concerns about Omar's tax filings to the House Ethics Committee.

As previously reported by Fox News Digital, Craig told Drazkowski in a letter that he could submit information himself to the Office of Congressional Ethics (OCE), which was renamed to the Office of Congressional Conduct in 2021.

Ilhan Omar with Ahmed Hirsi in 2018. (Kerem Yucel/AFP via Getty Images)

The congressional ethics process allows members of the public and lawmakers to submit information concerning alleged misconduct, although the independent ethics office and the House Committee on Ethics have separate procedures.

In late October, Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said on Fox News that the federal investigation into Omar regarding potential immigration and marriage fraud had been referred to the Department of Justice (DOJ) as a "large case" that targets multiple individuals rather than just one person.

The DHS did not respond to Fox News Digital's request for comment on whether the investigation is looking into the 2014 and 2015 joint tax filings flagged by the state lawmakers in 2019.
