#6011. Newsom’s death-row commutations spark DA revolt as prosecutors warn of legal fight

,Former DOJ prosecutor Jim Trusty reacts to Gov. Gavin Newsom's plan to commute hundreds of death sentences, criticism from victims' families and questions about the decision's timing. He also weighs in on the federal investigations into Rep. Ilhan Omar's fraud allegeations.

At least a dozen California district attorneys are publicly condemning Gov. Gavin Newsom’s plan to commute all 548 death sentences in the state, arguing the sweeping move overrides juries, disregards victims’ families and defies the will of state voters.

Newsom announced the plan Thursday, saying state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California’s values. The move would convert the sentences to life without parole, though 382 cases still require approval from at least four California Supreme Court justices, according to the Associated Press.

In response, prosecuting attorneys across California have raised concerns about victims’ rights, the governor’s constitutional authority and the role of the California Supreme Court in reviewing hundreds of the commutations.

"Justice requires case-by-case consideration, not one-size-fits-all decisions," Sacramento County District Attorney Thien Ho said in response to Newsom's action.

THREE STATES CARRY OUT EXECUTIONS ON SAME DAY AMID RENEWED DEATH PENALTY DEBATE

A table fitted with restraints holds down death row prisoners while they are given a lethal injection at Huntsville Unit (Walls Unit) in Huntsville, Texas. (Greg Smith/CORBIS/Corbis via Getty Images)

Orange County District Attorney Todd Spitzer described the move as "mercy for the wolves" that inflicts "the ultimate act of cruelty on the sheep."

Spitzer noted that regulations implemented by California’s Board of Parole Hearings on Oct. 1 allow thousands of prisoners serving life without parole to become eligible for parole, arguing that Newsom's latest death row commutations could put these inmates "one step closer" to release. Newsom’s office says, however, that the affected prisoners will remain in prison and are not eligible for release.

"The Governor is attempting to appear courageous with this decision. He is not courageous. His commutations are solely based upon power and political aspirations," added San Bernardino County District Attorney Jason Anderson, who accused Newsom of caring more about politics than people. "Courage would require speaking with crime victims and the surviving family members of the people murdered by these death row inmates."

CALIFORNIA SUED OVER POLICY THAT COULD FREE SERIAL KILLERS AS LACI PETERSON'S FRIEND SOUNDS ALARM

Anderson cited the case of Kevin Cooper, a California death-row inmate convicted of killing Josh Ryen’s parents and sister, along with Ryen’s friend, Chris Hughes, and leaving Ryen for dead.

Anderson said Newsom had not contacted Ryen or Hughes’ mother, Mary Ann Hughes, to ask their views on a possible commutation — and that Ryen has never received a call from the governor.

"Courage would require the Governor to follow Marsy’s Law, California’s constitutional rights belonging to victims of crime," Anderson continued. "He has not. I am not aware of any victims’ family members receiving notice of these commutations from the Governor."

Meanwhile, Anderson and several other California prosecutors who spoke out against Newsom's death row commutations also framed the dispute as a challenge to the jury system and an override of the will of California voters. They said jurors in each case heard the evidence, weighed all the necessary factors and decided whether death was warranted, with judges and appellate courts later reviewing those verdicts. Newsom’s blanket action, they argue, substitutes one statewide decision for hundreds of individualized judgments.

NEW YORK BILLS COULD ABOLISH LIFE WITHOUT PAROLE FOR SERIAL KILLERS AND COP KILLERS, CRITICS WARN

San Bernardino County District Attorney Jason Anderson answers questions during a news conference outside the San Bernardino County Sheriff's Department in Rancho Cucamonga on Friday, April 10, 2026. (Anjali Sharif-Paul, The Sun/SCNG)

"A single order commuting all of them would set aside hundreds of individualized judgments without examining the facts of even one," San Luis Obispo County District Attorney Dan Dow said.

"It invalidates the exhausting, emotionally wrenching work of our citizens who sat on juries, weighed horrific evidence, and delivered lawful verdicts. Most unforgivably, it is a slap in the face to victims and their families," said San Joaquin District Attorney Ron Freitas. "This decision does not bind wounds; it tears them wide open, all over again."

The prosecutors pointed out that California voters rejected death-penalty repeal initiatives in 2012 and 2016 while approving Proposition 66 in 2016, which changed procedures for challenging death sentences and was promoted by supporters as a way to speed up the process. The district attorneys argued Newsom’s blanket action overrides those ballot-box decisions without repealing the law.

"A blanket commutation is a mass erasure of justice. It overrides the will of California voters who have repeatedly defended capital punishment at the ballot box," Freitas added in his response to Newsom's action.

A correctional facility is shown in an undated file photo as California district attorneys condemn Gov. Gavin Newsom’s plan to commute all 548 state death sentences. (Getty Images/iStockphoto)

Some California prosecutors who spoke out against Newsom's controversial move signaled that the dispute may not end with public statements. San Diego County District Attorney Summer Stephan said her office was "researching any lawful remedies" that would allow victims’ voices to be heard and considered, while Kern County District Attorney Cynthia Zimmer was more direct, saying, "This assault on justice will be challenged."

Los Angeles County District Attorney Nathan Hochman acknowledged that commuting existing sentences falls within the governor’s constitutional authority, but said, "That power does not extend to eliminating the death penalty. Only the Legislature or the voters can do that." Newsom’s clemency announcement clarifies that it "does not repeal the death penalty" and is limited to commuting California’s current death sentences.

Fox News Digital reached out to Newsom's office for comment but did not hear back in time for publication.

Alec Schemmel is a Politics Reporter for Fox News Digital who is originally from Charlotte, North Carolina but now resides in the Washington D.C.-Baltimore Metro Area.

#6007. Biden-appointed judge probes whether ICE skirted judge's release orders in Colorado cases

DHS Secretary Markwayne Mullin told Fox News Digital that Biden-appointed judges are "almost impossible" to work with as immigration enforcement battles intensify.

A Biden-appointed federal judge ordered ICE's Denver field office director to appear in court Monday amid concerns that immigration authorities violated judicial release orders by immediately re-arresting detainees, including at least one instance outside a detention facility.

Nathan Rasper has been summoned as part of an inquiry involving 14 people who were re-arrested after being released from immigration detention. The Department of Homeland Security (DHS) maintains that ICE complied with lawful court orders and that a judicial release order does not permanently shield individuals from immigration enforcement.

"When an illegal alien is ordered released by a judge, that does not immunize the alien from immigration enforcement for all time," DHS said in a statement to Fox News Digital. "ICE may seek to re-arrest the alien in a way that complies with the judicial decision. To suggest that ICE cannot do this is nothing short of sabotage."

The Nov. 5 order issued by U.S. District Judge Charlotte Sweeney followed an earlier ruling requiring the government to identify every case in Colorado since Oct. 27 in which ICE re-detained someone within 12 hours of a court-ordered release. Federal officials subsequently identified 14 petitioners who had been re-arrested after being released, but the filing did not provide the precise timing of when the re-arrests occurred. She directed officials to provide the exact dates and times of those releases and re-arrests and identify any additional individuals immediately detained following court-ordered releases.

A man holds his immigration paperwork while handcuffed after being detained by Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) agents outside an immigration courtroom, June 17, 2025, at the Jacob K. Javits federal building in New York. (AP)

OBAMA, BIDEN JUDGES DRAW FRESH FIRE AS TRUMP’S IMMIGRATION COURT CLASHES HEAT UP: ‘ALMOST IMPOSSIBLE’

The additional reporting requirements followed a Nov. 4 hearing in which Sweeney questioned whether ICE was circumventing judicial orders by briefly releasing detainees before immediately taking them back into custody. At that hearing, she ordered Rasper to appear at a follow-up hearing Monday to address the agency's practices.

The inquiry stems in part from the case of Ramon Quintero Alvarez, who was released from immigration detention on Oct. 27 before ICE immediately took him back into custody, according to a government court filing.

In a Nov. 3 court filing, federal government attorneys said they would not submit a legal brief defending the lawfulness of Alvarez's continued detention.

The immigrant detention facility operated by the privately owned GEO Group for U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) in Aurora, Colo. (Jim West/UCG/Universal Images Group via Getty Images)

The filing prompted Sweeney to convene the Nov. 4 hearing at which Assistant U.S. Attorneys Andrew Soler and Kevin Traskos appeared alongside ICE Assistant Field Office Director Nichole Black, who testified under oath about the circumstances surrounding Alvarez's re-arrest.

APPEALS COURT BLOCKS TRUMP ADMIN FROM HOLDING MIGRANTS WITHOUT BOND FOR OVER 90 DAYS

At a Nov. 4 hearing, Sweeney ordered Alvarez be released and barred immigration authorities from detaining him again unless the government demonstrated, by clear and convincing evidence at a bond hearing, that he posed a flight risk or danger to the community.

The government must submit the additional information requested by Sweeney by 8 a.m. Monday, ahead of Rasper's scheduled court appearance later that morning. The proceedings could shed light on ICE's compliance with judicial release orders, but Sweeney has not indicated whether she intends to pursue contempt proceedings.

Dozens of immigrants are detained by US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) agents inside the Federal Plaza courthouse in New York City on Thursday, June 26, following their legal proceedings. The arrests, which took place shortly after court appearances, have sparked criticism from immigrant rights advocates, who say courthouse enforcement undermines due process and deters community members from seeking legal protection. (Photo by Lokman Vural Elibol/Anadolu via Getty Images) (Lokman Vural Elibol/Anadolu via Getty Images)

The dispute comes as ICE faces similar legal challenges across Colorado, where other Biden-appointed federal judges, including U.S. District Judges Nina Wang and S. Kato Crews, have presided over cases involving disputes over immigration detention and court-ordered releases.

Fox News Digital reached out to the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the District of Colorado for comment.

#5979. An unexpected activist emerged to help block a $5 billion data center in Maryland

Taylor Frazier McCollum has been active in the opposition to the possible data center at the site of the demolished Landover Mall in Prince George's County, less than a mile from where she lived. If the project is built, the development could occupy more than 80 acres. Tyrone Turner/WAMU for NPR hide caption

The overgrown grass and weeds seem to stretch into the horizon on nearly 90 acres of empty land in Landover, Md. It used to be Landover Mall, but that closed in 2002, and the site has been mostly vacant since.

In 2024, the developer that built the mall won approval from county planners for a $5 billion data center campus, and word began spreading through the largely Black community nearby.

"I was like, 'Oh, hell no,'" said Taylor Frazier McCollum, describing the moment she learned about the project. She lived less than a mile away from it. "Why would you want to bring water-polluting, diesel-generating data centers right down the street from where people live, work, play? It just didn't make sense to me."

Frazier McCollum is one of a number of Americans whose political awakening was triggered by data center development. Across the country, community members have taken to local council hearings to voice their opposition. They've prompted — though estimates vary — about 500 moratoriums on data centers nationwide.

Unlike a lot of land-use activists trying to protect their homes from threats in their backyards, Frazier McCollum does not own a home. In 2024, she and her young daughter were evicted from their apartment. She was working at a waxing center that cut wages, and she could no longer cover her rent.

"It was difficult," Frazier McCollum recalled. "Because I created the ideal life paying for us to live in this apartment and have our own space ... and then to have it taken away ... It felt like something that wasn't ever going to end."

They've moved around and were living in her grandfather's home when she learned about the proposed project less than a mile away.

Taylor Frazier McCollum in front of her grandfather's home, where she lived when she heard about the proposed data center site less than a mile away in Landover, Md. Tyrone Turner/WAMU for NPR hide caption

She created a petition that gathered over 25,000 signatures, leading to widely attended protests and culminating in multiple local moratoriums imposed on data centers throughout the county. It blocked the $5 billion project that was set to break ground between 2026 and 2027.

The fight pushed Frazier McCollum to the forefront of local politics. She went to county meetings, testified before Maryland legislators and endorsed political candidates. She found herself in a coalition of socialists, environmentalists, people angry about their electric bills and neighbors new to organizing.

Local media interviewed her, and people in data center circles called her "the Landover girl."

Staci Hartwell, a longtime local environmental activist, says she spent years struggling to get traction on the data center issue before Frazier McCollum came along.

"This is something that was happening in her neighborhood, and it was really like her awakening," Hartwell said.

But with data centers paused, some in Prince George's County worry about declining tax revenue. Last year, a local Six Flags closed. The NFL's Washington Commanders announced plans to return to neighboring Washington, D.C. Thousands of federal workers have lost their jobs. The county faces a budget deficit.

The possible data center site, which was once the Landover Mall, in Prince George's County. If the project is built, the development will occupy more than 80 acres. Tyrone Turner/WAMU for NPR hide caption

In September, Frazier McCollum appeared at a county council meeting to advocate for a fund for roof repairs, something her grandfather's home needs. Council member Wanika Fisher, who supports moving some data center projects forward, reminded her that the county needs development.

"Our county has an economic future that we have to grapple with," said Fisher, addressing the request. "I don't see where the money is coming from. I think we have to have a real conversation. And I'm not saying that a data center is the answer, but I'm saying it's worth the hard discussion."

With the Landover project on hold, Frazier McCollum now wants a full ban on that and other data center projects. And with grants Hartwell and others have found, Frazier McCollum has taken her message to Black communities fighting data center growth in Atlanta, Memphis and even across the globe in Zambia.

"I feel like so many people are depending on this fight to be successful," Frazier McCollum explained. "Not just for myself, but even across the country, to set precedent for other people that are fighting against hyperscale data centers."

She's lost out on work to attend public meetings or knock on doors, and she's relied on family for rides and childcare.

Her father, Lacy McCollum Jr., sees the data center fight as rooted in civil rights.

"We're talking about the right to water, the right to clean air, the right to a place to live. That's everybody's fight, right?" Lacy McCollum Jr. said. "While I'm afraid for her, we need those people. We needed the Medgar Everses. We needed the Martin Luther Kings. We needed the Malcolm X's. People like Taylor, they jump out here so the people behind them get to make progress."

Some urge her to run for office. She says she wants to pay off her debt and traffic tickets and find a home first.

"I didn't want to have to focus on housing and the campaign and doing everything else," explained Frazier McCollum. "Now is not maybe the moment for it, but I do have aspirations to run for something."

In the meantime, she's pitching plans to turn the old Landover site into a sports complex or affordable housing, or to use some of it to support heritage tourism in the county. But none of those ideas, she acknowledges, would be as lucrative as a data center campus.

#5960. RE+ will host another clean energy job fair this year

Job seekers hoping to launch or advance a career in clean energy can connect with employers and explore new opportunities at the RE+ Clean Energy Job Fair on Nov. 18, at the Las Vegas Convention Center. Hosted by GRID Alternatives, the Women of Renewable Industries and Sustainable Energy (WRISE) and Green Jobs Board, the fair is free for job seekers, and everyone who registers will receive a complimentary pass to the RE+ Expo, North America’s largest clean energy industry event.

The job fair will run from 11 a.m. to 2 p.m. and attendees can connect with hiring companies. The fair is open to people of all skill levels.

“The future of clean energy is about building sustainable careers. When we connect job seekers with employers, we bridge the gap between opportunity and talent, strengthen the workforce, and ensure that the people who need access to these careers have a seat at the table.” said Lyric Darru, grants manager of workforce development at GRID Alternatives. “By connecting job seekers directly with employers and opening the door to the broader RE+ expo at no cost, we’re helping more people explore pathways into clean energy careers and ensuring the benefits of this growing industry reach more communities. We thank our partners and sponsors for their support in bringing this event to Las Vegas.”

In 2025, more than 37,000 people attended RE+, an event with more than 1,300 exhibitors from businesses in solar, energy storage, electric vehicles, grid technologies and other sectors. This year’s conference will take place November 16–19 at the Las Vegas Convention Center.

“WRISE is excited to co-host this important event with GRID again this year,” said Lauren Friedman, senior manager of programs and events at WRISE. “The continued success of the job fair year over year speaks to the lasting strength and enthusiasm for our industry. We’re glad to make these opportunities available to the WRISE membership and our larger clean energy community.”

#5939. Ousted Paraguayan president appears before prosecutors

Paraguay's President Santiago Peña waves after a meeting with Venezuela's interim President Delcy Rodriguez at the Miraflores Presidential Palace in Caracas, on September 19, 2026. Federico Parra/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña was impeached and placed under house arrest Friday after the collapse of a prominent U.S.-owned bank with ties to Paraguay's government. The fallout has left millions across the country without access to their money.

Peña is one of Washington's closest allies in the region and was a shareholder in Ueno Bank, a digital lender that served about 3 million customers — more than half of the country's adult population.

He was known as a pro-market leader focused on making it easier to do business in the country, but legislators said he jeopardized state assets and benefitted himself through his connection to the bank.

Ueno Bank previously said more than a million people opened bank accounts for the first time using its services, which included generous cash back offers on groceries.

A trial has proceeded against Peña, and he appeared before prosecutors Saturday morning. The last president ousted from office was Fernando Lugo in 2012.

Peña has denied any wrongdoing but on Thursday he announced his resignation. Lawmakers refused to accept that resignation, and instead moved forward with the country's impeachment process.

At proceedings inside the country's Senate Friday, legislators unanimously found Peña guilty of malfeasance in office, according to the Senate's website.

Prosecutors then accused him of a breach of trust, among other charges, before placing him under house arrest.

"I want to apologize to the Paraguayan people if I have failed you," Peña said in a speech posted to social media.

Demonstrators burn an image depicting Paraguay's President Santiago Pena, during a protest over the banking crisis and his resignation, at the main square in the surroundings of the National Congress in Asuncion, on November 5, 2026. Daniel Duarte/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Pedro Alliana, the country's vice president, was sworn in as president to serve out the rest of Peña's term until August 2028. In a speech on Friday, he announced he would take action to compensate people whose money was lost in the bank's collapse.

Paraguay's central bank said it seized control of Ueno Bank on Tuesday because its solvency ratio, an assessment of a company's long-term financial viability, no longer met legal standards.

Protests erupted across Asunción, the country's capital city, following the seizure.

NPR's John Otis and Catherine Osborn contributed reporting to this story.

#5937. PJM’s Emergency Order Should Be a Wake-Up Call for Michigan: Build More Power Before the Next Crisis

By Terence Parker, Michigan resident and Solar Mounts LLC team member

The U.S. Department of Energy’s October 12, 2026, emergency order directing PJM Interconnection to use additional generating resources should be a wake-up call for Michigan and the entire PJM region. Effective October 13–16, the order authorizes specified power plants to operate as needed for reliability and allows backup generators at large facilities to help reduce grid demand during emergencies.

This does not mean blackouts are inevitable. However, it highlights the serious challenges facing our electricity system and the need to add capacity before emergency measures become routine.

As a Michigan-based company, Solar Mounts LLC, a designer and manufacturer of commercial and utility-scale solar mounting systems, recognizes the potential implications for electricity prices, air quality, local businesses, and families across the state.

Rising Costs and the Consequences of Delayed Generation

PJM’s capacity auctions have exposed the difficulty of securing enough electricity for future demand. Prices reached $329.17 per megawatt-day for the 2026–2027 delivery year and $333.44 for 2027–2028, with the latter auction falling short of PJM’s stated reliability requirement.

Source: Vicinity Energy’s Analysis of PJM Capacity Auctions

Capacity costs are only one component of electricity bills, but rising prices signal growing pressure on consumers. We need more power before shortages force increasingly expensive emergency responses.

The October 2026 order permits designated generating units to exceed specified operating limits when necessary for reliability. It also authorizes PJM to direct backup generation at data centers and other large facilities, potentially including diesel generators as a last resort.

Running fossil-fuel plants more frequently can increase emissions, depending on the units involved. Operating diesel generators will worsen local noise pollution and expose operators to high diesel prices. If these measures become more common, we should ask why cleaner generation and storage are not coming online faster.

Weather Uncertainty Makes Planning Urgent

NOAA’s October 15, 2026, winter outlook anticipates warmer-than-average conditions across much of the northern United States, including the Midwest, influenced by a strong El Niño.

Source: NOAA’s 2026–2027 U.S. Winter Outlook

Seasonal forecasts cannot predict every period of extreme demand. Aging infrastructure, maintenance, changing weather, and growing electricity consumption reinforce the need for advance planning. Temporary authority to run additional fossil-fuel resources cannot replace long-term investment in generation.

Solar, Wind, and Batteries Can Help Close the Gap

Utility-scale solar, wind, and battery storage can help meet demand on timelines that many conventional generation projects cannot match. Suitable solar projects may be built within 18 months, depending on permitting, equipment, and interconnection. New nuclear facilities generally require substantially longer development periods.

Solar and wind will reduce emissions when they displace electricity from fossil-fuel generators. Batteries store surplus electricity for periods of higher demand. Note: actual emissions benefits depend on which generators are displaced and when electricity is delivered.

Faster interconnection reviews are also essential. Qualified solar projects shouldn’t remain in queues for years because of avoidable administrative delays. Better coordination among PJM, utilities, regulators, developers, and state governments, alongside necessary transmission upgrades, will bring capacity online sooner.

Solar Development and Agriculture Can Coexist

At Solar Mounts LLC, we witness concerns about installing solar on productive farmland here in rural Calhoun County (home to Solar Mounts LLC). However, well-designed projects can include restoration plans that allow agricultural use to resume when leases end. Solar leases can also provide farmers with supplemental income when crop prices fail to keep pace with production costs.

Agrivoltaic systems combine electricity generation with compatible crops or livestock. Thoughtful design and community engagement can balance renewable energy development with agricultural productivity, rural traditions, and farmland preservation.

Opportunities extend beyond prime farmland. Parking lots, commercial properties, brownfields, landfills, and other underutilized sites can accommodate solar generation. Solar carports provide electricity, shade, and weather protection, while community solar expands access for people unable or unwilling to install their own panels.

Solar Mounts LLC supports practical, site-appropriate solutions that expand generation while respecting farmers, landowners, and rural communities.

Hyperscale Data Centers Must Help Provide Their Own Power

Growing data center demand, particularly in Virginia and other parts of PJM’s territory, is adding pressure to an already constrained grid. Consequently, developers of new hyperscale facilities should demonstrate how they will secure adequate electricity and pay their fair share of supporting infrastructure. Dedicated generation, renewable energy contracts, batteries, flexible demand, and grid upgrades can all contribute.

Requiring every facility to build a gas-fired peaker plant is not necessarily the best answer. Gas turbines face equipment and supply-chain constraints, while additional fossil-fuel generation carries emissions and operating costs. A combination of solutions tailored to each location is more practical.

Existing facilities should also reduce peak demand where possible. Energy efficiency remains one of the most immediate ways businesses can limit electricity consumption.

Solar Mounts LLC sees the opportunity to expand commercial and utility-scale solar infrastructure across Michigan and the country. We encourage this expansion by supporting faster approvals, better grid planning, and a serious commitment to adding power generation and energy storage before shortages worsen.

Reliable electricity underpins our economy, jobs, and daily lives. Repeated emergency orders, rising capacity costs, and greater reliance on high-emission generation are not sound long-term strategies.

We shouldn’t wait for the next crisis to build the power system we already know we need.

#5930. Donated solar project powers community center in unincorporated New Mexico village  Solar Power World’s Projects of Impact

A building that started as a one-room schoolhouse has through the decades expanded to become a lifeline for a New Mexico community far from any city infrastructure, and a recent solar installation has brought new resilience to this place.

Children run outside of Ojo Sarco, New Mexico’s one-room schoolhouse in a photo from January 1943. The structure still stands today as the Ojo Sarco Community Center. Credit: John Collier, Jr./Library of Congress

The structure housing the Ojo Sarco Community Center (OSCC) was founded in the early 20th century. Ojo Sarco is an unincorporated community home to about 300 people that is located about 43 miles northeast from Santa Fe.

“We have no political voice, really,” said Carol Miller, a grant writer and board member for the Ojo Sarco Community Center. “We’re pretty neglected. We’ve lost resources in the past. We were the first wave of people to lose our post office many years ago. Everybody has to commute pretty much for everything.”

That commute is about 40 minutes round trip for basic amenities, she said.

The community center has tried to fill in those resource gaps with services like a twice-monthly food bank, summer meal programs for kids, safety classes and outings for its residents, such as a night at the opera. The OSCC also recently met all the qualifications to be a state library.

PPC Solar, also known as Paradise Power Company, from Taos, built a 7.5-kW solar project paired with 10.6-kWh of LFP energy storage at the OSCC. The contractor built the array at no-cost to the community center. Every year PPC donates a solar array to a regional nonprofit organization, and after three years of applying, the OSCC was awarded the project.

CEO Daniel Weinman’s father founded Paradise Power Company in the 1970s, and the company has installed solar in off-the-grid communities since the start. Its PV donation program is built out of that tradition.

“We’ve always wanted to find a way to give back to the community,” Weinman said. “The best thing that we found — and we’ve been doing it for over a decade now — is doing an annual nonprofit donation system … We’ve had farming organizations, land conservation organizations, and it goes on and on. Really, it’s about finding the right fit. We wanted to make a good impact for this community center, and we felt resiliency was the best way to do that.”

Solar Power World spoke to both PPC Solar and organizers with the OSCC about what effect this solar project has had in the village since it was built in fall 2025.

Larry Miller, project manager and OSCC board member: I think it’s a transformative project for our small community. We like to talk about the history of the community center, and now this is part of it. If someone doesn’t notice the solar panels on the roof, we point it out to them. It’s integrated into our lives here.

Carol Miller: It’s lowered our electric costs, which is really important for us, because we’re such a shoe-string operation. We’ve had some fun talks with the community because they all know we’ll have the charging capacity. We still have internet available, even with the power out. All the kids that come to our library programs, they’re solar oriented. The benefit of this company and its staff just has ripple effects throughout our greater community.

PPC Solar donated a solar plus storage project to the Ojo Sarco Community Center. This array will keep power running in this facility that has become a haven for Ojo Sarco residents. Credit: PPC Solar

Preston Bastardo, sales and design, PPC Solar: The building itself had some challenges with mounting the array on the roof, but that just took some exploration in the attic and making sure that we could land things where we needed to. The building has two large areas, and so fo installation of batteries had some restrictions, there was a good mechanical closet that we were able to use for batteries. Then as far as backup circuits, we did a partial facility backup where we had a critical loads panel next to their main panel. I think everything went pretty smoothly.

Weinman: Well, it really goes back to our roots. In 1972, there was no technology supporting solar at the time. My dad was a pioneer in the industry and he really developed his own controls and safety protections and found used panels and used batteries and was bringing power to places where there was no power. Our house, for example, we started with kerosene lanterns. We didn’t have electricity until we had a solar power system, and it was really life changing.

Being able to build on that legacy and look at our community in that light and be able to offer systems like this to communities that need it — I think that really adds to that legacy and really is profound in a lot of ways. It feels great to be able to do this and be in a position where we can help others realize and experience the benefits of solar power. We want to continue to participate in any way we can.

Carol Miller: We’re a very old community and proud of it. And having worked with local businesses and our electrical co-op, we know it’s really the exception and not the rule because we own our power. Any technology that is shared equally in a low-income community has ripple effects beyond what I think some of us imagined a number of years ago. It’s for everyone.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5921. Stratos Data Center Will Emit Air Pollution That’s Linked to Premature Deaths and Health Economic Damages for Utah & States Downwind

A new report models emissions from different on-site methane generation power scenarios to show potential impacts on the people of Utah and residents of multiple downwind states.

Canadian businessman and television personality “Mr. Wonderful” (aka Kevin O’Leary) and his businesses have proposed the Stratos data center in Box Elder County, Utah, near Salt Lake City, that  will be one of the largest data centers in the world, requiring as much as 9 gigawatts (GW) of on-site power from burning methane gas. The Box Elder County Commission approved the project in May—under highly contentious circumstances and under a highly unusual arrangement within a project area created by Utah’s Military Installation Development Authority.

A new report prepared by EmPower Analytics Group for NRDC estimates that the air pollution, health harms, and economic damages from the proposed Stratos data center would be substantial: “[a]t the potential 9 GW full build-out, air pollution from Stratos’s methane-gas power generation is estimated to cause approximately 5–45 additional premature deaths and $75–$690 million in health-related economic damages every year.” The report also discusses additional health harms that extend beyond premature deaths and include new asthma cases, nonfatal heart attacks, hospital visits, and lost work and school days.

These ranges reflect scenarios that model the health harms and economic damage caused by air pollution from two possible methane-gas primary power generating technologies: modern combined-cycle combustion turbines and methane gas–fired reciprocating internal combustion engines (RICE). The primary harmful air pollution consists of tiny soot particles—particulate matter of 2.5 microns or less (PM2.5)—about 30 times smaller than the width of an average human hair.

The report further finds that over a 30-year period, at the potential 9 GW full build-out, the health and economic harms from this PM2.5 air pollution “could accumulate to approximately 856–1,350 premature deaths and $13.5–$20.7 billion under gas-fired RICE, compared with 145–253 premature deaths and $2.3–$3.8 billion under combined-cycle gas.”

Of the two methane-gas primary power generation technologies evaluated in the report, one uses a smaller number of large gas combustion turbines, while the RICE scenario reflected electricity generated by many individual engines operating in parallel. Indeed, the RICE scenario is patterned on a methane gas–powered data center with 69 RICE generators that the Utah Division of Air Quality permitted in Millard County in early 2026. RICE generators produce higher emissions than combined-cycle gas at the same generation capacity. Accordingly, the report finds that “[t]he technology used to power Stratos could make a five- to sixfold difference in its estimated health harms.”

Importantly, the modeled combined-cycle gas scenario does not estimate the emissions from the use of on-site diesel generators for backup power because those plans by Stratos remain unknown. (The report modeled diesel backup generators for the RICE scenario, scaled from the Millard County permit example.) Diesel generators are significantly dirtier than comparably sized methane gas generators, emitting 95 percent more smog-forming nitrogen oxides than an equivalent gas generator as well as 98 percent more non-methane hydrocarbons and 59 percent more carbon monoxide. Harmful PM2.5 emissions will be even higher from any data center employing diesel generators (the scale of that additional pollution would depend on whether they were truly used for “emergency conditions” versus being run more frequently; state permitting parameters for backup diesels is a critical issue for all data center developments).

The highest concentrations of harmful air pollution from the Stratos data center will occur near the facility in Box Elder County, neighboring areas of northern Utah (including Salt Lake City), and southern Idaho. But the health harms and economic damage from Stratos would extend well beyond these areas: The report finds that more than 80 percent of health harms and health-related economic damages occur outside the state, reaching downwind populations in Colorado, Wyoming, and as far away as Illinois. The report appendix identifies the full list of potentially impacted states.

The Stratos developers have maintained from the start that the massive data center will be powered by methane gas. “That’s never going to happen,” Utah Governor Spencer Cox told the Salt Lake Tribune in May. “The very first phase will be natural gas, but the other phases should not be. They should be nuclear, and they should be geothermal, and solar and other technology.” But the most recent filing by the Stratos developers with the state still show plans for the full build-out of the data center to be powered by methane gas. This filing shows that the developers now say that they plan a 7.5 GW data center using the dirtier and more harmful RICE generators that burn methane gas. None of these filings commit the developers to doing or not doing something different from what they are saying now, a reality that has characterized this data center project—and many other proposed data center projects nationally.

Data centers in Utah or anywhere else, of course, need not be powered by highly polluting, harmful fossil fuels like methane gas. Other data center developers have been doing better jobs showing how, in stark contrast to the Stratos proposal, it is possible to power these facilities with cleaner energy—such as a robust portfolio of wind, solar, geothermal, and batteries—and maxing out demand side resources.

NRDC commissioned this important EmPower Analytics report to raise awareness and concerns about the harmful health impacts and associated economic damages from dangerous air pollution caused by data centers like the proposed Stratos Project in Utah and by the proliferation of data centers across the United States. These harms arise especially from hyperscaler data centers powered by fossil fuels, like Stratos. While the dangers and costs of excessive water use by data centers are more well known, the deadly toll of air pollution—a silent killer—deserves greater attention and concern.

Harmful air pollution should be met with the best air pollution control technologies available. Data centers should be powered by new, additional, deliverable clean energy that does not release air pollution, along with batteries, storage, and other smart solutions. If someone nicknamed Mr. Wonderful wants to earn that name, he should live up to it, starting with the Stratos data center.

By John Walke, Director, Federal Clean Air & Senior Attorney, Environmental Health, NRDC

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5920. The industry races into Las Vegas for RE+

The largest tradeshow focused on the U.S. solar, storage and clean energy markets is back in Las Vegas for 2026. RE+ was the 24th largest tradeshow in the United States in 2025 by attendance, and this year’s showing (Nov. 16-19) is expected to be just as large.

The Formula 1 series will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend after RE+ concludes. Photos courtesy: F1 and the Las Vegas Grand Prix

The U.S. solar and storage industries have gotten so big that it appears only the Las Vegas Convention Center can accommodate a tradeshow bringing together nearly 40,000 clean energy professionals in one place. RE+ will be held at the Las Vegas Convention Center each November for the foreseeable future.

The convention center is tucked away from the main strip of casinos, but there is still plenty in the area to see and do. The site is also easily accessible, with a stop on the Las Vegas Monorail.

Conference education programming begins on Monday and sessions continue throughout the show at various stages on the exhibit hall floor. The exhibition opens on Tuesday.

Exhibitors will be spread across three halls in the convention center — the main entrance is at West Hall which extends via an over-street walkway to North Hall, and then Central Hall is accessed beyond a lobby area. There will also be a few outdoor booths to peruse located near food trucks. Visiting outdoor exhibits in November will be more comfortable, as the average high temperature is just 70°F, unlike the late summer heat of September.

Special events at RE+ include a golf tournament on Monday and the Run with the Sun 5K on Tuesday. The “Energy in Motion” block party will be at the Convention Center on Tuesday starting at 7 p.m. in the West Hall.

Formula 1 will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend following RE+. The course takes drivers down the Las Vegas Strip and around the Sphere, and road closures to the public will begin on Thursday. The Las Vegas Convention Center is several blocks north of the racing circuit, so the conference shouldn’t experience disruptions. After-conference activities might be affected later in the week, and attendees should plan a little extra time getting to the airport if leaving before the weekend.

Solar Power World editors will be covering all the sights at RE+ this year, so be sure to follow along on LinkedIn and right here on the website. But first, here’s a preview of a few must-see booths on the tradeshow floor.

Strata supports reliability across the entire lifecycle of stand-alone storage or hybrid renewable power plants.

Construct solar safer, faster, and cheaper. Meet Built’s AI Piling Solution: a fully autonomous piling system for utility-scale solar. Hydrelio aiR Optim is our flexible floating racking and anchoring system. It comes from 15+ years of FPV knowledge and expertise, and the first patented and industrialized floating solar solution. Compatible with battery systems from SolarEdge, FranklinWH, Lunar Energy, EcoFlow and Fox ESS, IslandDER is trusted by leading national installers, offering more versatility and compatibility than any meter collar on the market.

#5918. China Truck Sales Now Majority Electric. What’s Next?

EV heavy trucks hit 50.3% market share in China for September, with sales up 34% from a year ago. Long-haul big rig market share hit 64.5%. China is on track to have over 40% EV market share for the entire year, reaching their 2030 goal in a matter of months. This is not the first time China’s heavy truck market share topped 50%, but that previous blip in December last year was prior to subsidies ending, followed by a dip in January. Current sales rates are not bolstered by the subsidy and are now on a much more stable upward trajectory.

The speed of EV adoption for heavy trucks in China has recently grown much faster than for cars, having risen from just 13.6% in 2024. When I visited Guangzhou in November of last year and again in April of this year, I noticed a lot more green plates on trucks, indicating that they were electric. It seems that my perception is backed by sales data.

With the addition of LNG trucks, diesel’s share of heavy trucks has dropped to roughly 40%. While LNG sales have also declined with the rate of EV adoption, diesel trucks could soon make up less than 1/3 of sales and decline further from there. China could feasibly double its 2030 goal and hit 80% EV penetration, by which point many diesel models are likely to be discontinued.

With these levels of market penetration, in addition to retirements, China’s diesel demand is on track to fall by about 230,000 barrels per day this year, despite increased freight movement. Every additional electric truck that replaces a diesel truck then builds on that reduction. Due to their duty cycle, each electric truck sold has a much greater impact on fuel consumption than each car sold. And the price of fuel combined with the duty cycle has a much greater impact on overall truck economics.

To be clear, these improving results are not due to subsidies, as they appear in the year after subsidies were cut. They are also not due to mandates, as the 2030 goals were met before the ink was dry on their announcement. EV trucks are now more affordable to own and operate than diesel trucks in China. The increase in diesel prices has only increased the cost gap.

Last year, roughly 90% of all electric truck sales globally were in China. That percentage is set to expand this year, despite rising sales in some other countries. Those sales are simply not rising fast enough outside of China to keep pace with China. In comparison, the EU is looking at 2.5% EV market share this year. China is on pace to average roughly as many electric trucks sold per week as Europe sells per year. The US EV truck market looks like a rounding error in comparison at less than one percent.

In addition, China has rapidly been building out heavy truck charging infrastructure as part of the 28 million charging facilities planned to be in operation by the end of 2027, up from 21 million last March. This not only focuses on the major cities but also extends into more rural areas that have been slower to adopt electrification. In addition, several companies are building out battery swap stations. While we have yet to see if battery swap or MW+ charging wins in the truck market, expect the competition between the two approaches to be fierce.

Within Tier 1 cities, I could see a potential phase out of diesel trucks in the near future. Hainan province is already planning to end ICE passenger car sales by 2030, and I expect that to extend to diesel trucks as well. Although rural areas have been slower to adopt, they also tend to be less wealthy and thus more impacted by the changing economics. If diesel prices stay elevated while charging becomes convenient, many rural businesses will not be able to afford to buy new diesel trucks. With the end of diesel truck sales, strict emissions standards and a potential return of scrappage incentives could take remaining diesel vehicles off the road at an accelerated rate.

With fewer diesel trucks on the road in the largest market globally, diesel consumption is set to decline. In addition, the supply chain for diesel parts will also start to decline. As protectionism has targeted electric vehicles, rather than internal combustion  engine (ICE) vehicles, a decline in Chinese (ICE) parts has a potentially significant impact on global markets.

Of course, as EVs take over the majority of heavy truck sales in the fiercely competitive Chinese market, Chinese manufacturers will start looking outside of the country for growth.

The traditional thought would be to look at wealthy Western countries that have substantial buying power. However, many Chinese companies may be a bit reluctant to focus on those markets after the protectionist efforts against electric passenger vehicles. Many European markets, like Germany, also have very high electricity prices, which makes the financial proposition less attractive for commercial vehicles that are heavily motivated by costs. Chinese companies may start focusing elsewhere.

The Middle Corridor could become a focus. This essentially rebuilds the Silk Road land route from China, across the Caspian Sea, on to Turkey. Many of the countries along this route  once had prosperous trade, only to fall into decline when ocean trade routes were established. However, land transport is much easier to electrify than long ocean voyages. China is investing heavily with countries like Kazakhstan along the route with renewable electricity infrastructure. Electrified rail is expanding by 26-fold in Turkey. Electrified trucking is the next logical step, connecting the rail lines to more local production along the route.

Other Belt and Road Initiative (BRI) countries could also benefit. In countries like Ethiopia, which has expensive diesel but electricity prices to businesses of just $0.017/kWh, electric trucks could make perfect sense to transport between new factories and new ports. Trading partners like Vietnam and Thailand could also see an influx.

All this is not to say that local companies will not benefit. Unlike passenger cars, heavy equipment tends to be more modular. Due to the bulk and mass of the vehicles, final assembly is more likely to be localized, with greater use of local suppliers.

Overall, we could see much of the “Rest of the World” (ROW) leapfrog legacy economies in EV truck adoption. The purchase price is no longer prohibitive in China, and the operating costs are much lower. With infrastructure buildout and increasing renewable installed capacity, electric trucking is poised to take over rapidly.

That is not to say that the West will be completely left out. Countries with less prohibitive trade policies toward EVs will likely see increased adoption. While EVs have taken over passenger car sales in Norway, their trucking industry still has a way to go at just 14.4%. And the geographically most eastern country typically lumped in with the “West,” Australia, is likely to see EV truck sales follow a similar pattern to EV car sales. If Canada continues to open up, they could also benefit. Of course, as the majority of the economic benefit from electric trucks comes from their use, inflated trucking costs hurt every company that moves products by truck, and those inflated costs then lead to inflation throughout the economy. That impacts overall affordability. As such, there is a chance that protectionists do not put as much effort into undermining electric truck imports as they put into passenger EVs.

And the EV heavy truck global supply chain will become increasingly well established. This could mean more parts availability for brands outside of China to build competitive vehicles, if those companies have access to global supply chains. Competitive markets will also drive innovation, moving the technology forward at an accelerating rate. Meanwhile the supply chain for diesel trucks will shrink.

While the US still seems to be figuring out how to make a safe EV Semi, battery-powered heavy trucks are becoming increasingly mature products elsewhere. As electric trucks move from majority share to complete market dominance in the largest market, other markets will follow, and the transition will become inevitable. Hopefully, the largest oil producing country in the world will not be left too far behind.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5913. How Does Oahu Get to an Economically Resilient, 100% Clean Energy Future?

Oahu is a unique place. It’s the home of one of the largest clean tech incubators in the world, Elemental Impact, for a reason. Exorbitantly high electricity rates combined with ample natural resources make it an incredible laboratory to bring clean tech solutions to market. Rapid investment for WWII created a grid that was dependent on foreign oil. Path dependency (the idea that “what you know” becomes what you continue to do) hampered innovation and resulted in the creation of infrastructure (multiple power plants across multiple islands) that still, today, burns diesel fuel as large generators, to produce electricity for the island. Throw in a dependence on imported fuels that must come at least 3,000 miles to arrive, being the strategic hub for the entire Pacific theater of the U.S. military, one of the wildest weather years and hurricane seasons the island chain has ever seen AND the state’s economic dependency on tourism dollars, high cost of living, and low wages, and and wow – you’ve got yourself a puzzle and a half to solve in order to get the place to a sustainable future.

Analyst Mike Barnard loves a challenge, so he set forth to put together a roadmap to help Oahu get to 100% clean energy, which helps recirculate money in the state, lower costs, create jobs and foster economic resiliency.

Recently I gave a talk at Hawaii Tech Week in which I summarized this report, added in my own experience running an energy efficiency service that has now greened more than 20,000 homes and is saving Hawaii residents over $10 million a year on their utility bills, and then compiled the new and coming technologies that can help the island get off fossil fuels.

Mike’s report did something very important. The state’s own reporting and strategy looks at the big picture, a very difficult mountain to climb. Mike looked at that mountain and removed some of the false summits and unachievable viewpoints, resulting in a much more achievable and strategic pathway. What he did was eliminate the things we simply cannot fix right now – long distance flights, maritime bunkering, and military fuels – and tackled the problem of Oahu’s actual energy consumption for its residents. The result was that it was achievable, and there are technologies right now today that can be deployed in what he refers to as a “no regret stack” first and foremost.

I combined that with my approach, which was to showcase the economic resilience it creates. Case in point, an LED bulb saves so much money on Oahu that it pays for itself in 18 days. Every day after that, it is like change plinking in a piggy bank for the person who put it in. That builds economic resiliency for those who need it most. From LEDs to e-bikes to heat pump water heaters to balcony solar – it all adds up – fast payback periods, and financial independence.

Mike comes back to the things we simply can’t fix right now and argues that the tech will come – but for the duration of the 2020s and into the 2030s, we should be installing parking lot solar canopies, agrivoltaics, heat pump water heaters, and more.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

#5911. XPENG Sales Soar!

XPENG had a fantabulous sales month in October. We can give you the numbers, but as you can see well enough in the chart above and below, the company’s sales soared to a whole new level last month.

Reaching 50,237 vehicle deliveries, that meant 22% sales growth month over month and 20% sales growth year over year. There were 41,256 XPENG vehicles delivered in September 2026 and 42,013 delivered in October 2025, the company’s previous monthly record. Will it be another year until XPENG beats its monthly sales record again, or is the October 2026 result just the beginning of more record sales in coming months?

Well, there are some reasons to expect even more growth. “In October 2026, the XPENG L05 was officially launched in the Chinese mainland, with deliveries set to begin on November 1, 2026,” XPENG shares. “On October 26, 2026, XPENG obtained a demonstration operation license to conduct Level 4 Robotaxi services in Guangzhou. The approval enables XPENG Robotaxi to advance from technology validation to real-world operational and business model validation, marking a key milestone toward the commercialization of XPENG’s Physical AI technologies.”

A big portion of this sales growth came from tremendous growth in sales outside of XPENG’s home market of China. “Overseas deliveries also reached an all-time monthly high of 13,326 units, up 166% year-over-year,” the company shares.

Overall, things are looking very bright for XPENG. And those EV sales are helping the planet and humanity as well. “XPENG’s electric vehicles delivered from January to October 2026 are expected to reduce life-cycle greenhouse gas emissions by more than 5.77 million tons compared to internal combustion engine vehicles, equivalent to the carbon absorbed by 95.43 million young trees over 10 years,” XPENG wrote in closing, and I’ll close with that highlight as well.

Oh, wait, I’ll also add that XPENG’s big spike in sales in October helped the company to shoot high above similar (but different) rival NIO in their long-term battle as the best selling smart EV startup.

NIO didn’t have its best month, but maybe it will surge again in November and/or December.

#5907. Participation in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico VPP program doubled in 2026

Sunrun announced that participation in its Puerto Rico distributed power plant has doubled to more than 60,000 customers, representing nearly 70,000 home batteries. The distributed power plant frequently and reliably delivers 45 MW of dispatchable power for four hours at a time, providing critical support to Puerto Rico’s electric grid.

Since April, Sunrun has dispatched its power plant more than 60 times to weather severe energy shortfalls and prevent rolling blackouts. During an extended period of unseasonably hot temperatures this fall, Sunrun dispatched its home batteries to the grid for 18 consecutive days beginning October 13.

Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant has scaled rapidly since 2024, when approximately 4,000 customers were enrolled. Enrollment surged to more than 30,000 customers in 2025. Sunrun is the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing program.

Puerto Rico’s electric utility operator, LUMA, said in June and again in July that energy from home batteries helped “maintain system stability and prevent outages that would have affected thousands of customers.”

“What began in Puerto Rico out of necessity for family resilience has become a vital blueprint for the U.S. mainland, where severe weather and the proliferation of AI data centers are demanding new energy solutions,” said Sunrun CEO Mary Powell. “Sunrun is uniquely positioned to provide these solutions, whether to support a grid at its breaking point or to provide capacity for hyperscalers looking to come online.”

Sunrun’s increased dispatching capacity in Puerto Rico comes after residents on the island experienced an average of 36 hours of power outages in 2025 that were not caused by major adverse weather, which is 19% more than the previous year, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration. When severe weather is factored in, residents experienced an additional 23 hours of power interruptions.

Customers who participate in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant choose how much energy to share, with most contributing a small portion of their total battery capacity. This approach ensures they retain reserve power for personal backup power in the event of grid outages. Participants are financially compensated for the power they choose to share, while Sunrun generates recurring revenue by operating the distributed power plant.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5899. PowerBank completes 3-MW New York community solar project

Renewable energy developer PowerBank has completed the 3.26-MW Camillus community solar project in Camillus, New York, and has received permission to operate. Solar Advocate Development, LLC, the project’s owner and EPC, built the array on a capped landfill, which it acquired from PowerBank in spring 2025 in a $7.3 million transaction.

“A closed landfill is land that most people would write off,” said Andrew van Doorn, president and COO of PowerBank. “At Camillus, our engineers and construction partners turned it into a 3.26-MW source of clean power for local households. Reaching permission to operate on a site like this reflects decades of hands-on experience, and it’s exactly the kind of project where that experience matters most.”

The project will soon accept community solar subscriptions. Solar Simplified will manage customer acquisition and subscription services for this community solar project.

#5891. “War” Secretary Pete Hegseth Endorses 15 Minute City Model

You just can’t make this stuff up. While conservatives around the world are waging holy war against the “15 Minute City” concept, US Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth has gone all-in on the idea. His newly announced “Fortress America” military resilience plan includes a new vision for military bases that embraces walkability and quality of life. If that sounds like the 15 Minute City plan in action, it is. Oh, lordy…

Walkable communities are as old as the hills. That’s what people did before automobiles hit the scene and cities were organized around cars. Credit for applying the walkability model to 21st century urban planning goes to the Franco-Colombian scientist Carlos Moreno, who formalized the 15 Minute City concept in 2016. The idea got a further boost of attention when the Mayor of Paris, Anne Hidalgo, pitched walkable, bike-able neighborhoods as part of her successful re-election campaign in 2020.

The COVID-19 lockdown of 2020 was also a pivotal moment, with a surge in work-from-home households drawing attention to the need for local resources as a matter of survival and resiliency in case of a widespread emergency.

The 15 Minute City model covers a lot of ground, but the basic idea is that people should be able to do their shopping, recreation, socializing, and other essential routines within walking or biking distance from their homes, making cars largely optional within the neighborhood. Walkable access to schools and health care is also part of the plan.

The argument for 15-minute cities (also called 15-minute neighborhoods or 15-minute communities) is also pretty straightforward. Walking is healthier than sitting in a car. Avoiding injury or death from car accidents is another plus. Walking also reduces carbon emissions, and it saves money on gas.

In addition, walkability levels the playing field, so to speak. Everyone in the 15-minute zone has walkable access to basic resources, regardless of whether or not they can afford a car. In other words, there is a significant equity and inclusion element to the concept…

…and, that’s where the trouble kicks in. In 2022 the World Economic Forum posted an article by Lisa Chamberlain, the founder of the urban communications and business firm Strategies for City Builders. Chamberlain describes a 15 Minute City as characterized by diversified, mixed-use neighborhoods built to withstand shocks like COVID-19 and climate change.

“As climate change and global conflict cause shocks and stresses at faster intervals and increasing severity, the 15-minute city will become even more critical,” Chamberlain emphasized, with a further emphasis on equity and inclusion:

“Specifically, communities that foster and maintain social and economic relationships don’t have to be wealthy, but they do need to be walkable and safe, with both residential and commercial buildings intact. And, I would add, for 15-Minute Cities to thrive, not just survive crises—and this cannot be stressed enough—they must also have plenty of mixed-income and equitable housing, as well as digital access.”

It didn’t take long for the conspiracy machine to kick into action around the globe. In February of 2023, for example, the Australian news organization ABC radio summed up the 15 Minute City conspiracy theory thusly:

“To some, it’s an urban planning concept that promotes sustainable and healthy living.

“To a small group of others, it’s a plot by ‘tyrannical bureaucrats’ to take our cars and control our lives, which could lead to a real-life Hunger Games scenario.”

“Limiting cars and promoting pedestrian or bike access is framed as a slippery slope to government-run, open-air prisons,” ABC continued.

Yikes! ABC also cites the Canadian psychologist Jordan Peterson. who railed against “idiot tyrannical bureaucrats” who “decide by fiat where you’re ‘allowed’ to drive…and, make no mistake, it’s part of a well-documented plan” (emphasis added).

Ohfergawdsakes, of course there is a well documented plan, and it’s called urban planning. And, that’s where the US Department of Defense comes in.

On September, in a move that sailed under the CleanTechnica radar, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced the new “FORTRESS America” plan, with FORTRESS representing “Federal Operations for Resilience, Troop Resources, Energy, Supply, and Survivability.”

Stripping away Secretary Hegseth’s testosterone-fueled purple prose in the September 30 announcement, FORTRESS AMERICA applies the 15 Minute City urban planning model to major Defense Department bases in the US, ensuring they can operate independently of civilian infrastructure for a sustained period of time. That includes everything needed to support community life in addition to the supplies needed to sustain military operations.

FORTRESS America even goes beyond the initial 15 Minute City concept. As envisioned under the “Next Great American Base” element of the program, a central feature of the plan consists of access to a reliable source of on-site energy.

Don’t just take my word for it. “The program aligns basing and infrastructure projects to ensure they are collectively advancing toward greater independence,” Hegseth stated in the official order, with independence referring to the capability to sustain operations within a facility that is cut off from civilian resources. In other words, sustainable urban planning for military facilities.

Hegseth tasked the Under Secretary of War for Acquisition and Sustainment to carry out the new order. The base design part of the order was passed along to Dale Marks, the Assistant Secretary for Energy, Installations, and Environment.

Marks did not let the 15 Minute City grass grow under his feet. In a public presentation on October 29, he emphasized the importance of building quality of life into a sustainable model for military facilities in the US.

“Let us build installations worthy of the warriors who call them home,” Marks emphasized. “The nation shouldn’t expect warfighters to focus on high stakes, multidomain operations when they come home to base housing with black mold, failing plumbing and leaky roofs.”

“Substandard living conditions are not minor inconveniences. They are an active drain on morale, combat readiness and our retention of top-tier talent,” Marks elaborated.

It’s not just a matter of rehabbing military housing. In the presentation, Marks made the case for a full blown 15 Minute City model. “The Next Great American Base will include a compact, walkable concept with premier schools, advanced healthcare and town centers that foster community,” Marks continued, neatly summing up the fundamentals of 15 Minute Cities in a nutshell.

Marks anticipates that state governors and other stakeholders will compete to see where the first “Next Great American Base” will be constructed. Once complete, the new facility, and its 15 Minute City, will serve as a model for future projects.

As for the on-site power generation angle, nuclear energy gets the pole position — for now. As Marks and Hegseth both emphasize, though, speed is of the essence. Results are demanded within months, if not weeks. That leaves plenty of room for solar-enabled microgrids to throw their hat into the FORTRESS America ring. After all, if speed-to-power is the name, solar and batteries are the game…

Image: The 15 Minute City model calls for walkable, bike-able access to basic services and resources, a concept that has been embraced by none other than the US Department of Defense (courtesy of Rutgers University).

Tina has been covering advanced energy technology, military sustainability, emerging materials, biofuels, ESG and related policy and political matters for CleanTechnica since 2009. Follow her @tinamcasey on LinkedIn, Mastodon or Bluesky.

#5888. Nabrawind’s Crane-Free Wind Towers Face a Supplier Test

Spain’s Nabrawind has developed a way to install exceptionally tall wind turbine towers without bringing an enormous crawler crane to the site. Its Nabralift system uses a tubular upper tower supported by a three-legged steel structure. Hydraulic equipment raises the assembled turbine while modular sections are inserted below it near ground level. The lifting frame can then be removed and used on another installation. This addresses genuine logistical and construction problems, particularly at remote sites where specialist cranes are expensive to transport and where wind can interrupt conventional lifting operations.

The company has progressed beyond demonstration-scale hardware. In September 2026, UL Solutions issued type certification for Envision’s 7.8 MW turbine on Nabrawind’s 188-meter tower. Fortescue is using 17 of those tower configurations at the 133 MW Nullagine wind project in Western Australia’s Pilbara region, where construction is underway. Nabrawind previously erected a 160-meter prototype in Spain in under two weeks and has supplied smaller commercial towers in Morocco and France.

The engineering benefits are credible. Nabrawind reports using roughly 20% less tower steel than comparable conventional tubular designs and reducing foundation concrete by as much as 80%, depending on the design and site. Those are supplier claims rather than independently established savings across all wind farms. The potential to avoid large cranes, simplify transport and raise turbines in windier conditions could also matter substantially to total installed cost and construction schedules. High hub heights only earn their keep when those costs are justified by the electricity generated over the project’s life.

The current deployment has an unusual commercial structure. Fortescue acquired Nabrawind in September 2025 and is building Nullagine to serve its own mining operations. Fortescue has the capital, internal electricity demand and industrial incentive to support a technology it owns. Successful installation in that context would be important evidence of construction performance, but it would not establish that an unrelated wind developer can finance and maintain the same system under ordinary third-party commercial arrangements.

Nabralift’s proprietary construction approach creates a procurement issue. Conventional tubular towers can be sourced from several qualified manufacturers, and experienced contractors can compete for much of their construction work. A developer selecting Nabralift currently depends on one supplier for the core tower system, specialist lifting knowledge and associated support. Wind turbine original-equipment manufacturers also create supplier dependencies, but concentrating another critical part of the project in a proprietary installation system requires its own contract and risk assessment.

A type certificate provides valuable technical assurance about the certified turbine-and-tower configuration. A lender or insurer still needs enforceable delivery schedules, long-term warranties, repair and maintenance provisions, clarity on replacement parts, and a workable remedy if the supplier’s capacity or commercial priorities change. These questions become more important when a wind farm must operate for 25 years or longer and there is no qualified alternative provider of the same system.

Nabrawind’s next important proof point is an independently developed and financed large wind farm that accepts those risks on commercial terms. Qualified installation partners, credible long-term service arrangements and greater competitive capacity could make its engineering advantages easier for developers to buy. The 188-meter tower has earned technical attention; its broader market depends on financing and procurement confidence catching up with the machinery.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Nabrawind’s self-erecting design, Fortescue’s Nullagine deployment and the supplier concentration that independent wind developers would have to manage. It follows my earlier assessment of 300-meter wind towers, where construction economics were the central challenge.

#5885. ARRAY Technologies and Shoals plan to merge companies

Solar tracker and racking manufacturer ARRAY Technologies and electrical balance of systems (eBOS) provider Shoals Technologies have initiated a stock-for-stock merger to combine both companies.

“This transaction represents a compelling opportunity to create meaningful value for customers of both ARRAY and Shoals by bringing together our complementary strengths. Although structural and electrical systems must operate seamlessly together in the field, they have historically been designed and sourced through separate channels. This combination changes that. Together, we expect to deliver a differentiated, technically integrated and interoperable platform that simplifies project execution, enhances project economics and accelerates innovation for customers,” said Kevin Hostetler, CEO of ARRAY.

According to the press announcement, ARRAY will be Shoals’ parent company after the transaction, with Shoal’s CEO Brandon Moss assuming the role as CEO for the overall company. ARRAY’s CEO Kevin Hostetler will transition to a special advisor role for the company.

“Today marks an important milestone for Shoals, ARRAY and the customers we serve. By bringing together two highly complementary businesses with experienced teams, we are creating an integrated platform with broader capabilities, deeper customer relationships and a stronger foundation for long-term growth. We believe the combined company will be positioned to better service customers, deliver more innovative and integrated solutions, realize meaningful synergies and pursue expansion opportunities across the solar value chain, data center markets and beyond,” said Brandon Moss, CEO of Shoals.

The companies plan to create packaged warranties across their structural and eBOS products. As manufacturers, both ARRAY and Shoals have supplied their products to solar and energy storage projects globally.

ARRAY is known for producing single-axis solar trackers that use an inter-row rotating mechanism. Over the years, the company has iterated on its trackers to use multi- and dual-row architectures, and to be deployed on undulating topographies. These rotating solar structures are supported with proprietary tracker operations software with considerations such as shading and hail and wind stowing.

Shoals manufactures combiner boxes and cable management solutions for multi-market scale solar and energy storage projects, as well as data centers.

“Having had the privilege of serving as chairman of both ARRAY and Shoals, I have seen firsthand the complementary products, talented teams and shared commitment to customers offered by both companies,” said Brad Forth, chairman of the board of directors of ARRAY and Shoals. “Together, ARRAY and Shoals are well positioned to deliver enhanced value to customers, shareholders and employees. I also want to thank Kevin for his leadership and important contributions to ARRAY, as well as his valuable insights as he transitions to a Special Advisor role. I look forward to continuing to work with Brandon, who brings proven leadership, deep industry expertise and strategic vision to lead the combined company in this next phase of growth.”

Both ARRAY’s and Shoals’ boards have reportedly unanimously approved the merger, with Forth abstaining from the vote. The merger is expected to complete in Q1 2027.

While not mergers, ARRAY Technologies has been acquiring companies producing technologies complementary to solar trackers. Last year, ARRAY acquired fixed-tilt racking and foundations manufacturer APA Solar Racking, and just acquired Affordable Wire Management, a cable management solutions provider, this past September.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.
